PREFACE 

Early America, as the title indicates, deals with the beginnings of the United States. Taking as its starting point the continent of North America a thousand years ago, the volume portrays in broad sweeping strokes the coming of the Northmen, the awakening of Europe, the great explorations, the making of settlements in the New World, the intercolonial rivalries and wars, the break between the English colonies and the Mother Country, and the establishment of the new nation. Throughout, the customs, occupations, culture, and living conditions of the people are given major attention. 

Especial effort has been made to make the volume teachable. With this end in view and in accordance with modern educational thought, the subject matter has been organized in large blocks or units focused upon central unifying themes. Illustrations and maps have been selected to illuminate the text rather than merely to adorn the book. The teaching aids consist of a wide variety of devices, including activities to be carried out, problems to be solved, and tests to be answered.

Mention should be made of the able assistance of Mina M. Selee, a teacher of wide experience in the middle grades of the New York schools. Her alert and tireless efforts in the preparation of the manuscript are sincerely appreciated by the authors. The vocabulary has

vi 

EARLY AMERICA 

been checked with the Thorndike Word List and more than 85% of the words used will be found in the first 2500. The meanings of other words have been worked out in the text and the exercises. Sentences and paragraphs have been recast to avoid textual difficulties. In short, everything possible has been done to make the book a serviceable educational tool. J. A. W. H. C. H.

EARLY AMERICA AMERICA: A SLEEPING WORLD 1. AMERICA: AN UNKNOWN LAND 

Two great triangles of land, one above the other, and each with its broad side at the top, stretched lazily across millions of square miles. The upper triangle turned a cold shoulder toward the Arctic Ocean; the lower one pointed chilly feet toward the Antarctic. On the east roared the Atlantic Ocean, separating the two vast triangles of land from Europe. The broad Pacific lapped their west coast, and beyond it lay Asia and the islands of the sea.

A thousand years ago the people of Europe and Asia knew nothing of the two triangles of land which formed the continents of North and South America. And the people who lived in America at that time had no idea. that other people dwelt in other lands beyond the reaches of the great oceans.

On the eastern coast of North America, by the Atlantic, the natives had a narrow plain across which to roam. West of that lay a mountain range, the Appalachians (ǎp á la'che ánz), stretching from north to south. In the central part of the continent lay an enormous valley, washed by five great lakes of fresh water and by the long Mississippi (mi si sip'pē) River, whose branches reached like greedy fingers into the very heart 1

૨૭ 

EARLY AMERICA 

BUFFALO GRAZING ON THE PLAINS 

of the country. Between the central valley and the 

Pacific were the Rocky Mountains, wearing caps of snow and carrying vast forests on their backs. 

This was and is - the continent of North America a tangle of forests, a network of rivers, a mesh of lakes ; deserts and prairies, rolling country and mountains, wastelands and swamps. 

Beneath the earth a thousand years ago lay hidden great quantities of coal and iron, silver and gold, oil and gas. Only a few of the natives found such minerals and made use of them.

The plains were thick with thousands of buffalo; the forests gave shelter to deer and bears, to wild cats and poisonous snakes; the rivers and lakes teemed with fish.

The country was a vast wilderness with plenty of room for men and beasts.

AMERICA: A SLEEPING WORLD 

3 

2. THE INDIANS OF A THOUSAND YEARS AGO 

The natives were dark-skinned people with high cheekbones and straight black hair. They hunted and fished, fought and worshiped, a thousand years ago, without interference from an outside world of which they knew nothing. They ate wild fruits and berries, roots and nuts, fish and game, sometimes only a step ahead of starvation.

Many tribes of these dark-skinned Indians, as they were later called, lived in different parts of North America. Some were more skilled than others; some roamed over many miles and lived in tents; others built houses of mud or stone or bark, and stayed in one place. 

Whether they were rovers or home-dwellers depended on the way they made their living. 

The Indians had very few things they could call their own-arrow heads and bone tools, clay pots and cooking vessels, stone axes and clubs. They did not own the land on which they lived, as we do, but they claimed the right to the forest for hunting and to the lakes and streams for fishing. 

When the Indians began to depend upon corn and vegetables for food, they found that they had to live near their fields. The corn-growers gave up their wandering and hunting to settle down and care for their crops. As more and more Indians raised corn, the groups of dwellings grew larger, and villages came into being. Sometimes they would leave their fields and go on long hunting trips.

Their houses were crude affairs. In some parts of the

EARLY AMERICA 

aze 2862 53 892 

AN INDIAN VILLAGE 

This picture was drawn several hundred years ago. What does it show? country there were only wigwams (wig'womz) or tepees (tē pēz) of skins and bark. In the Southwest they were of stone or of sun-dried brick, sometimes five or six stories high, and sheltered several families. 

Corn-growing also led to the making of better and more beautiful pottery to take the place of the old clay and stone pots that once were used. were used. Some of the

AMERICA: A SLEEPING WORLD 

5 Indians became skillful in making bowls, pitchers, and bottles of artistic design (dē zin').

The growing of corn and other vegetables showed the Indians how important the weather is to the successful farmer. They worshiped the sun, rain, and wind gods, hoping to get good weather conditions. The Indians in the southern part of North America made images or idols (I'dolz) to represent these gods, and built places in which to worship them.

The Indians were seldom seen without bow and arrow, tomahawk (tom'á hawk), or club. Such weapons were used not only to get food in the woods, but to rid the natives of their enemies. War was their delight, and from childhood they were trained in warfare. The savages knew no better.

Perhaps we shall never know for how many hundreds or thousands of years such dark-skinned natives had lived in the forests of America, on its prairies, or along its seacoasts. There are no written accounts of early 

American history that go back that far. But we are certain that a thousand years ago the people of Europe and Asia had no knowledge of the North American Indians who worked, played, and fought on a slumbering continent. 

3. THE VIKINGS: THE FIRST VISITORS 

About a thousand years ago there lived in Europe around the bays of Norway, Sweden, and Denmark a bold race of men called Vikings (vi'kingz). They were called Vikings, not because they were kings, but because they lived on a vik or bay.

EARLY AMERICA 

The Vikings spent their lives at the water's edge and became the most skillful sailors of their time. Their boats were small but very graceful and speedy. They were driven through the water partly by sails and partly by oars. Their prows were high and often had on their tops a carving of some terrible-looking animal 

A VIKING SHIP 

with staring eyes and wide-open mouth to b frighten their enemies. In these boats the Vikings roved about the seas in search of plunder or a good fight. 

The Vikings were fond of all kinds of sports that called for strength and endurance. Swimming matches, weight-lifting, tug-of-war, wrestling, jumping, spear-throwing, ball-playing, and butting heads together were some of their favorite kinds of amusements. 

One of the most famous of the Vikings was a large, powerful man called "Leif (laf) the Lucky.' 

His real name was Leif Erics-son (ĕr'ik sun) and his father's name was Eric the Red.

Settlements had been made by the Vikings in Iceland and Greenland. The King of Norway, who was a good friend of Leif, sent him in the summer of the year 1000 to teach Christianity to the settlers in Greenland. Both

AMERICA: A SLEEPING WORLD 

ad ir ad Leif and the King had become Christians some time before.

Leif started out on this missionary (mi'shŭn â'rē) nd journey, but soon lost his way. Instead of touching the coast of Greenland, as he expected to do, he went far to the south and soon found himself on the shores of a new and strange land. This new and strange land proved to be America. al to [1 28 !

Leif and his men left their boats and went ashore to look the country over. They gazed in open-eyed won- 

D der at the plants, trees, and animals such as they had 3 never seen before. Finally, in their wanderings they found a great abundance of wild grapes which they ate with much delight. They named the place Vinland (vin'land), or the land of vines.

Nobody knows exactly where Vinland was, but it was probably near Massachusetts (măs'á chu'sets). Here

Leif and his men, about thirty in number, spent the winter. Then other settlers came, but they must have had a cold, hard time of it, for they soon went away again. 

All this happened before the invention (in věn´shun) of printing, and no written record was made of the new land until long afterwards. Stories of the voyages of the Vikings, however, were handed down by word of mouth, and were called the sagas (sä'gáz) or sayings of the Northmen. We can now read the sagas in print. 

Very few people of the time ever heard of Leif and his voyage to America. Even Leif himself had no idea that he had found a great continent. He was really lost, and did not know where he was. 

The story of the strange visit of the Vikings is inter-

EARLY AMERICA 

9000

VIKINGS ON THE AMERICAN SHORE 

esting, but nothing ever came of it. No colonies were started, and the forests of the unknown world were not cut away. It is of no more importance to us now than the "visit of a flock of sea gulls."

The real discovery (dis kuv'er e) of America was not made until nearly five hundred years after the first visitors had come and gone. And America, the unknown world, continued to sleep, undisturbed.

PART ONE OLD EUROPE WAKES UP

OLD EUROPE WAKES UP 

For thousands of years a large part of the world was unknown to the people of Europe. Then, about six hundred years ago, Europeans began to wake up. The thrilling adventures of the daring and clever Polos of Venice aroused the interest of Europeans in the rich lands of India and China, known as the East. The people of Europe wanted the spices, the gold and silver, the silks and satins, and the precious stones which the new lands could supply. 

Besides being interested in the riches of the East, the Europeans, who were very religious, longed with a deep desire to see the Holy Land. Thousands of pilgrims trudged hundreds of weary miles to visit the holy places where Christ had lived. 

There they met the Turks, who had seized the Holy Land, and for many years the European Christians fought to drive the Turks out. The wars that followed were called Crusades, because they were fought for the sake of the cross. While in the Holy Land, the Crusaders had a better opportunity to enjoy the products from the East. 

But the East was thousands of miles away, and Eastern goods could be brought to Europe only over trade routes that were long and full of danger. Robbers and pirates seemed always ready to steal the goods and take the lives of the traders. Then, too, the Turks collected high taxes from the traders for the right to cross their lands. A new, all-water route to the East was greatly needed. 

To find a route to the East, Prince Henry the Navigator opened a school for sailors in Portugal. After years of failure, Diaz and Da Gama, sailing south, finally found an all-water route around Africa to India.

About the same time two new inventions helped trade and travel-the compass and printing. The compass gave sailors their traveling directions, and printing brought books and information to all people who could read. Soon knowledge spread rapidly through the European world. 

It had taken a long time, but old Europe was waking up.

Comstock 

PRINCE HENRY THE NAVIGATOR

1. HOW MARCO POLO BROUGHT TO EUROPE THE KNOWLEDGE OF THE EAST 1. WHY EUROPEANS WERE INTERESTED IN THE EAST 

We must all eat to live, but sometimes our appetites are poor. Perhaps the food does not taste right, or perhaps it all tastes alike.

That was often the case when Marco Polo (märʼkō pō'lō) was a boy. At that time people were usually unable to feed their cattle over the winter. Instead, they killed most of them in the fall and smoked or salted the flesh for winter use. In those days there were no iceboxes. Meat was usually tough (tuf) and had a strong taste; cooks were generally not very skillful. Someone discovered, however, that spices would preserve meat and in addition would improve its flavor as well as add to the attractiveness of other foods. 

But spices did not grow in Europe. They came from lands thousands of miles away-far to the East. 

Marco Polo's father and uncle were traders in spices. 

They traveled thousands of miles from Venice (věnʼis), their home city, to secure spices to sell in Europe. The trade paid them handsomely, and they became very wealthy.

But how could they become rich through buying and selling spices? Today nutmegs and mace, cloves and 11

EARLY AMERICA 

allspice, pepper and cinnamon are so cheap and last so long that most people think very little about them. But in the time of Marco Polo spices were very expensive, because it cost a great deal to bring them from the faraway spice lands of the East.

But spices were not all that the Polos brought back to Europe. They had costly precious stones to show to surprised Europeans (ūrō pēʼánz)-diamonds, rubies, sapphires (sǎf'írz), pearls, emeralds. Among their treasures from the East were also silks, satins, velvets, linens, and other fine materials. Such wares were much in demand in Europe; for both men and women liked to dress richly, especially at the receptions and parties held in the palaces of the kings and nobles. 

2. MARCO POLO GOES TO CHINA 

Marco Polo had often heard the exciting stories of his father and his uncle, and he had seen the beautiful things they had brought from China. It was natural that he, too, should want to visit the spice lands. He was a large, sturdy lad, now seventeen years old, and he was lonely in Venice. He felt that he was old enough to see the world, and the hardships of the journey meant nothing to him. He would work and he would suffer, if necessary, but he felt that he must go with his father and his uncle when they started on their second long trip to the East.

You can well imagine how he teased his father to say yes. At last he got his wish, and the little company set out in a fine Venetian (vě ně'shan) ship. 

At Constantinople (kon'stǎn ti nō'pl) they had to

MARCO POLO 

13 leave their vessel and travel overland. The days dragged by, yet each held some new interest for Marco. Sometimes on foot, sometimes on horseback or on camels, they slowly journeyed eastward, trading with the strange people they met on the way. 

For three years they plodded on. Mountains and deserts did not hold them back, nor did the burning summers and freezing winters. On the way they saw little yellow people weaving silk, carving ornaments, and making trinkets of gold and silver.

They passed an oil well, a strange sight to Europeans, and they saw their first coal.

A VENETIAN SHIP 

What are the long straight lines from the side of the ship to the water? 

At last they reached 

China, then known as Cathay (kǎ thā'). The elder 

Polos already knew the ruler of the country, Kublai Khan (koo'bli kän'), and they went straight to his summer palace. Here they were received very kindly. (See map, page 24.)

The Grand Khan, as Kublai Khan was known, liked the young man as soon as he met him. He wanted 

Marco to enter his service, and the boy was delighted to do so.

It took Marco only a short time to learn several of the Eastern languages, and to become acquainted with

EARLY AMERICA 

THE POLO BROTHERS RECEIVING A GIFT FROM THE GRAND KHAN 

This picture was drawn several hundred years ago. It shows how the artist thought the Grand Khan must have looked as he sat upon his throne. His servant is giving a mirror to the travelers. the ways of the strange little yellow people. His knowledge of languages helped him greatly when he carried messages for the Grand Khan to different parts of the country. Sometimes such errands required as long as six months. When he returned, he told the Khan about the people he had seen and described how they lived and worked. You may be sure this pleased the emperor and he paid Marco well for his services. 

Seventeen years passed by-a long time to spend in a strange country. Marco's father and uncle had become old men. They longed to go back to Venice, for they were homesick. They had become very rich, for the Grand Khan had allowed them to trade in different parts of China. They wished to live at ease among their old friends. The emperor would have liked them to remain, but at last gave his consent for them to leave and

MARCO POLO 

15 they started for home. This was in the year 1300. How many years ago was that? 

3. MARCO POLO RETURNS TO VENICE 

After a journey of four years by ship and by camel, by donkey and on foot, the Polos came at last to Venice, three shabby travelers. No one recognized them, of course, for people change greatly in twenty-four years. 

Many of their old friends had died, and those who still lived found it hard to believe that the poor travelers were the rich Polos.

Yes, they were rich, but they were also wise. They had made themselves look poor so that robbers would not attack them on the long journey.

Several days after their return the Polos gave a great feast to which they invited their friends. To the surprise of their guests, they appeared in one gorgeous garment after another, first silk, then satin, then velvet. 

But greater surprises followed. From the seams of their shabby traveling garments they took out rubies and pearls and diamonds. Then the people of Venice believed that they were really the Polos, and honored them by giving them high positions in the city. 

For several years they lived comfortably in Venice, always glad to tell about their adventures in China. 

4. THE BOOK OF SER MARCO POLO 

Several years after the Polos returned from China a war broke out between Venice and Genoa (jen'ō á). 

Marco was given command of a Venetian ship. In the battle that followed he was captured with his ship and

EARLY AMERICA 

spent the next year in prison. While confined in Genoa, he told his fellow-prisoners about his wonderful years in China.

One of the prisoners asked Marco why he did not write a book about his travels. But Marco did not like to spend long hours writing a book. Instead, he dictated his story to the other prisoner, who wrote its title on the front page: The Book of Ser Marco Polo. This book is very exciting. It contains not only what really happened to the Polos in China and on their long journeys, but also what Marco imagined had happened, all woven together into a good story. 

The book was copied and carried into other lands. 

Many people believed all of it, but some believed only the parts about the gold and silver, the precious stones, the rich cloths, and the spices of the Far East. 

They had seen these things when the Polos came back to Venice, and "seeing is believing."

MARCO POLO 

In the book Marco told of sailing so far south that the 

North Star disappeared below the horizon. Men wondered at this; for in Europe they could see the North 

Star every clear night throughout the year. Perhaps, after all, the earth was round, and not flat. Who could say?

MARCO POLO 

17 02 The book also told of a great sea east of China and 

Japan. Perhaps, said thinkers and wise men, that sea is the Atlantic, which reaches westward around the earth to far-away China.

The people who read The Book of Ser Marco Polo felt a new interest in geography and travel. They, too, wanted to find a way to China and to become rich, like the Polos.

2. HOW THE CRUSADES AWAKENED EUROPE'S INTEREST IN THE EAST 1. WHY THE CRUSADES BEGAN 

People who believe in Jesus Christ are called Christians. It so happened that long before Marco Polo went on his travels in China, thousands of European Christians had also traveled to the east of Europe. Their goal, however, was not China, but the land where Jesus had lived.

These Christian travelers desired to visit the holy places where Jesus was born, and taught, and died. 

These places, and others that Jesus knew, are called the Holy Land. Eager to see this sacred region, thousands of Christians trudged over Europe and western Asia or sailed across the Mediterranean (mědi ter rā'nē ăn) Sea, suffering great hardships on the way. Because they had to travel on foot a long distance, they were called pilgrims.

For hundreds of years pilgrims had visited the Holy Land, collecting little keepsakes, or relics, to carry home to Europe. No one had stopped them or charged them money for entering the Holy Land. 

Then a warlike people who were not Christians conquered the Holy Land and held it for more than a thousand years. They were the Turks, a strong, fighting 18

THE CRUSADES 

19 people, whose homeland touched the Mediterranean Sea and reached far back into Asia (a'zhá). The Turks saw a way to make money from the pilgrims. "Why not charge the hated Christian a tax to come into the Holy 

Land, which is now ours?" they asked one another.

But in spite of danger and expense the pilgrims continued to visit the holy places. Sometimes they were robbed or killed by the Turks; sometimes they were carried away as slaves. But on they came.

Naturally the Christians did not like to have the Turks in the Holy Land. They thought that the country where 

Christ had lived ought to belong to Christians. Finally they became so excited and angry that they formed a great army to drive the Turks from the Holy Land, crying out, "It is God's will; it is God's will!" From all parts of Europe they came, proudly wearing the red cross of the Church on their breasts. This was the badge of the Crusader (krū sād'er), which means a fighter for the cross. The wars for the holy cause were called 

Crusades (krū sādz'). The badge of the Turks was the 

Crescent. 

2. WHAT THE CRUSADERS LIKED IN THE EAST 

Thousands of Crusaders started for the Holy Land. 

Some of them burned with the desire to rescue the Holy Land from the Turks. Some were tired of living peaceful lives in Europe and were eager for travel and adventure. Others among them sought for trade, for power, for lands.

The Crusades lasted for almost two hundred years. 

Sometimes the Turks would win, sometimes the Chris-

EARLY AMERICA 

tians.

CRUSADERS MARCHING TO JERUSALEM 

Then there were months of rest between these battles of the Cross and the Crescent. 

While the Christians were in the Holy Land, they often had to buy goods from their enemies. The Turks had strange things to sell-good things, the Christians thought, which they had found it hard to get in Europe. 

These rare products were spices, silks, ivory, and precious stones. The Europeans came to know and to want the goods which the Turks had secured from India and China. Can you see how the desires of the Christians would bring about more trade with the East ?.

Besides buying goods which came from the East, the

THE CRUSADES 

21 Christians also purchased products raised in the Holy Land or in the countries near by. They greatly enjoyed the new foods-oranges, lemons, plums, rice, and sugar.

When they returned to Europe, they took the new products with them so that they could raise them on their own lands.

The Crusaders also became fond of silks for clothing instead of scratchy wool; fond of beautiful rugs and carpets for their homes which were bare and plain ; and fond of soft mattresses for their hard beds. They also learned to use soap and to take baths. They liked the beauties and comforts of life in the Holy Land, and naturally they wished to enjoy the same things in their own countries.

Thus a lively trade developed between Europe and the Near East, as the Holy Land and the countries in western Asia are called. Many Italians, who were the leaders in this trade, became very rich. Ships from Italy, especially from Venice and Genoa, carried soldiers, pilgrims, and supplies from Europe to the Holy Land, and brought back the products of the East which Europeans wanted to eat and to wear, but could not obtain in their own countries.

The Crusaders did not succeed in their attempt to drive the Turks out of the Holy Land. But the ways of living of the Crusaders were greatly changed by the months and years they spent in strange lands. Although they failed to carry out their first plan, they returned to Europe with new products and new ideas, and with a desire for more travel.

Most important of all, perhaps, the Crusades, as well

EARLY AMERICA 

as the travels of Marco Polo, made the people of Europe interested in the East and eager to trade with it.

3. WHY TRAVEL TO THE EAST WAS DIFFICULT 1. TRADE BY THE NORTHERN ROUTE 

1 Now that most of the people of Europe wanted the products of the East, what routes did the traders take to supply their desires? The answer to the question will be clear by examining the map on page 24, which shows the three main trade routes to the East. 

For many years Genoa and Venice were rivals for the rich Eastern trade. The merchants of Genoa used the northern route more than the other two. But there were traders also from Venice and Florence, as well, who secured spices, fine cloths or fabrics, perfumes, and precious stones by way of the Ægean (ējē′ăn), Black, and 

Caspian (kǎs'pē ăn) Seas. Most of the northern route was by land. 

To the market-places along the Caspian Sea and in

Asia Minor came the products of China and India to be sold to the Italian merchants. In order to reach the trading centers, the precious goods were carried by Chinese junks, by caravans, by mule trains, on horseback, or on men's shoulders.

It was a weary march of many months over mountain and desert during burning summers and freezing winters. The men often suffered from hunger and thirst, 23

TRADE ROUTES TO THE EAST 

for it was hard to carry enough food and water for themselves and their camels. They were sometimes attacked by wild tribes, and many were killed and left in the wilderness. Others died from the hardships. 

You will readily see that they could not carry heavy goods such a long way. Camels heavily loaded could travel only about two miles an hour. Then, too, goods were often lost or soiled from too much handling. So the traders dealt chiefly in goods that were small but costly.

The Eastern merchants bought from the Italians such

TRAVEL TO THE EAST 

25 European goods as they could secure in the marketplaces near the Mediterranean-woolen and linen cloth, hides, coral, and metal. A long line of towns and cities grew up on the shores of the Black Sea and the Mediterranean.

But Italian traders were not to hold the northern route forever. In 1453 the Turks captured Constantinople, which was the key city for the trade by the northern route. You will remember that the Turks made the 

Europeans pay for entering the Holy Land. Here was another chance for the Turks to collect money, and from that time on they made the traders pay heavy taxes to load and unload the Eastern products at Constantinople. 

2. TRADE BY THE MIDDLE ROUTE 

Although the northern route brought the most money to European traders, the middle route was the oldest. 

On the map you will see that it followed the Mediterranean Sea to Asia, then overland to the Tigris (tí'gris) and Euphrates (ū frā'tēz) Rivers, and by way of the Persian Gulf to India. The overland journey was made by caravan, of course, and was much shorter than the northern route.

Eastern traders came from India to the head of the Persian Gulf, where their goods were taken to the Black 

Sea and to the Mediterranean. The traders brought not only the products of India, but also those from China. 

In their cargoes were spices and pepper, precious stones of many kinds, chains of gold, drugs and perfumes, beautiful fabrics of silk and linen, and rugs and dishes, especially what we now call china. There was sugar, too,

EARLY AMERICA 

which some Europeans thought was the best of all the products of the East.

It was the short journey by caravan across a small part of Asia that made the middle route the best for 

Eastern trade.

Then the Turks captured that part of western Asia over which the route ran, and the traders had to pay a heavy tax for permission to pass that way. Besides the tax, there was always the danger of being robbed or killed by the Turks, who seemed to lurk everywhere. 

Thus the taxes and the losses made the middle route an expensive one. The traders needed a better way to get the products of the East. 

3. TRADE BY THE SOUTHERN ROUTE 

The southern route followed the Mediterranean, the Red, and the Arabian (á rā’bē ăn) Seas to India. (See map, page 24.) There the European traders received products from the Far East. 

Chinese and Japanese junks gathered goods along the coast of China and Japan and among the islands to the south, buying and selling as they went. Few Chinese merchants went farther west than Malacca (má lăk'á), which became a great Eastern trading center. Traders from India brought their products to Malacca to sell, and they, in turn, bought what the Chinese had to sell from their country.

The Indian traders carried their Eastern purchases from Malacca to Calicut (kǎl'i kŭt) and the ports near by. From Calicut the cargoes went by ship up the west coast of India to the head of the Persian Gulf and over-

TRAVEL TO THE EAST 

27 To land to the Mediterranean by the middle route, or west across the Arabian Sea, up the Red Sea to its head, across to the Nile, and down to the Mediterranean by the southern route. 

Because the caravan journey was short by the southern route, the traders were able to buy and sell more of the heavy goods. Rugs and carpets, spices, metals, sugar, and precious stones were carried to Europe over this route.

Products like these could be bought in India and China at very low prices. But the cost of the long journey to and from the East made it necessary to charge much higher prices in Europe. It is said that goods were sold for five times as much in Europe as they cost in the East. It is no wonder that the traders became rich! 

But there was always the danger from the Turks. 

They had conquered Constantinople, interfering with the traders who used the northern route. Then they had taken over that part of Asia used by the merchants along the middle route and taxed them heavily. And now they were drawing near to the southern route. 

A new route to the East must be found, for the trade was too valuable to lose. But the new route, if possible, must be one which the Turks could not block, and one which did not require so much loading and unloading. 

An all-water route would be the best. But how could this be found?

4. HOW PORTUGAL FOUND A WATER ROUTE TO THE INDIES 1. PRINCE HENRY THE NAVIGATOR 

Could an all-water route to India be found? Many 

European countries were interested in the problem, particularly the countries in western Europe. The journeys we have read about so far were all made by Italians, for in those days the cities of Italy had the best ships. 

Then, too, Italy was favorably placed on the Mediterranean Sea; for the Italians were nearer to the East than any other European people except the Greeks, who at that time were controlled by the Turks. 

Another small country of Europe, Portugal (pôrʼtūgål) by name, had a long seacoast on the Atlantic Ocean. 

And the Portuguese (pôr'tū gēz) were waking up. If the Italian trade with the East was hindered or stopped by the Turks, the Portuguese could make a great deal of money by opening up a wholly new passage. "What should this new passage be?" was the question that occupied much of their time and attention. 

The answer came from Prince Henry the Navigator (năv'igātôr). He was one of five sons of the King of Portugal. But court life did not suit him, nor did life in the army. As a boy he had liked to be alone and to study. When he became a man, he left his father's 29

ROUTES OF DIAZ AND DA GAMA 

court and took up the study of the stars and the making of maps and new instruments for the use of sailors. He had a tower built near the sea where he could carry on his work without interruption or interference. 

For forty years Prince Henry continued his studies. 

Sometimes he sent out ships, and when they returned he listened eagerly to the captains' reports. In this way he was able to add to his maps and to correct his first mistakes.

By looking at the map on this page you will see that the northern coast of Africa is not far from Portugal.

PORTUGAL'S WATER ROUTE 

31 Prince Henry, of course, knew about this northern coast, for he had been there in 1418, but he did not know how large Africa was. This he wanted to find out, for he believed that a ship could reach India by sailing south and east around Africa.

Very few people had this idea. Some wise men of the day declared that India could never be reached in that way and that Africa had no southern end. Prince

Henry thought the plan worth trying, however, although he knew that it would require a great deal of careful thought, as well as money, ships, and men. 

Prince Henry established a school for sailors and shipbuilders, the first of its kind in the world. The teachers were the best map-makers, the most skillful makers of instruments to help sailors, the ablest shipbuilders, and the greatest travelers Prince Henry could find. The sailors who studied at this school were sent out in ships which were built to sail down the coast of Africa and around it, if that were possible. 

Ship after ship started out, but all came back; their captains said that Africa seemed to have no end. Many of the ships sailed farther south than those that had gone before, but still the end of Africa was not reached. 

The Portuguese traded with the people they found along the coast of Africa, as Prince Henry had ordered them to do. 

But they were too fearful to sail far from shore. 

Most sailors in those days believed the strange stories which they had been told about unknown seas. They feared the sea monsters that might devour them in a single mouthful. They were afraid that the sun might send a shower of fire to burn them. They feared that

EARLY AMERICA 

giant waves might swallow their vessels. They feared everything that they had heard and all that they imagined about the "Sea of Darkness," as the Atlantic was then called.

Sixteen years went by before the Portuguese sailed beyond the deserts of northern Africa. During these years they traded with the Arabs (ărăbs) along the coast, but now they came upon black people, who are called Negroes today. The sailors seized some of them and took them back to 

Portugal, where the Negroes were sold as slaves. GA ·00000 

DANGERS IN THE SEA OF DARKNESS 

A lively slave trade soon grew up. More ships than ever now went from 

Portugal down the coast of Africa. The slave traders who ventured to sail farther south found a land where there was gold. 

The natives exchanged gold dust, ivory, and slaves for the horses and trinkets brought by the Portuguese. 

The slave trade and the gold and ivory made many people eager to go to the southern part of Africa. But the route was Prince Henry's, for his money and his school had started the voyages. Portugal grew rich and powerful through the African trade, and for a time people seemed to forget that Prince Henry's plan was to discover an all-water route to the Indies. 

But Prince Henry had not forgotten. He continued his efforts to improve maps for his sailors and to take from them the fear of the "Sea of Darkness." But

PORTUGAL'S WATER ROUTE 

33 before the Portuguese reached the southern point of 

Africa, Prince Henry died. 

2. DIAZ SAILS AROUND THE CAPE OF GOOD HOPE 

The work begun by Prince Henry went on after his death until at last success came. The first sailor to go around the southern end of Africa was Diaz (de'äth), who had set sail from Portugal in two small ships. He was blown by a great storm, farther and farther to the 1 south, far out of sight of land. After the storm he sailed to the north for several days when, to his surprise, he saw land to the west of him instead of to the east. 

Without knowing it, he had sailed around the southern end of Africa! (See map, page 30.)

By this time his sailors were so frightened that they demanded that he turn back and take them home. Although he was unwilling to do so, Diaz consented and returned to Portugal. He did not succeed in reaching 

India by an all-water route, but he showed that such a voyage was possible.

The King of Portugal listened eagerly to the report which Diaz made. When the King saw the name, "Cape of Storms," which Diaz had written on the map at the southern end of Africa, the King changed it to "Cape of Good Hope," for now, he declared, there was "good hope" of reaching India by the route Diaz had found. 

3. VASCO DA GAMA REACHES INDIA 

A few years later the King sent a Portuguese nobleman, who was also a sea-captain, Vasco da Gama (väs'kō dá gä’má) by name, with a small fleet of four good

EARLY AMERICA 

ships. He ordered Da Gama to follow the route of Diaz, but to keep on until he reached India. 

After rounding the Cape of Good Hope Da Gama refused to turn back, as his men urged. Thus it happened that in 1498 he reached India by an all-water route. He traded with the natives, although at first they did not trust the Portuguese strangers.

VASCO DA GAMA 

Several months later Da 

Gama returned to Portugal with his report of a successful voyage and a cargo of the rich products of the East.

He was received with high honors and was later made ruler over the land he had discovered.

Now that India had been reached by an all-water route, Portugal sent ships to the 

East every year to trade with the people. Trade no longer centered in the Mediterranean Sea over the three main routes. As the years passed, the Atlantic Ocean gradually became the chief scene of the world's trade, and Portugal won first place among European trading countries.

Vasco da Gama was the first European to sail directly to India. But Diaz, by sailing around the southern end of Africa, had prepared the way for him. Before either 

Da Gama or Diaz made his important voyage, Prince Henry the Navigator had thought out the plan. He had

PORTUGAL'S WATER ROUTE 

1 furnished the money and the school for sailors and he had held to his idea for almost fifty years. Perhaps he deserves the main credit for finding an all-water route to the Indies !

5. HOW A NEW WORLD WAS FOUND 1. THE COMING OF THE COMPASS AND BETTER MAPS 

For hundreds of years sailors had been afraid to lose sight of land. They had hugged the shore, and had dreaded to go to unknown places! About the time of 

Prince Henry, however, several new inventions aided the Portuguese sailors in making their long and dangerous voyages.

At sea all directions look much alike. During the daytime when the sun is shining, it is possible to say which way is east and which is west. And at night, if the sky is not clouded, one can tell whether one is sailing north or south. But on stormy days and nights, when the sun and the stars could not be seen, the sailors of early days had no way of finding directions and therefore they did not like to sail out of sight of land.

About the time of the Crusades several Italian travelers brought to the Mediterranean a wonderful invention to help sailors. It is believed that this instrument was first made in China, for the Chinese used it long before the Europeans had ever heard about it. The new invention was the compass, with its needle pointing steadily toward the north.

Those of you who have seen a compass will remember its round face, like that of a clock. But instead of the 36

A NEW WORLD FOUND 

37 hours, there are the letters N for North, E for East, S for South, and W for West. 

You have probably heard that the flyers of today all have compasses in their airplanes. People who explore strange places, deep jungles or swamps, also carry compasses so as not to lose their way. No matter how stormy or foggy the day may be, the needle of the compass always points to the North.

After the compass came into use, sailors were not so frightened when land disappeared from sight. 

They depended on the compass to give them their direction. It helped to make sailing safe, and more sailors were willing to start out on long voyages. 

Prince Henry had compasses put on the Portu- NW WNW

WSW

SW SMS NNW

NNE

SE NE ENE ESE SSE 

HOW A MARINER'S COMPASS LOOKS 

guese ships that sailed down the west coast of Africa, and Da Gama used a compass to help him find India by an all-water route. 

Improved maps and charts also made voyages safer.

Prince Henry, as we have seen, had the best map-makers in the world working for him in his school for sailors. 

You will remember, too, that his maps were changed and corrected whenever new places were found. On the maps were shown the routes taken by the vessels and dangerous places were also pointed out-sharp rocks,

EARLY AMERICA 

sand-bars, and swift currents. Such maps and charts helped sailors to avoid many dangers at sea.

Laws, too, were passed to aid sailors. Before setting out on a voyage each ship was inspected by officers to make sure that it was strong enough for the trip. Even today inspectors carefully go over all vessels before they are allowed to sail in order to see that they are fireproof, water-tight, and able to travel safely through all kinds of weather. The invention of the compass and the improvement of maps and charts caused more people to travel, and travel brought greater learning. 

2. THE INVENTION OF THE PRINTING PRESS 

About the year 1450 another invention added to the learning of the people. Marco Polo's book was written by hand, and all copies of it made at that time were handwritten. This was a slow, tiresome process; books cost a great deal of money and only the rich could afford to own them. Then, about one hundred and fifty years after Marco Polo, a German named John Gutenberg (goo'ten berg) invented the printing press, and in a short time printing presses were set up in many parts of Europe. The days of hand-written books were over. 

The first printing press used what is called movable type. The letters were of lead, and hundreds of them, capitals and small letters, were stored in separate, small boxes. To "set the type," as it is called, the right letters were placed in a metal groove and "locked up." 

Next the ink was spread on the letters and a sheet of paper was pressed down on the inked type. In this way the printer could set the type and print a whole page

A NEW WORLD FOUND 

39 

GUTENBERG SHOWS HIS FIRST PROOF 

at once. Later he learned how to print several pages at a time.

After the invention of the printing press, many more books were written and printed. Because printing was quicker than hand-writing, books were sold much more. cheaply than before. Since even the poor could afford to buy books, people could learn quickly about new countries and new inventions.

Thus Gutenberg's wonderful machine helped to wake up the people of old Europe. It also forced them to find cheaper material than parchment for their books. In the days of Marco Polo and the Crusaders, parchment

EARLY AMERICA 

or sheepskin, was used for books, but it was very expensive. About the time Gutenberg invented the printing press, however, merchants began to bring paper from China.

Another invention that came from China was gunpowder, although in the early days it was used only in firecrackers. Later, a German found that the powder could be used to throw objects for some distance. Then cannons were made and gunpowder was found to be of great help in protecting people and their lands from their enemies.

If it had not been for the compass, few men would have dared to sail into the broad and unknown Atlantic, and America might still be a sleeping continent, unknown to Europe.

If it had not been for printing, few people of Europe would have learned of America until long after it was discovered.

Both inventions helped to prepare the way for Christopher Columbus (kris'tō fer kólum'bus). The building of better and larger ships, the introduction of the compass, the making of new maps and charts, and the printing of books of many kinds, all aided him in his voyage from Europe in search of a new water route to the East-and to the finding of a new world.

PART TWO THE SPANIARDS HAPPEN UPON A NEW WORLD

THE SPANIARDS HAPPEN UPON A NEW WORLD 

Through his reading and study Columbus, a Genoese sailor, came to believe that the earth was round and that India and China could be reached by sailing west. His idea was not new, but he was the first to put it to the test. 

From the King and Queen of Spain Columbus received money and ships with which to make the journey. Deaf to his sailors' grumblings, fears, and threats, he sailed on and on until land was reached. But the island he discovered did not lie off the coast of India, as he supposed. It was part of a New World which he had found by accident. He claimed the land for Spain.

Columbus made three other voyages to the New World, but he never found the riches of the East. 

Other Spaniards then came to America to search for gold, to preach Christianity to the Indians, and to add to the glory of Spain. They explored the wilderness, cut their way through to the Mississippi River and the Pacific Ocean, and conquered the civilized Indians of Mexico and Peru. Cortez and Pizarro sent to Spain large fortunes in gold and silver. 

The New World seemed to be the land of promise, and the King of Spain encouraged many Spaniards to come and settle in the territory.

Homes and missions and schools were built by the Spanish colonists. Farmers raised food for themselves and to sell to 

Spain. Spanish priests taught Christianity to the natives, and the Indians learned to work and study and worship as the Spaniards did. Spanish power grew as the colonies grew, and the mother country, Spain, became one of the richest nations in the world.

The Spaniards were now very much awake.

COLUMBUS' FLAGSHIP

6. SPAIN WAKES UP 1. THE MOORS ARE DRIVEN OUT OF SPAIN 

Five hundred years ago the people who lived in Spain were Spaniards and Moors. The Spaniards were Christians. The Moors, however, were Mohammedans (móhăm'ě dǎnz), who prayed to Allah (ǎl'lá), acknowledged

Mohammed (mó hăm'ěd) as their religious teacher, and read the Koran (kō răn') instead of the Bible. It was hard for the two peoples to understand each other and to live in peace together.

The Spaniards 

Come over to Spain from Africa many centuries before to take what they could get for themselves. In the mountains of the country there had once been rich gold and silver mines, but the Spaniards had lost most of this wealth to their conquerors. were bitter toward the Moors who had In the north and west gradually grew up the Christian kingdoms of Aragon (ără gon), Castile (kǎs tēl'), and Portugal.

They spent much of their time fighting the Moors. The final victory of the Christians was made possible by the marriage of King Ferdinand (fer'dinånd) of Aragon and Queen Isabella of Castile. Their people united in a mighty effort, and in 1492 succeeded in conquering the hated Moors. Most of the Moors returned to Africa, but a few of them remained in Spain 49

EARLY AMERICA 

MOHAMMEDANS AT PRAYER ON THE DESERT 

and became Christians and lived at peace with the Spaniards. 

2. ART AND LITERATURE HELP TO WAKEN SPAIN 

Now that their troubles with the Moors were at an end, the Spaniards were able to give their time and money to something besides fighting. There were lands to be had in other parts of the world. Gold and silver might be discovered, and trade might bring riches to Spain. 

And after five hundred years of fighting, Spain was in sad need of money.

About this time the new art of printing found its way into Spain. Books and poems were written, some of them about religion, for the Spaniards were loyal Catholics; some of the books were about romance and adventure. 

One of the Spanish writers of the time was Cervantes

SPAIN WAKES UP 

51 (sēr văn'tēz), whose Don Quixote (dón kē hō'tā) has been called one of the world's greatest books. When you are a little older, you will enjoy reading about this strange knight and his many thrilling adventures. You may even read the book in Spanish when you are in high school. 

Besides gifted authors and poets, the Golden Age of

Spain produced many fine artists and architects. The artists made pictures and statues and stained glass windows and decorated borders for walls and ceilings, while the architects planned buildings of all kinds― cathedrals and chapels and palaces. Seville (se vil') and Toledo (tō la'dō), two of Spain's largest cities, contained many beautiful buildings famous for their paintings, sculpture, and iron work.

The advances in literature, art, and building awakened Spain. The country now had powerful rulers, people who were interested in books and art, rich cities which were the centers of religion and learning, and sailors who were searching the seas for new lands and new routes to the East.

While Spain was waking up, America still slumbered. 

The Indians stalked their prey, set their crude traps, paddled down swift streams, and wandered across plains and through forests. They knew nothing of Spain, or of 

Europe, for that matter. We shall see what the coming of the Spaniards meant to the Indians, who were the first Americans.

7. HOW THE SPANIARDS HAPPENED TO COME TO AMERICA 1. THE EARLY LIFE AND TRAVELS OF COLUMBUS 

While Spain was waking up, Christopher Columbus was a boy, working and playing along the waterfront of 

Genoa, a beautiful Italian city. You have heard of

Genoa before. It was an important shipping center for the trade with the East.

Christopher was the son of a wool-comber and weaver. 

His father taught him wool-combing, too, but adventure beckoned the boy far out to sea.

He was a restless lad, not content to sit quietly in school. The swelling sails and the foam bounding off the ship's prow kept calling him, and he started out to sea rather early in life, beginning to sail when he was only thirteen. This, of course, put a stop to his schooling, and most of his later education was picked up on the docks, on shipboard, on his travels, and at the courts of kings and queens.

Columbus knew a great deal about geography. He was a skillful map-maker, and for a time made his living by drawing maps and charts for others.

When Columbus grew to manhood, he was tall, wellbuilt, and strong, with red hair and beard. His eyes were clear, steel-gray, and showed him to be firm. When 53

EARLY AMERICA 

he had money enough, he liked to dress well, in the style of the day; and he was fond of yellow beads, red caps, and bright shoes.

Not far from the home of Columbus in Genoa there lived two noblemen who owned many ships. Columbus was fortunate in meeting them, and in making himself liked, for they took him on several long voyages. 

One of these voyages proved especially exciting.

While on the Atlantic, the Italian ships met a number of French vessels and a desperate fight began. Soon flames shot up from several of the ships and the crews had to jump overboard. Columbus jumped with the others, but he was picked up and taken to land. He had been wounded in the fight. When he came to his senses, he coast of Portugal, near the city found himself on the of Lisbon (liz'bon). (See map, page 30.)

Cold, wet, and weary, Columbus was very wretched. 

But it was fortunate for him that he had been shipwrecked at this particular place. Lisbon was then an important seaport, and the Portuguese were among the greatest sailors in the world.

After Columbus had recovered from the shock of the shipwreck, he began to go about the docks and talk with the sailors. He was greatly interested in studying their maps and looking at their instruments. He may have been a nuisance to the sailors, but he learned much about what was going on in different parts of the world. 

When Columbus left Lisbon, he went to England. 

There, too, he chatted with sailors, and learned about the world outside the Mediterranean. For several years he roamed about in an aimless sort of way and then drifted

back to Lisbon. The docks, the shipyards, and the sailors' yarns continued to attract him. He also found a still greater attraction in Lisbon, for there he met a beau- Etiful girl named Felipa (fě lip'á). She was the daughter of a noble family, and it is said that her father in later years helped Columbus to work out his great plans. 

While in Lisbon, Columbus was joined by his younger brother, Bartholomew (bär thōlō mū), a fine, handsome lad of nineteen. Bartholomew had a good education and was skillful in making maps and charts of the sea. He later became his brother's right-hand man. 

2. COLUMBUS PLANS TO REACH THE EAST BY SAILING WEST 

When the two brothers were not sailing the sea, they usually spent hours on the docks, talking with the sailors. 

Wherever they happened to go, they found little groups of men talking excitedly about the East, the East, THE 

GREAT EAST. 

You will remember that trade with the East had made Genoa rich. Merchants who wished to buy the goods of the East often purchased them from the traders of - Genoa, where Columbus was born. 

Then, in 1453, the Turks took possession of Constantinople and seriously interfered with the trade from China to Genoa. They made the caravans pay heavy taxes as they passed through the country and at times threatened to stop them altogether. The Turks also conquered other regions in western Asia and northern Africa and it seemed probable that they might soon interfere with all trade to the East.

Merchants and sailors were greatly disturbed. They said to one another: "We must find a new trade route to 

India and the Great East."

On the streets of Lisbon Columbus heard that the Portuguese planned to reach the East by sailing around the southern point of Africa. Years before, as we have seen, Prince Henry had set up a school for sailors where he trained men and sent them down the west coast of Africa, In this way he hoped to go around Africa and reach India by way of the Indian Ocean.

Columbus did not like this plan. The voyage was sure to be long and dangerous. It also meant crossing the equator, where the heat was said to be too great for human beings, and the water of the ocean was believed to

boil constantly. Then, too, a queer kind of worm was said to bore holes in the sides of the ships and send them to the bottom of the sea.

Columbus thought he had a better plan. Together with other learned men of the day he believed that the earth was a globe or sphere, and that if he sailed west from Spain he would reach India. He did not know, of course, that a great continent lay in the way, although he thought that he might discover islands which had not been known before. But most important of all, he wanted to find an easy, all-water route to the Great East, and to teach Christianity to the natives there. 

The more Columbus traveled and studied, the more convinced he became that the western route was the best way to the Indies. He finally made up his mind he would sail toward the sunset. No one had ever done so before, but he would make the voyage. He would find the East. 3.

COLUMBUS PREPARES FOR THE GREAT VOYAGE 

But to start was not easy. Columbus needed several ships and many men to run them. The expedition would cost a great deal of money and he was a poor man. Besides this, he might not be able to get sailors to join him, even though he had the money for the trip. Few men would be willing to leave their homes and families to sail out into the "Sea of Darkness."

But Columbus was not discouraged. He laid his plans before the King of Portugal and asked for help. The 

King listened with interest and then asked a number of learned men what they thought of Columbus' plan. They

EARLY AMERICA 

COLUMBUS AT THE COURT OF QUEEN ISABELLA 

only laughed at him, and on their advice the King sent 

Columbus away.

Then the Portuguese King secretly sent a vessel from the Cape Verde (verd) Islands, with orders to sail to the west to see if India could be reached that way, as Columbus had planned. The sailors returned to Lisbon unsuccessful, and frightened half to death. They were certain that the plans of Columbus were all a dream. 

When news of this shabby trick reached Columbus, he was very angry, and he decided to leave Portugal at once and seek help elsewhere.

Columbus then set out for Spain to ask aid of King Ferdinand and Queen Isabella. Again he told his story and again the wise men were asked what they thought. 

They shot question after question at Columbus. Sev-

eral of them did not believe that the earth was round. "Do you think," asked one of them, "that we are standing on top of a globe and that on the opposite side of the earth there are people with their feet up and their heads hanging down?" "And do you think," asked another, "that on the opposite side of the earth the snow, rain, and hail fall up instead of down?" "And what about the trees ?" inquired a third. "Do they grow with their roots up and their branches down?" The wise men thought it was all nonsense and advised the King not to help Columbus. 

Then Bartholomew, the younger brother of Columbus, went to the King of England to request his aid. The 

King, who was very stingy, did not wish to risk his money and Bartholomew pushed on to France, but again met with disappointment.

When Portugal, England, and France refused to help him, Columbus returned to Spain. The King and Queen, Ferdinand and Isabella, who had just won their great victory over the Moors, now told him that they would supply the money to fit out three ships for the expedition (ĕx'pě dĭ'shun). Columbus was delighted, and at once began to prepare for the voyage. His opportunity had come at last.

Three small ships were soon made ready, the Santa Maria (sǎn'tă mă rē’á), the Pinta (pēn'tá), and the Nina (nēn'ya), or the "Baby." Nowadays most people would think anyone rash or foolish who would dare to cross the ocean in such tiny boats.

Ninety sailors and thirty other men made up the company that embarked on the three vessels. These included

EARLY AMERICA 

royal officers, pilots, a doctor, and several other persons, one of whom could speak several languages. 

4. THE DISCOVERY OF AMERICA 

Finally all was in readiness, and on Friday, August 3, 1492, a little before sunrise, the sails filled out and the three ships started toward the unknown. They set sail from Palos (pä'lōs), while their friends on shore waved them a tearful goodbye. (See map, page 56.)

Columbus himself wrote an account of this wonderful voyage. It is an interesting story. Three days after starting, the rudder of the Pinta was broken. Columbus thought that several of the sailors, who were afraid and wanted to go back to Spain, had disabled the ship. But the rudder was repaired at the Canary Islands, and the ships again started on their way. 

Signs of land appeared and disappeared, but no land was seen. The sailors grew nearly mad with fear. The winds drove them farther and farther from the shores of Spain. Would they change so as to bring the ships back again? That was the question which many of the sailors asked one another. 

Storms also came.

Then many of the men gave up in despair and begged Columbus to turn back. 66 'Brave Adm'r'l, say but one good word: 

What shall we do when hope is gone?' 

The words leapt like a leaping sword: 'Sail on! sail on! sail on! and on!'"'

But there was no comfort in this answer for the frightened sailors. They began to murmur and to mutter threats. "Why should all of us go down to death be-

cause of the folly of a madman ?" "Why not leave him to the sea which he loves so well and turn back to our homes? The ocean tells no secrets."

But Columbus was not the kind of man who could be frightened or who would take orders from his sailors. 

He met them quietly and told them that he had set out to find the Indies and that with the help of God he would sail on until he found them. "Then, pale and worn, he kept his deck, And peered through darkness. Ah, that night 

Of all dark nights! And then a speck-

EARLY AMERICA 

On the very next night at 10 o'clock Columbus saw a light ahead. It was moving slowly to and fro in the darkness. All eyes were strained in that direction. 

Finally at 2 o'clock on the following morning land was distinctly seen in the moonlight.

The three small ships now pulled up, drew in their sails, and the men waited impatiently for the day to break. This was Friday, the 12th of October, 1492, a date every American knows. 

The sailors sang, "Glory be to God on High," guns were fired, and flags were unfurled. Columbus, clad in armor and wearing a crimson robe, went ashore. He bent low and saluted the ground. He then unfurled the royal banner of Spain, drew his sword, and took possession of the land in the name of the King and the Queen. He had probably landed on what is now known as Watling's (wät'lingz) Island, but he named it San Salvador (sănsăl'vá dōr), meaning Holy Savior.

By this time the people living on the island had gathered on the shore near the landing place. At first they stood back in fear. Then they dared to go nearer. Their skins were dark and their bodies entirely naked. They had coarse, straight hair on their heads, but none on their faces. They wondered at the beards of the strangers, and when they became bolder they reached out their hands and touched them. They also looked in amazement at the white hands and faces of the newcomers. They thought that Columbus and his men were "visitors from the sky."

The Spaniards and the natives made themselves understood by means of signs. They eagerly exchanged gifts

with one another. Red caps, glass beads, small bells, and other trinkets were given by Columbus. The darkskinned natives, in turn, presented him and his men with parrots, cotton thread, darts, arrowheads, and other things they had made. 

Columbus called the red men Indians, because he believed that he had discovered an island lying off the coast of India. And as Indians they have been known ever since.

One of the first duties of Columbus was to tell the Indians about Christianity, for he wanted to make Christians of them. He thought he could accomplish his purpose more easily by love than by force, and the Spaniards and the Indians soon became very friendly. 

5. EARLY SPANISH COLONIES IN AMERICA 

Columbus felt that his work was not done. He had made an important voyage and had found several new islands, but he had failed to find gold, silk, spices, and the precious stones of the Indies.

In the next ten years he made three other voyages to the west in search of the land of the spices. His efforts were unsuccessful, however, although he discovered many new islands and although he added the northern coast of South America and the coast of Central America to Spain's American possessions. After each voyage he returned home weary and sad, but he still believed the 

East could be found by sailing west.

On his first voyage he had lost his largest ship, the Santa Maria. It was dashed to pieces by the waves on a sandbank. A second ship, the Pinta, had set sail be-

fore Columbus was ready, and he had only the tiny Nina left. It was too small to carry all the men back to Spain, so forty of them were left to make a settlement on the island of Haiti (hā'tē).

They had provisions enough to last for a year. For shelter the men built a rude fort and set up guns for their protection. This was the first colony in the New World. 

When Columbus returned the following year, 1493, he sailed with seventeen ships and fifteen hundred men. 

With him was his younger brother, Diego (dē ā'gō), and also Ponce de Leon (pōn'thā dā lā ōn'), who later discovered Florida.

When Columbus reached the colony with fresh supplies for the forty men he had left behind the winter before, he found the fort destroyed, the provisions gone, and not one of his men to greet him.

EARLY AMERICA 

Grieved and disappointed, but not willing to give up, Columbus set out to build a new fort and town which he called Isabella. A church, a market-place, a storehouse, and several houses were erected, and a stone wall was built around the settlement to protect the colony from all enemies.

But the Spanish colonists in Isabella had constant trouble with the Indians and frequent quarrels among themselves. They were in a new land, endured many hardships, and had few amusements. They also suffered because they had no able leaders. Columbus was away much of the time, exploring the country, and his brother Diego, who proved to be a weak leader, was in charge. during his absences. Things went from bad to worse, and finally the colony died out. 

In spite of the misfortunes and failure of the first two colonies, other Spaniards soon settled in the New World. 

The first settlement to live and grow was made in Santo 

Domingo (sǎn'tō dō min'gō) in 1496.

Columbus had failed to find the way to India and China, but he had found America. He never realized his great discovery, however, for he died believing he had found the Indies. His was the honor of awakening the New 

World from a slumber of centuries. 

6. HOW THE LAND COLUMBUS DISCOVERED RECEIVED THE NAME America 

The sailors of Europe, now wide awake, were eagerly driving their ships in many directions. They no longer feared the "Sea of Darkness" as they had feared it before the great voyage of Columbus.

IC

was

Americus Vespucius (á mer'ĭkus věs pū'shus) another Italian sailor. He was born in the old city of 

Florence, not far from the birthplace of Columbus. He became acquainted with Columbus, and after talking with him, made up his mind that he too would like to make a voyage into unknown seas. 

Like many other Italians, he went to Portugal. There he had the chance to set out with a party of Portuguese sailors who were planning a voyage down the east coast of South 

America (1501). He was delighted, of course, for he and the other sailors thought this might be the new and short way to the riches of the East.

southwestern route had not yet been found.

At first the voyage was 

AMERICUS VESPUCIUS 

very pleasant, and the sailors enjoyed the mild climate and the wonderful scenery. Then their troubles began. 

Great icebergs appeared floating in the sea, threatening to sink the small ships. Again and again, they were tossed by the storms like corks upon the giant waves.

Frightened, Vespucius and his fellow sailors turned for home.

They had gone farther south than any other sailors up to this time, for they had reached the mouth of La Plata (lä plă'tá) River.

EARLY AMERICA 

Vespucius had learned that it pays to advertise. He was also a very interesting writer. His letters contained glowing accounts of his wonderful voyage to the south. 

As a result of his story America received its name. 

People could now see from the long line of seacoast that the newly-discovered land was not an island but a continent. Accordingly a German geographer, who read 

Vespucius' letters, suggested (in 1507) that this new part of the earth be called America, in honor of Americus Vespucius.

8. HOW SPAIN SOUGHT  1. THE POPE'S LINE OF DIVISION 

Before Columbus made his last voyage to America for Spain, another European nation had found an all-water passage to the East. For years, as we have seen, Prince 

Henry the Navigator of Portugal had sought a new route to India and in 1498 Vasco da Gama, sailing under the Portuguese flag, had reached India by the route shown on the map on page 30. He brought back to Portugal a cargo of spices worth sixty times the cost of his long voyage.

Spain was envious of the success of Portugal, and for several years the two countries tried to outdo each other in trade and discovery. The Pope thought it wise to try to settle the difficulty before war broke out between them. 

To end their disputes he drew a line on the map from north to south far west of Europe to separate the lands they might discover. This was known as the Pope's 

Line of Division. 

The Pope declared that the Portuguese should have all the non-Christian lands which they might find east of the line, and the Spanish were to have all the lands they might discover west of it. 70

EARLY VOYAGES OF DISCOVERY AND THE POPE'S LINE OF DIVISION

EARLY AMERICA 2. PONCE DE LEON FINDS FLORIDA 

The colonies begun by Columbus grew in size and number during the next three hundred years. Explorers returning to Spain told fascinating stories about the strange land and its riches. 

IN SEARCH OF THE FOUNTAIN OF YOUTH The Spanish King en couraged his people to cross the Atlantic to found settlements and to search for gold. In this way many small 

Spanish villages and towns sprang up on the islands of the West 

Indies and in Panama. 

As the hunt for gold spread, the early colonies spread also. 

You will recall that on one of his voyages 

Columbus had with him a Spaniard named 

Ponce de Leon, who later became governor of the island of Porto Rico (pōr'tō re'kō). Men have called him "only another Spanish gold hunter," but he was more than that. Like all the early explorers, he sought for gold, but his main object was to find a good place for a colony. 

The Indians had told Ponce de Leon of a Fountain of Youth in the wilderness to the north. They said that

anyone who drank its water would never grow old, and that old people who drank it would become young again. 

This interested Ponce de Leon keenly, for he was a wrinkled old soldier. Crossing the sea to a land he called Florida, he beat through the bushes and undergrowth, drank from every spring, and bathed in every o fountain he found. But alas! His wrinkles did not ti vanish. He did not find the Fountain of Youth, nor has anyone else done so to this day. Instead, he gave Spain la claim to the mainland of North America. 

3. BALBOA DISCOVERS THE PACIFIC 

Another Spaniard who explored the mainland of North

America was Balboa (băl bō'á). He was a farmer on one of the Bahama (bá ha'má) Islands, but he found farming rather dull. Adventure and exploration were more to his liking. He owed so much money to people on the island, however, that they would not let him leave until he paid all he owed. It is said that he made a clever escape by having himself nailed up in a barrel and shipped with a load of provisions to the Isthmus of Panama (īs’mŭs of păn'á mä). (See map, page 71.) 

In Panama he learned from the Indians of a strange and wonderful country and of a mighty sea beyond the mountains to the west. Along the shores of this sea gold was said to be very plentiful, and in the waters of the tumbling mountain streams nuggets of gold were said to glisten.

With a handful of Spaniards, a few Indians, and a pack of bloodhounds, Balboa set out in 1513 to cut his way to the "Golden Sea." Although it is only forty-five

EARLY AMERICA 

BALBOA REACHES THE PACIFIC OCEAN 

Balboa and his men are here pictured as well dressed, fresh, and clean. 

They do not look as if they had suffered on the journey through the jungles of Central America and Panama. Is the picture true or false? miles across the Isthmus, the journey took eighteen days through the tangled swamps and over the rugged mountains. Finally from the top of a peak Balboa gazed down in silence upon the South Sea, now called the Pacific Ocean. On the heights he set up a huge wooden cross. Then the little band crept down to the edge of the sea, where Balboa waded into the water and in the name of Spain took possession of the sea and all the territory it touched. 

4. CORTEZ CONQUERS MEXICO 

Up to this time the Indians whom the Spaniards had met in the New World had been like wild savages. In 

Mexico, however, they found people that were very dif-

ferent from their island or northern cousins. One tribe, the Aztecs (ǎz'těks), had conquered their neighbors and had built up a large empire in Mexico. The capital, Mexico City, contained many fine palaces and beautiful temples of stone.

The Aztecs were skillful in working gold and silver, in carving stones, and in weaving fine cotton cloth. 

Paved roads connected the capital with the seacoast three hundred miles away. Although horses were unknown, runners were trained to carry news and messages swiftly over the paved roads, and the emperor could therefore know what was going on in all parts of his land.

The religion of the Aztecs showed their savage traits. 

They worshiped many gods in great temples resembling the pyramids of ancient Egypt. Captives taken in war were cruelly sacrificed to the war god and their skulls were piled as trophies in mounds outside the temples. 

The empire of the Aztecs was conquered by Hernando Cortez (her năn’dō kōr těz′). As a young man he had attended a university in Spain. Stories of the New 

World interested him more than did his books, however, and when the opportunity came he joined a group of settlers going to Haiti. Here he soon tired of the quiet life of a planter, and joined an expedition that conquered Cuba. In the island of Cuba rumors came to Cortez of rich native cities on the mainland to the west. The governor of Cuba sent a fleet to find out. Although jealous of Cortez, the governor was persuaded to put him - in command of the expedition and before the governor could change his mind, Cortez set sail.

EARLY AMERICA 

CORTEZ FIGHTING THE AZTECS 

There are few stranger stories in history than the story of the conquest of the Aztec empire by the mere handful of Spaniards who followed Cortez. But we must remember that the Aztecs had earned the hatred of their neighbors by their cruel deeds and that the tribes did not care to fight for their masters. In some cases they even joined the Spaniards in the attack. 

Shortly after landing in Mexico, Cortez burned all his ships but one to prevent his unruly followers from de-

serting him. Then he marched inland, and reached 

Mexico City with little difficulty.

The Aztec emperor, Montezuma (mon'tē zū’má), could easily have crushed the little army of three hundred Spaniards and their three thousand allies. But he hesitated, fearing that Cortez might be a god. He had heard that a white god had left Mexico many years before, promising to return.

Montezuma's hesitation brought his undoing, for he was soon in the power of Cortez. The Spaniards then seized large quantities of Aztec gold and silver. But an uprising of the natives against the white conquerors and the unhappy emperor drove the Spaniards from the city. 

Montezuma was killed during this struggle. 

Forced back to the coast, Cortez spent months preparing for a second attack. This time he met with strong fighting, but his efforts were at last successful, and after a long siege, the capital was taken by storm. In the months of the siege the Aztecs suffered from hunger and thirst, for the Spaniards kept food supplies and water from reaching them. With the conquest of Mexico by the Spaniards in 1521, Aztec power came forever to an end. (See map, page 71.)

Mexico City rapidly became the leading Spanish settlement in the New World. The Aztec wealth helped to make Spain for a time the richest country in Europe. 

5. DE SOTO DISCOVERS THE MISSISSIPPI-AND DEATH 

Another Spanish seeker of wealth, particularly gold, who opened up new lands for Spain was Hernando de Soto (her năn'dō dā sō'tō). He had served as a captain

EARLY AMERICA 

DE SOTO DISCOVERS THE MISSISSIPPI 

in an expedition in South America and had found there a fortune in gold and silver. For a time he was governor of Cuba, but he was not content to remain there; he wanted to explore the unknown lands to the northwest.

With nine ships, six hundred and twenty men, as well as horses, dogs, and a large drove of hogs, De Soto landed on the west coast of Florida. He then made his way inland, and for three years wandered about in swamps and jungles until he came to a great river, swift and wide, which he crossed. He named this river the 

Rio Grande (rē'ō grän'dá), but we now call it by its Indian name, the Mississippi. Upon the discovery of this mighty river De Soto claimed for Spain all the land touched by its waters. (See map, page 71.) 

But De Soto was looking for gold, and pressed on into the wilderness. By this time he had only a few men left.

The hardship of pushing through thick woods, worn out and hungry, and over Indian trails with warlike redmen always ready to strike them down, had greatly reduced the members of the expedition. When De Soto died of swamp fever, his few remaining followers buried him in the waters of the Mississippi. He had found no gold in 

North America, but his expedition had opened a vast new territory for Spain.

Another Spaniard who worked his way into the southwestern part of what is now the United States was Coronado (kō'ró nä'dō). It was he who discovered the 

Colorado River, thus extending Spain's possessions still farther to the west. How immense the Spanish empire was becoming! 

6. PIZARRO CONQUERS PERU 

And it was to grow even more, for Cortez was not the only Spaniard who fought successfully against civilized Indians in America. After Cortez conquered Mexico, Pizarro (pă zăr'ō), who had been living in Panama, set out to find what lay along the shores of the South Sea which Balboa had discovered twenty years before. In what is now Peru (pē roo') Pizarro came upon the Incas (in'kázs), who put up a brave fight. It took him a year or more to defeat them and work his way inland to their capital. (See map, page 71.) 

Luckily for Pizarro, he captured the Inca who ruled the country and held him prisoner while the natives scurried around and gathered up great quantities of gold and silver to pay as reward for their ruler's release. This task required several months. Then, when the Incas

EARLY AMERICA 

thought they had paid the price Pizarro had demanded, he put their leader to death. For all his bravery as a soldier, the Spanish commander had feared the native ruler and wanted him safely out of the way. Thus by hard fighting and by trick-
