7. THE FIRST VOYAGE AROUND THE WORLD 

But the description of Spain's explorations and discoveries would be incomplete without the story of Ferdinand Magellan (fer'di nănd mă jěl'ăn). 

He came from a noble 

Portuguese family, and was a man of unusual courage. He sailed boldly into unknown seas and as boldly fought pirates and savages in different parts of the world. 

Magellan had a disagreement with the King of Portugal and left his native land in anger. When he offered his services to the King of Spain, he was welcomed as a fine sailor and a sturdy fighter. So it was that Magellan, with five small ships and two hundred and fifty men, set sail in 1519 to find a western passage to the Indies (see the map on page 81).

Hardship and danger accompanied the expedition from beginning to end. In spite of terrible storms, Magellan forced his way through the straits that bear his name

EUROPE 

• ASIA

SPAIN AFRICA OCEAN MAGELLAN'S VOYAGE, 1519-1522 

south of South America and started across the broad Pacific. The voyage was frightful and the men endured three years of suffering from hunger, thirst, and hardship that we can hardly imagine. The brave commander was killed in the Philippine (fil'i pēn) Islands, and only one vessel, the little Victoria (vic tō'ri á), and a mere handful of half-starved men lived to finish the journey without him.

Magellan's voyage was important in several ways. It proved for the first time that the earth is round and not flat. It showed that there is much more water than land on the earth's surface and revealed the immense width of the Pacific Ocean. It proved, too, that America is a separate continent and not a part of Asia, as men had thought before the voyage was made.

9. LIFE AND WORK IN  1. REASONS FOR THE GROWTH AND SPREAD OF THE SPANISH COLONIES 

Most of us are willing to go out of our way to earn money, for we want the things that money can buy. 

Some people suffer hardships and risk their lives for money. So it is with gold, for gold and money are much the same. Perhaps you have heard of the rush to California in 1849, when gold was discovered there. You may have seen motion-pictures showing the sufferings and dangers met by the gold-hunters from all parts of the United States who expected to become rich in a short time.

It was the gold and silver in the New World that beckoned to the Spanish explorers. The country was a wilderness, to be sure, and the natives were often unfriendly, but no one knew how much wealth lay hidden in America.

The first gold rush began early in the 16th Century. 

Montezuma had given Cortez treasure worth more than six million dollars in our money. Most of this was lost before Cortez set sail for Spain, but he had a million dollars' worth of gold and silver to show the Spaniards when he returned home in 1528. Pizarro, too, 84

F received nearly six million dollars in gold when he plundered Peru.

For several years the Spaniards did not work the silver mines. As early as 1545, however, Spain claimed the gold mines of Potosi (pōtō'zi) from which most of Peru's gold had come. After 1550 the American mines were sending to Spain as much as eleven million pesos (pāʼzōz) each year. A peso in those days was worth $3.00 in our money; the total in dollars is easy for you to find.

The King of Spain received as his royal share onefifth of the New World's treasure-an arrangement which made him a very wealthy ruler. He urged his people to conquer American lands and to find riches for themselves and for Spain. 

The desire for gold was not the only reason the Spaniards came to the New World. The Spanish Christians, who were Catholics, wished to teach the Indians and to make them members of the Catholic Church. Priests were sent from Spain to teach and to preach and to establish missions. With the priests came farmers and workmen who would be likely to stay in America and build homes. By their example the Christian way of living could be taught to the Indians. 

The King of Spain would permit only Spaniards to come to the New World, and for many years there were no other European colonists in North and South America. 

The Spanish settlers farmed, hunted, fished, and mined, but they did not find life easy. Clearing the wilderness and making roads was slow, hard work. But the King wanted to build up his American empire and his minis-

EARLY AMERICA 

ters led people to believe that life in the New World was easy and that the colonists were rich. 

In our time, as you know, the United States censustakers make a count of all our people every ten years. 

In the days of the Spanish settlements, however, no count was made. Some writers have one opinion of the number of people; some, another. We cannot be sure how great the Spanish population was, but it has been said that in 1574 there were 150,000 Spaniards living in America and that they had established more than two hundred cities and towns. Most of the towns were in Mexico, Central America, South America, and the

West Indies. 

One of the early towns was St. Augustine, Florida, the first permanent settlement in what is now the United States. In this colony were more than 2500 soldiers, sailors, priests, married men with their families, workmen, and mechanics. Each had his own work to do, for the new land was no place for a lazy man. 

2. THE SPANIARDS AND THE INDIANS 

The Indians Columbus found on the islands off the coast of America were friendly when they were well treated, a simple group of savages who lived in huts and rude shelters in small villages. But they were not civilized. They lived by hunting and fishing, and they used stone tools. When they fought to protect themselves and their possessions, they crouched in thickets and stealthily let their arrows fly.

Indians like these lived on the mainland, too. De Soto and his men met just such uncivilized natives, but they

Courtesy American Museum of Natural History AN AZTEC WOMAN TEACHING A GIRL TO WEAVE 

We cannot say how many hundred years ago this picture was drawn, but it is known to be very old. found them to be cruel. If the Spaniards themselves had not been cruel to the Indians, it is likely that the natives would have been friendly. There was no friendship between De Soto and the Indians he met as he explored the North American wilderness.

Hundreds of miles to the southwest of the Mississippi the Indians were civilized. These were the Aztecs whom 

Cortez met in Mexico. No rude huts sheltered them; instead, they had beautiful stone buildings. They worshiped in stately temples. In Mexico City they had a pyramid that was even larger than the Great Pyramid of Egypt. It may not have been built by the Aztecs, but by civilized people who had lived there in an earlier

EARLY AMERICA 

Courtesy of Carnegie Institution of Washington MAYAN TEMPLES IN CENTRAL AMERICA 

Colonel Charles A. Lindbergh flew over the jungle in 1929 and saw these ruins. On that flight pictures were taken of Mayan buildings so far from towns and villages that they had probably not been seen before by white men. time. But the Aztecs themselves were a nation of builders.

Still farther to the south in Mexico lived the Mayans (mi'äns), who were even more civilized than the Aztecs. 

They built their houses and temples and palaces of limestone, and their cities were reached by good roads and bridges. They were a busy people, and their work shows much thought and planning. They were artistic, too, for they beautified their buildings with color and skillful carving and statues.

There must have been several millions of Mayans to have accomplished so much. They worshiped different gods in their wonderful temples; they developed a sys-

tem of writing; they worked out their own calendar. 

In many ways they served as a model for the Aztec civilization.

Another nation of civilized Indians was found by Pizarro in Peru, known as the Incas. The Incas raised crops and used farm implements that were better than those used in Europe. White potatoes, corn, and cotton grew in abundance in the well-watered fields of the Incas. 

They had wide, smooth roads, sometimes paved with stone, with storehouses of food and arms at regular distances, for the Incas were great fighters and believed in being prepared for war.

The temples and buildings of the Incas, like those of the Aztecs, were made of stone. Their religion, also like that of the Aztecs, was very cruel, for they offered human sacrifices to their gods. 

In the cities and towns in America the uncivilized Indians were taught by missionary priests from Spain. 

They were treated kindly, but they were made to work and to go to church. If they resisted, they were punished.

Missions or little churches were established where the Indian children were taught. Some of the children later became priests and nuns and teachers among their own people.

Near the seacoast and in the cities the Indians were often used as slaves by the Spaniards, although this was forbidden by the King of Spain and by the Pope. 

According to the Spanish law, the Indians were to be made citizens and to enjoy many benefits. But it sometimes happened that the rulers sent by Spain to enforce

EARLY AMERICA 

the Spanish laws were unfair and cruel to the Indians whom they were supposed to protect. 

3. CLASSES OF PEOPLE IN NEW SPAIN 

As we have seen, the Spaniards in America were not all explorers and gold-seekers. We have mentioned the priests, the farmers, the workmen, and the soldiers, but there were other classes as well. 

Late in the 16th Century, that is, a little before the year 1600, Spain was probably the richest country in the world. Her colonial possessions in North and South

America were extensive, and each year millions of dollars' worth of gold and silver and other American products poured into her harbors.

The King appointed his favorites to the high positions in the colonies. They received large salaries for their services. They had the best houses and wore the most expensive clothes and rode the finest horses in America.

The King's officials were proud of having been born in Spain, and, like Spanish noblemen, kept apart from the less fortunate colonists.

The Spaniards who were born in the West Indies were known as Creoles (kre'ōlz). Although some of them were very wealthy, they were not permitted to mingle with the Spanish-born officers. Among the Creoles there were many lazy men who thought themselves too good to work. The slaves might suffer hardship, if necessary, but the Creoles wanted to be free to enjoy life. 

The Indian slaves were often captives in war; much of the heavy work fell upon them. You will remember that Indian slavery was forbidden by the King of Spain

ere!

and by the Pope, but Spain was three thousand miles from America, and evil Spaniards paid little attention to the law of a land so far away. In time, however, Negro slaves were brought from Africa to the West Indies by Dutch and English merchants, and Indian slavery gradually died out. 

Some Indians married Negroes and a new half-breed people, called Zambos (zăm'bōz), was added to the population in America. Some of the descendants of these may still be found in America. Besides these, S there were mixed Spaniards and Negroes; these were of called Mulattoes (mŭ lăt'tōz). ez

Many of the poor people of New Spain were among the mixed races. They lived in rude huts, for the most part, and were rather uncivilized. They were thought by the nobles to be as unimportant as slaves. 

4. MAKING A NEW LIVING IN NEW SPAIN 

As might be expected, the chief ways of making a living in Spanish America were mining, farming, and trade. 

At first, of course, the mines attracted the Spaniards, and the gold rush already mentioned claimed their attention. But in the meantime they had to eat, and food had to be raised in America. From Spain they had brought grapes, lemons, oranges, coffee, rice, and sugarcane. These products grew well in the rich soil of 

Mexico, South America, and the West Indies.

The Indians taught the Spaniards how to raise potatoes, cotton, and corn, and it was not long before the Spanish pupils were more successful farmers than their 

Indian teachers.

EARLY AMERICA 

The products of the mines and the farms in Spanish America were sold only to Spain, for the King would not permit trade with other European countries. He wanted to be sure that Spain's rivals-England, Holland, and France-should not grow rich from trade with the 

Spanish colonies.

The Spanish colonists did not like this arrangement, and after a while they dared to disobey the King's laws and to carry on a secret trade with merchants from other European countries. Such trading was called smuggling. The Spaniards knew it was against the law, but they thought the King's trade laws were not right. 

5. THE GOVERNMENT OF  

Just as the states and provinces of old Spain had been widely separated, and their people unfriendly and not united, so the Spanish colonies in America remained separate. Few good roads were built and travel was difficult. To be sure, the Spaniards had brought horses to the New World, but they were not used to longdistance riding.

Each colony had its own life and interests and cared nothing about what was going on in another Spanish colony. There was plenty of work to do at home, and little time was left for hard, slow travel. 

The King of Spain tried hard to keep in touch with what was going on in the Spanish colonies, for they furnished his chief source of wealth. For their government he selected eight Spaniards who had spent some time in America. These men formed what was called "The Council of the Indies." They, in turn, divided the

ar h: 10 ht colonies into groups, or vice-royalties, each group under a Spanish governor or viceroy.

The vice-royalty of New Spain, with Mexico City as its capital, included North America, the West Indies, and the Philippines (fil'i pēnz), a very large and scattered territory for one governor. The other important viceroyalty was that of Peru, with Lima (lē'mȧ) for its capital city. Under the Spanish rule the colonists had no real freedom. They had to accept laws made for them in Spain, and they had no right to choose their own rulers.

The King of Spain usually dealt with the colonies through the Council of the Indies, which, in turn, passed LES the royal commands to the viceroys who were in the New World to enforce the laws among the people. Sometimes the King dealt directly with the colonies, sending letters of instructions, or edicts (e'dicts), to the viceroys. 

Travel was so slow and the settlements were so far apart that it often took more than a year for the King's edicts to reach the viceroy. By that time the need for the new laws might have passed. htt We have already seen that the colonists disobeyed the King's trade laws which interfered with their commerce. 

As you go farther into the study of history, you will see that the Spanish colonists disobeyed other laws, too, and finally withdrew altogether from Spain and set up their own governments. 6.

EDUCATION IN SPANISH AMERICA 

We have already learned that the Spanish priests were the first teachers in the New World and that a number of

EARLY AMERICA 

their Indian pupils became priests and nuns and helped to carry on the work of educating the natives. 

Schools for Indians sprang up in villages, towns, and cities, and were well attended. One writer tells us that as early as 1522 there was a school in Mexico City with more than a thousand Indian boys as students. Here they were taught not only the three R's (reading, 'riting, and 'rithmetic), but also handwork, music, painting, and religion as well. More time was given to religion than to any other subject. The Spanish language, of course, was used. 

Perhaps you have heard from your older brothers and sisters in high school that Spanish is somewhat like Latin. 

From this has come the term "Latin America," which means, of course, the countries in America where the people speak a language which has come from the Latin.

Just as today many boys and girls want more than a grammar school education, and are eager to go to high school and college, so in those days some of the Indians were not content with what they learned in the early schools. It is not surprising, therefore, that higher schools, including a university, were finally established, for in 1551 the University of Mexico was founded. 

From this time on the natives were able to study from Mexican grammars and dictionaries printed in Mexico. 

There were a few printing presses in the New World as early as 1540 and the natives were beginning to feel the need of books of their own, for at first all their books had come from Spain.

Mexican scholars began to write for their own people,

95  and books on the history of man, on languages and religion, were printed on Mexican presses. Newspapers and magazines were also published, for educated people wanted to know what was going on in their own country. 

During the three hundred years that Spain ruled in America fourteen universities were established. Not only was religion taught, but languages, science, law, and medicine, as well.

10. PORTUGUESE NEIGHBORS IN SOUTH AMERICA 1. CABRAL DISCOVERS BRAZIJ 

You will recall that Prince Henry of Portugal had trained many mariners in his famous school for sailors and that he had sent out one ship after another to find a route to India by sail- ing around the coast of Africa. What re joicing there was in 

Portugal when Da 

Gama finally reached the goal! 

A Portuguese noble named Cabral (kä - bräl'), who set out to follow Da Gama's route in 1500, sailed too far to the west and accidentally reached the shores of Brazil in 

South America. Cabral realized that he had not reached India and that a new land na

CABRAL CLAIMS BRAZIL FOR PORTUGAL 

97

EARLY AMERICA 

had been found.

This land he claimed for Portugal. 

The Pope's Line of Division had already been drawn on the map (in 1494), separating the Spanish claims from those of Portugal. Since Brazil was found to be east of that line, there was no dispute about Portugal's claim to it (see page 71). Brazil is a large country, covering nearly half of the South American continent. The language used there is Portuguese. 

2. PORTUGUESE SETTLERS IN BRAZIL 

While Spain was sending colonists to other parts of North and South America, Portugal was not napping. 

Gradually the Portuguese established a number of colonies in Brazil, and the mother country governed them wisely. To be sure, several of the Portuguese governors were dishonest and cruel, but that was the fault of the governors themselves, not the fault of the laws they were to enforce.

The first settlers were farmers who raised coffee and sugar, most of which they sold to the Europeans. In time there were many large plantations in Brazil, and their owners became very wealthy men. 

Some years later mines were found and, of course, there were farmers who gave up tilling the soil to seek a greater fortune in precious metals.

Unlike their Spanish neighbors to the north and west, the Portuguese did not establish fine schools and universities. Those who wanted an education had to go back to Europe to attend school and college. They brought their books from Portugal and did not try to write or print their own.

PART THREE THE FRENCH EXPLORE THE NEW WORLD

THE FRENCH EXPLORE THE NEW WORLD 

The enormous riches that came to Spain as a result of the Spanish discoveries in the New World aroused the King of France. Europe was stirred with wars, and the French King needed money to carry on the fight. He thought it might be a good thing to send someone to search for a short route to China and the Indies, and at the same time to see to it that France got some share of the New World's treasures. 

Verrazano, an Italian sailing for France, was the first to be sent across the Atlantic. He was followed by Cartier, whose ship nosed into the St. Lawrence River, while eager French eyes kept watch for a northwest passage to the East. 

Champlain was the next Frenchman to make an attempt. 

It was he who discovered Lake Champlain, founded the city of Quebec, and explored the Great Lakes. And he it was who enraged the Iroquois Indians and made them enemies of 

France forever.

Joliet and Father Marquette, a Jesuit priest, worked their way inland and explored the upper part of the Mississippi River, but it remained for La Salle to reach the mouth of the "Father of Waters" and claim the Louisiana country for France.

Along the rivers and lakes French forts and trading posts occupied important positions and gave the French a very strong hold on the interior of North America. The Jesuit missionaries, too, carried the teachings of Christianity to the Indians in the wilderness and won their friendship. Woodrangers lived among the natives and showed them how to trade their furs for goods from France. 

Thus, from the Gulf of St. Lawrence west to the Great 

Lakes and south to the mouth of the Mississippi River, Frenchmen explored the interior and helped gradually to arouse a slumbering continent.

CHAMPLAIN EXPLORING LAKE SUPERIOR

11. LIFE IN OLD FRANCE 1. THE FEUDAL SYSTEM 

While Portugal, Spain, and England were making discoveries in America, France saw no reason why she should not have her share of the lands and riches of the 

New World. 

France had become the leading nation of Europe. 

For several hundred years during the Middle Ages, France had been divided among powerful nobles. Land had been seized in war by the nobles, or had been granted to them by the king who promised to protect them in the enjoyment of their possessions. For their part, the nobles vowed to support the king, to pay taxes to him, and to furnish soldiers to fight for him when he called upon them to do so. In this way the nobles became the king's vassals (văs'álz).

The nobles also had vassals. They, in turn, granted land to other nobles usually not so high in rank, who took the same vows of loyalty to their overlords. The lesser nobles again granted land to their vassals, and so on, until we come to the smallest division of land called the manor (măn'ôr).

The peasants (pěz'ántz), or serfs (serfs), as they were called, were the real workers on the manor. They were almost like slaves. They were not allowed to move 105

EARLY AMERICA 

about from one place to another as they chose. Serfs were a part of the manor; and, if the estate changed hands, the serfs went along with the land. They were expected to take care of the fields, raise the food, and do all of the work.

The entire plan of kings, higher nobles, lower nobles, vassals, and serfs was called the feudal system. The lord gave his vassal the right to hold a large piece of land, or the right to hold a high office, or the right to collect a toll, or to have a mill. In return for such favors, the vassal knelt before his lord, and with his hands between his lord's hands promised to serve the lord faithfully in whatever he might be called upon to do. 

2. THE KING'S TROUBLES WITH THE NOBLES 

It was not long before the nobles became more powerful than the king. The small land-holder, or the weak freeman without any land at all, was always in danger. 

There were quarrels among the nobles, and enemies from the outside frequently made sudden attacks. The great noble or the great land-holder formed a little state of his own, with his own court and with a private army. 

His weaker neighbors came to him for protection. They wanted security and safety from robbery and attack; therefore, they were ready to become the vassals of a powerful protector.

With thousands of retainers and vassals, the great dukes, barons, and counts became very powerful. Sometimes they made war against one another or even against the king. Each claimed to be a ruler, with the right to carry on private wars of his own. Sometimes the nobles

LIFE IN OLD FRANCE

A FEUDAL CASTLE 

The high, thick walls make it look like a fort. Do you see the drawbridge just inside the entrance and the armed guards all ready for trouble? refused to help the king in his wars. It took a long time for the king to become the real ruler of the whole country.

But the king's power increased little by little. The strong feudal nobles gradually lost their lands and their vassals, and at last the king was recognized as the only ruler. The feudal system gradually disappeared. Then the feudal lords, and everyone else in the country, had to obey the king and his laws. 

3. FRANCE NEEDED MONEY TO CARRY ON WARS 

By the time of the age of discovery, the King of France was rich and powerful. He was ready to reach out for part of the New World.

His feudal wars were

EARLY AMERICA 

ended, but he had conflicts with other countries. He wanted to find wealth in America which might help him to.carry on his wars in Europe. 

The King of France feared that he might be conquered by the Spanish King who at this time controlled both Austria and Holland as well as Spain. Spain already had too much power, he thought. He did not want Spain. to gain still more power by gathering in all the lands and wealth of the New World.

Besides, the King of France did not like the "Pope's Line," by which the newly-found lands and seas were divided between Spain and Portugal. He did not believe that "Father Adam had made a will" by which the western world was to be given to Spain and Portugal. 

If there was such a will, he wanted to see it. Nobody could show it to him, of course. Since Francis I was also a Catholic King, he saw no reason why France, a Catholic country, should be left out. France must have her share of lands in America. He decided to send someone on a voyage of discovery.

12. THE DISCOVERIES OF  1. VERRAZANO IS SENT TO CLAIM NEW LAND FOR FRANCE 

By this time France was a wide-awake and powerful nation. You may be sure that she did not intend to let

Spain get all the land in the New World. 

England seemed likely to be a rival of France, too. In 

1497 and 1498 John Cabot had made discoveries along the coast of North America by which England had claimed the whole continent. Before long news might come telling of English settlements in America, and France could not afford to waste any time. 

Shortly after Cabot's last voyage French fishermen began to come over to the fishing banks off the coast of Canada. The supply of fish seemed never to give out. 

Year after year hundreds of fishing boats came from France and returned with fine loads of fish. The fishermen also carried back stories of the many fur-bearing animals in the region and the wealth to be made from trading in furs.

Such stories stirred the people of France. They became restless, and eager to have a claim on the new land. 

The French King first employed an Italian navigator for the American voyage. His name was Verrazano 110

(věr åt sä'nó). He was the first man to sail to America under the flag of France. He explored the Atlantic coast from North Carolina to Nova Scotia and left a rough map of the shore line. He visited what are now the harbors of New York and Newport, Rhode Island, and because of his exploration France laid claim to the continent of North America. Verrazano returned to 

France and soon disappeared from view. 

2. CARTIER'S FIRST VOYAGE TO AMERICA 

It was ten years before another exploring party was sent out from France. This was led by a French explorer, Jacques Cartier (zhäk kär tyā'), the discoverer of the St. Lawrence River.

What was it that drew the men of France to our northeastern shores? The region was bleak and barren, with long cold winters. The attraction was fish and furs, the hope of finding silks and spices, gold and precious stones. 

These brought the daring sailors of France to the Canadian waters. And there was always the hope of finding a sea passage to India. Cartier had visited some of the fishing islands across the Atlantic where the French fishermen had spread their fish to dry, and he was eager to see if he could not find a strait leading through them to 

India and China.

Cartier had lived in northwestern France, on the shore of the sea, at St. Malo (mä'ló). He had followed the sea from his boyhood, and his King, Francis I, had heard of him as a brave sailor and determined to send him on a voyage of discovery. 

In April, 1534, with two ships and sixty-one men Car-

EARLY AMERICA 

ENOS B. ComTork CANADIAN INDIANS WATCH THE APPROACH OF CARTIER ON THE ST. LAWRENCE 

tier sailed from St. Malo to look for a long-sought passage to the Far East. He reached Newfoundland (nu'fund land) in May, and entered the Strait of Belle

Isle, between Newfoundland and Labrador (lăb'rá dôr). 

He found the coast farther west barren and rocky, with shores "as steep as any wall."

Cartier saw many new islands. On one of them he set up a cross, to claim the region for France. Wild animals were plentiful, especially bears. There were great shoals of fish, mackerel and cod. The polar bears fed upon the fish. The sailors saw a big white bear swimming ashore to eat the fish which the sea gulls had caught and laid aside for their food. The bear would have eaten the birds, too, if he could have caught them.

Cartier left these icy shores and turned his ships southward to find a warmer climate. He sailed along the west shore of Newfoundland, but a storm drove his

ships out into the gulf. Again finding his course, he followed the coast of New Brunswick. He came to a bay, which he called Chaleur (shälûr'), the name by which it is still known. (Find the Bay of Chaleur on the map on p. 114.) He was much disappointed to find that this was not a strait which might lead him to the longpromised passage to China. Full summer had come; the weather was warm, and the shores of the bay were delightful. There were wild flowers, and wheat was growing wild in the fields. 

Cartier found that his supplies were running low. 

His ships were small; he had many men in his crews; and it was almost impossible for him to carry enough food for a year. If he stayed in America during the long hard winter, he knew he would have to ask food and shelter from the Indians. Besides, the autumn storms were gathering. For such reasons Cartier decided to bring his explorations to a close for the season. He returned to France, carrying with him two kidnaped Indians. 

3. HOW CARTIER FOUND THE ST. LAWRENCE RIVER 

The next year, in May, 1535, Cartier sailed again for America. By August he was in Canadian waters. He anchored in a small bay to which he gave the name St. Lawrence. This name soon spread to the Gulf of St. 

Lawrence, and then to the river. As he sailed west, the waterway of the gulf became narrower. The shores came nearer on either side. The country was beautiful in its autumn coloring of red and orange. He found that his ships were in fresh water; the salt water of the

EARLY FRENCH SETTLEMENTS 

CE sea had been left behind. As he approached the high cliffs of Quebec, he knew that he was in a river. It was the great River of St. Lawrence.

Cartier sailed up the newly-found river. In October he reached an Indian village. A group of Indians led him to the top of a steep hill, which he called Mount Royal. Here the present city of Montreal (mon tri awl') now stands. Further progress up the river was checked by rapids. Cartier called these La Chine (lä shēn') 

Rapids, the name by which they are known to this day. 

La Chine is French for China, but China was still ten thousand miles away. It seems strange that the explorer thought he was so near to that faraway country. 

At first the Indians were not friendly. They did not want the white men to go on up the river. The Indian chief had some of his warriors dress up in queer gar-

ne! Its ments and give a wild war dance. The chief told Cartier that these dancers were "evil spirits" from up the river who did not like strangers and would devour all who came to their country. That may have been enough to scare Indians, but Cartier only laughed and went on his way.

From the top of Mount Royal Cartier beheld the waters of the St. Lawrence and the Ottawa Rivers stretching far away to the west. Then his dream of a 

Northwest passage faded away. He saw forest-clad mountains and the great river reaching as far as he could see.

Cartier now sailed down the river. Knowing the Indians better than on his first expedition, he went into winter quarters close by an Indian village. The village was near the present city of Quebec (kwě běk'). He sought to learn what he could of the country and was told of its "gold and precious stones," but he found none of these treasures. 

4. WINTER IN NEW FRANCE 

Cartier and his sailors suffered from a long, hard winter. They had no warm clothes, no warm houses, and hardly enough to eat. The weather was extremely cold - much colder than they had ever known in their native land. They longed for "La Belle France" — their beautiful France. During the winter twenty-five of the men died from scurvy (skûr'vi), a disease that comes from eating too much salt meat. All of them might have died but for the kindness of the Indians, who furnished the white men with food and medicines.

EARLY AMERICA 

This kindness was poorly repaid by Cartier, who seized the friendly chief and eleven men of the village and carried them off to France. This was in the spring, after the ice had broken up in the river. Then Cartier and the few men who were left sailed back home. Before they went, Cartier gave the name of New France to the new region which he had discovered. 

Cartier carried no gold nor precious stones to his King, and he had to report that he had found no passage to India. This greatly disappointed the King. 

5. CARTIER'S VOYAGES DID NOT RESULT IN PLANTING ANY COLONIES 

Two more voyages were made by Cartier to these northern regions. He passed another winter of hardship and suffering in the wilderness, but made no attempt to plant a colony. Although no settlement came from Cartier's adventures, his name will live in American history as one of the greatest explorers and discoverers of his time. He died at St. Malo, his old home town in France, in 1557. 

6. EARLY FRENCH SETTLEMENTS IN THE SOUTH 

Nearly thirty years after Cartier's discovery of the St. Lawrence, the French attempted to plant a colony in another part of America. They went as far south as 

Florida and Carolina, where the climate was warm. 

Their plan was not to fish or engage in fur-trading or hunt for gold, but to establish a colony, or settlement of homes of French men and women living in America in 

French ways.

Car TE Their leader was Admiral Coligny (kō len'yē), the 

Protector of the Huguenots (hu'gě nots), as French

Protestants were called. Religious wars were going on in France and the Huguenots were being punished for their beliefs. Coligny wished to find a safe place in 

America for his fellow Huguenots.

Jean Ribaut (zhän rē bō') was chosen to head the expedition. The colonists came to Florida, sailed up the St. 

John's River, and stopped at a place not far from where 

St. Augustine now stands.

Ribaut with a few picked men went farther north to explore.

He came to a sound which he called Port Royal, within the present State of South Carolina. He named the country 

Carolina in honor of Charles, who was then King of France. 

ADMIRAL COLIGNY 

While Ribaut was away from the Florida settlement, the supplies there began to give out. In fear of starvation, the men quarreled, killed their leaders, built a rude ship, and sailed away to France. Ribaut could find no trace of the colony when he returned, and the first attempt of the French to establish homes in the south came to nothing. 

Two years later the French tried again in the southern region. They sent out colonists who built Ft. Caroline, about five miles up the St. John's River. The natives were friendly and gave the French some food and silver.

EARLY AMERICA 

The silver made the settlers restless. Instead of patiently cultivating the soil, they began to wander about in search of precious metals. But their efforts were all in vain. They quarreled among themselves and some of them, moved by hunger and fear, wandered away to Spanish settlements on the coast of Cuba. 

When the Spanish heard of the French settlement in FO 

PEDRO MENENDEZ 

Florida, they made up their minds to destroy it, for Florida was claimed by 

Spain. The Spanish government ordered Pedro Menendez (pā'dro měn ĕn'dĕth), to destroy the French colony. 

He brought over a force of nineteen vessels and fifteen hundred men, attacked the 

French, and utterly wiped out their settlement. The men of the colony were killed, and the women and children were carried away as captives. Menendez declared that he had killed the 

French not as Frenchmen but as people who had chosen their own belief instead of the belief of the Church. In this way people were made to suffer because of their religion. Thus the second attempt of the French to settle in the South failed. They did not try there again.

13. THE FATHER OF NEW FRANCE 1. How CHAMPLAIN EXPLORED THE GREAT LAKES IN TRYING TO ESTABLISH A FRENCH COLONY 

For the French explorations and settlements that were successful we must turn again to the north, to the regions of New France. Here we come to the adventures of another great Frenchman, the real Founder of New 

France, Samuel de Champlain (shăm plān').

Champlain was born in France, at a small port on the 

Bay of Biscay, in 1567, just ten years after Cartier died.

Like Cartier, he was from a seafaring family. His father was a sea captain. As a boy young Champlain had experience on the sea and had become skilled in mapmaking and navigation. As a youth he fought in the 

King's armies. He sailed for the first time into the St. 

Lawrence in 1603, nearly seventy years after Cartier discovered that river. During this long period furtraders and fishermen were the only Frenchmen who had visited the country which Cartier had called New France. 

Champlain had seen the shores of America before this expedition, while he was commanding a Spanish ship carrying Spanish soldiers to the Spanish West Indies. 

He was then only twenty-two years of age. He was gone two years on this voyage. He visited the main ports of the West Indies, landed at Vera Cruz (vě rá krōoz'), 120

FATHER OF NEW FRANCE 

121 

RES 

and pushed inland from there to the City of Mexico. He visited Panama, and there the idea came into his head that a canal might be built across the isthmus to connect the two oceans. It may be that he was the first man in all the world to propose this great plan. 

Here, across the Isthmus of Panama, was the real western passage to India and China which had so long been sought, the only one through the American continent that man has ever discovered. No one, of course, hat really "discovered" this passage. But three hundred years after Champlain, America decided to act on his reidea and to build a passage at Panama. The Panama 

Canal was built by the United States after the French had tried but failed to do so, many of their workmen re dying of fever and disease. But that is another story tier and we must return to Champlain. ily. ha While Champlain was yet a very young man, he had seen much of the world and had great ideas of advancing Hit the power and glory of France. When he sailed to 

Canada in 1603 he hoped to find the long-sought Northof west passage, and he also wished to convert the Indians to Christianity and to bring them into the Catholic Church. He explored the coast of Nova Scotia (nō’vá skō'shá) and sought to make a settlement at Port Royal, now known as Annapolis (ăn nă'polis). But the colony did not last. (See map, page 114.)

2. THE COLONY AT QUEBEC 

One failure, however, could never discourage a man like Champlain. In 1608 he sailed into the mouth of the 

St. Lawrence, and upon the shore, by a high rock tower-

EARLY AMERICA 

ing above the river, he began to build the first permanent, or lasting, French settlement in America. He named the new town Quebec, which is now one of the most picturesque (pik tur ěsk') and famous cities in America. In those early days the King of France declared Quebec to be the capital city of Canada and named 

Champlain as Canada's Governor.

In the first winter Champlain's settlers suffered severely from cold, hunger, and sickness. The same terrible scurvy, from which Cartier's men had suffered, did its deadly work. Twenty out of the twenty-eight men died before spring; and of the eight who were left four were sick.

In the spring Champlain had the settlers plant seeds and raise vegetables and corn. They could catch freshwater fish in the river. When they had plenty of fresh food, their sickness disappeared. The French finally learned from the English that to settle down and work the soil and build homes was the only way to establish colonies which would last. The French liked to go hunting and exploring, to wander in the forests and mingle with the Indians.

Champlain won the friendship of the Indians in Canada, the Hurons ( hū'ronz) and the Algonquins (ǎlgon'kinz). He joined them when they made war on the 

Iroquois (ir'ō kwoi) Indians, who lived farther to the south. The Iroquois had long been enemies of the 

Hurons and Algonquins; they had defeated these northern Indians in war, had driven them out, and had taken their lands. The Hurons and the Algonquins were eager for revenge. There were several tribes of the Iroquois

FATHER OF NEW FRANCE 

123 דור Er

CHAMPLAIN'S BATTLE WITH THE IROQUOIS 

who were banded together in a strong union. They were called the "Five Nations." In war against their enemies they acted together, and as a result became the strongest body of Indians in America.

Champlain led a war party of French and a few friendly Indians southward into the country of the Iroquois. In what is now New York State he discovered the beautiful lake that bears his name. He attacked the 

Iroquois, who, leaving their forts and villages, advanced across the open plains to meet the enemy. Champlain, clad in armor, with a plume in his hat, stepped out in front of his men and fired his gun. Two Indians fell dead and a third was wounded. One of his men fired, and another Iroquois fell dead. 

Then their warriors. 1 62

EARLY AMERICA 

turned and fled in confusion. They had never seen firearms before, and they could not stand up against them and fight.

In this way Champlain and the French made enemies of the Iroquois. If they had made friends of them, they might have won the whole inland region of America. 

Years later the Iroquois sided with the Dutch, and later with the English, and the combination was too strong for the French. It prevented their control of New York and gave the English colonies a better opportunity to become strong.

Nearly every year Champlain went to France and brought back with him settlers and supplies. In an inland expedition far to the west and north he discovered Lakes Ontario (on ta'ri ō) and Huron. He was surprised to see the waters of these Great Lakes reaching far away out of sight. He traveled chiefly by the rivers in light canoes with only a few men. At times the men had to carry canoes around swift rapids. At times their exploration led them away from the rivers. Then they tramped through dense forests, with their guns and provisions on their backs, which they found a very hard way to travel. Occasionally their supplies gave out and the men had to live on the fish they could catch, the game they could trap or shoot, and on wild fruit and berries.

On the shores of rivers and lakes Champlain set up crosses, as signs that the lands were claimed for the 

King of France.

Returning to the east, Champlain and his Indian allies again attacked the Iroquois, but this time he was unsuccessful. The Iroquois were well protected in their

FATHER OF NEW FRANCE 

125 stockade, or fort, and Champlain's men could not drive them out. Champlain had his men build a platform as high as the Indian fort, and from that high perch his gunners drove the Iroquois from their walls. But Champlain's Indians were impatient. They would not obey his orders. They rushed to the attack too soon and were driven back. Champlain was wounded in the knee and had to be carried from the field. His Indian followers began to feel that his power was gone. 

Because of his wound Champlain had to spend the winter among the northern Indians. He learned their language and shared their food and shelter and ways of living. They ate corn, deer meat, and fish.

Champlain had now come to the end of his exploring and fighting expeditions. He spent the last years of his life in trying to build up the colony at Quebec. But it was a slow-growing town, for it was hard to get French families to leave their homes and come out to settle in the wilds of America. Twenty years after its settlement Quebec had hardly more than a hundred French settlers. The English colonies in other parts of North 

America grew much faster.

14. THE FRENCH BUILD UP NEW FRANCE 1. THE JESUITS 

As we have seen, Champlain brought missionaries to America to make Christians of the Indians and to bring them into the Catholic Church. There were many orders, or brotherhoods, of these Catholic missionaries. 

The most numerous and the best-known among them : were the Jesuits (jěz'ū ĭts) or members of the "Society of Jesus." Each year the Jesuit missionaries had to send their Superiors in France a full account of all their dealings or relations with the Indians. There are now many printed volumes of these accounts, known as the "Jesuit Relations." In these books we find a complete story of how the Jesuit missionaries lived and worked with the American Indians.

The Jesuits extended their missions and schools to the northern lakes and rivers, braving every hardship and peril of the wilderness. They were not afraid of the dangers about them. They were obedient and were ready to go wherever they were sent. Self-sacrificing, gentle, kind, they were ready to die, if need be, in the service of the Catholic Church. And many of them did die in this way. Within fifty years thirty-two of the 

Catholic missionaries were scalped, tortured, and killed by fierce and savage Indians in North America. 127

EARLY AMERICA 

The priests were clad in coarse gowns and hoods, with wooden sandals on their feet. The sandals covered only the soles of their feet, with straps to hold them on, and the tops of the feet were bare and exposed to the cold. 

The priests shared the lives of the Indians, eating their food, suffering their hardships. They shared all but the cruel fighting of the Indians, and tried to keep them from making war. 

2. FRONTENAC'S PLAN FOR FORTS IN THE WEST 

In 1672 a new Governor came to New France, Count Frontenac (fron'tě năk) by name. He had been a warrior, fighting for the "Grand Monarch," Louis XIV of

France. During the ten years that he was the ruler of 

Canada, he was known as the "Iron Governor," because of his firmness and strength. 

Frontenac was a man of great boldness and activity.

It was his firm purpose to extend the power of France. 

He was not only ready to encourage the Jesuit missionaries to teach and civilize the Indians and to make them friendly to France, but he desired to extend the fur trade far back into the wilderness and to draw wealth from the country.

It was also his plan to strengthen the power of France in the vast regions of the West. He planned to build forts at certain points on the Great Lakes and rivers. With vessels to navigate these rivers and lakes, he would have control of their shores and of the trade of the whole country. In his imagination Frontenac could see a great French empire far inland from the Atlantic coast.

EST 3. How JOLIET AND MARQUETTE EXPLORED THE INTERIOR 

Frontenac had heard from the Indians of the "Great River" to the west far from the sea, in what is called the interior. He determined to send an exploring party to search for it. He learned of a French fur trader who had lived three years among the Indians and had explored as far to the northwest as Lake Superior (sū pēr'i ôr). This explorer's name was Joliet (zho'lya'). Frontenac asked Joliet to go on a long hunt for the "Great River." If he found that it flowed into the 

Pacific Ocean, then the way to India and China would be open.

It was usual for a missionary to go along on such a voyage. Frontenac asked the Jesuit missionaries to name one of their number to go with Joliet. They named Marquette (mär kět'), who was living at a mission station in northern Michigan (mish'i găn). Marquette was a brave priest who was ever ready to do what he could to serve his Church and to aid the natives. He had spent six winters among the Indians and during this time had learned six different Indian languages, or dialects (di'á lĕcts). 

Joliet and Marquette met at Mackinac (măkʼĩ năk), an island lying in the strait between Lake Huron and Lake Michigan. (Find this on the map, page 130.) It is a beautiful island, now famous as a summer resort, with fine hotels. In those early days it was so attractive that it was later called "the peerless gem of the unsalted seas. 99

Joliet came along from Quebec, a distance of a thousand miles, to meet Marquette on this beautiful

Joliet and Marquette spent the winter in getting ready for their voyage. They laid in a supply of Indian corn and smoked meat. The Indians built them a canoe. 

They set out in May, 1673, taking two birch canoes and four men to act as helpers.

They went through the Strait of Mackinac and along the west shore of Lake Michigan. They came to the "Wild 

Rice" Indians, so called because they lived largely on the

aid 

FATHER MARQUETTE AND HIS GUIDES CROSSING A WATERFALL 

It is not easy to lift a canoe over shallow water, waterfalls, and portages. wild rice growing there. Here they ate the wild ducks that came to feed on the rice. The Indians tried to persuade them to go no farther. They thought they might frighten the strangers with stories of fierce wild Indians and of great man-eating monsters in the deep waters. 

Marquette and Joliet paid no attention to these frightful tales and went on their way.

EARLY AMERICA 

Soon they came to Green Bay, and then paddled up the beautiful Fox River. They had to drag their canoes around the rapids, in places through tangles of wild rice. 

They went in their canoes as far as they could up the Fox. Indian guides then showed them the way to the 

Wisconsin River. They had to carry their canoes a mile and a half across the strip of land that separated the two rivers. Such a strip separating two rivers flowing in opposite directions is called a portage. Here the Fox flowed northeast into the Great Lakes and the St. 

Lawrence, while the Wisconsin flowed southwest into the Mississippi.

The voyagers now floated down the Wisconsin. They turned their backs on Canada and its waters, going toward the "Great River." They did not know, of course, whether it would lead them to the Gulf of Mexico or to the Gulf of California. They were in the midst of a vast wilderness where no white man had ever been.

They passed the Wisconsin dells, went by many islands covered with trees, past rocks and forests and prairies, by marshes and sandbars. "Finally," said Marquette, "with a joy that I cannot express," the canoes glided into the broad Mississippi. It was in this way that in 

1673 Marquette and Joliet discovered the upper Mississippi. The name is an Indian one meaning the "Father of Waters." It is the greatest river on the continent of 

North America.

The explorers now journeyed south for two weeks without seeing a human being. Large herds of buffalo appeared upon the banks, a strange sight to the travelers. Fish were plentiful in the river, and a big cat-fish

M almost upset Marquette's canoe. Finally they found men's footprints in the mud. A path led them to an 

Indian village where they found themselves among the "Illinois" (illi noi') Indians, who came swarming out of their huts. These Indians had never seen white men. 

We may well believe that they were surprised to see men so different from themselves in color and dress, Marquette with his cowl, or hood, and in his long, black, priestly robe, and Joliet and the other "pale faces" in buckskin hunting suits and fur caps, with their guns and powder-horns. 99 The Illinois Indians were friendly. They offered the pipe of peace and entertained their visitors, feeding them on buffalo meat, fish, and corn. They also offered some dog meat, to which the Frenchmen politely said, "No, thank you. 

The explorers bade farewell to these friendly Indians and went on their way down the Mississippi. They passed the mouth of the Missouri, a river that had come for two thousand miles from the great Northwest. It carried tons of yellow mud into the clear waters of the Mississippi and swept logs and branches of trees along in its swift current. The canoes were almost upset.

The travelers continued past the present site of St. 

Louis, Missouri (miz ūr'i), past the mouth of the Ohio, and as far south as the mouth of the Arkansas (ärʼkănsâw). (Can you find the mouths of these rivers on the map on page 130 ?) Here the Indians were unfriendly.

They threatened an attack from the shore. Several of the younger men waded into the river and threw their war clubs at one of the canoes. The old chiefs called off

EARLY AMERICA 

the young warriors and the French travelers were allowed to go in peace.

They feared to go farther down the river. Their old enemies, the Spaniards, were there, and they dreaded meeting more unfriendly Indians. They were completely worn out by their labors, and they knew that the journey home would be hard. So they turned back. 

The plan of reaching the mouth of the Mississippi would have to be left to a later time. Having gone so far, they could report that the Mississippi flowed into the Gulf of Mexico. 

4. THE JOURNEY BACK 

Marquette wished to return to his mission post in Michigan. Joliet had to go to his starting point in far-off Quebec, to tell Governor Frontenac about the expedition.

The return trip was a difficult job. The men had to paddle against the current. The work was hard, and the men were almost worn out. Their food was very poor and they had to face all kinds of bad weather. When they came to the mouth of the Illinois River they turned up that stream, having been told by the Indians of a short way to Lake Michigan. They went up the Illinois to the portage over which they carried their canoes, to the Des Plaines (des plānz') River, which carried them into the Chicago (shi kä'gó) River, which took them into Lake Michigan. This became one of the famous portages.

When they reached Green Bay, the explorers separated. Joliet went on to Quebec to give a report of the

Te voyage. He went by the Georgian (jôr'jĩ ăn) Bay and down the Ottawa (ŏt'tá wá) River into the St. Lawrence. 

When he came to the La Chine Rapids just above Montreal, instead of taking the long way around, he tried to 

The canoe was over- "shoot the rapids" in his canoe. turned and Joliet lost his maps and papers and almost lost his life. Two of his men and an Indian boy were drowned. Joliet had to make his report from memory. 2.3 

He had safely passed forty-two rapids on his long voyage, and now that he was almost home he met with this accident. He said that he had only his life left, but he was ready to offer that again in the service of 

Count Frontenac. 

Marquette had fallen ill on the return voyage, and did not recover his health for a year. When he was strong enough, he again went to the Illinois Indians, as he had promised to do, to teach them to be Christians. He established a mission station near where Peoria (pē ō’ri á) now stands. He fell ill again on the return trip and was not able to get back to his northern mission. 

He died in northern Michigan, on the shore of the Great Lake, on May 19, 1677. He was still a young man, under forty years of age, but he was worn out by his hard work and his long journeys. His two companions dug a grave by the little hut at Old Mission and buried him. Later his body was removed to the site of his beloved mission in northern Michigan.

Father Marquette lived and died a noble priest and a great explorer. His reports and papers were kept and sent to France. The story of his life has made him a famous man in American history, but while he lived he

EARLY AMERICA 

cared for neither fame, power, nor riches. 

Most of all he wanted to make Christians of the Indians and to help the poor.

15. LA SALLE, GREATEST OF THE FRENCH PATHFINDERS 1. LA SALLE DISCOVERS THE OHIO 

This

While the discoveries of Marquette and Joliet were going on, another explorer appeared upon the scene. was La Salle (lä săl′), one of the bravest and most daring men in all history. 

La Salle thought as Frontenac did — that there should be a great empire for France in America. He may have been the first to think of the plan. At any rate the two men worked heartily together. Frontenac became La 

Salle's friend and was ready to support him in his ex plorations. 

To carry out the plan of empire the French would have to keep the Dutch and English traders from the region of the Great Lakes and the Mississippi. They wished also to check the power of the Iroquois, who had been enemies of the French since the time of Champlain. 

When the English and the Dutch supplied the Iroquois with guns, they were able to give the French a great deal of trouble, and that was just what they did. 

La Salle was born in France in 1643. His family were 

Catholics, and Robert was placed in a Jesuit school to be educated for the priesthood. He taught for a while, but the young man did not wish to live a quiet life and 137

EARLY AMERICA 

take orders from others. He longed for action. He read of the new lands across the Atlantic and had a great desire to explore them. He dreamed of doing such things as Champlain had done for the glory of France. 

He left the Jesuit school and sailed for America when he was twenty-three years of age.

La Salle received a grant of land eight miles from Montreal up the St. Lawrence River. Here he lived for a time, built a stockade, and established a trading post. 

Here he talked with Indians who told him of a beautiful river far away. They called it the "Ohio" (ō hīō), an 

Indian word which means "Beautiful river." The Indians said that rivers not far away ran into the Ohio, and that the Ohio itself, after eight or nine months' journey, flowed into the sea.

This story fired the desire of La Salle to find out for himself. He started on an expedition of discovery up the St. Lawrence, and through Lake Ontario to a Seneca (sen'ě ká) Indian village on the Genesee (jĕn'ě sé) River. He crossed the mouth of the Niagara (nī ǎg'á rá) River; perhaps he was even able to hear the roar of the falls in the distance. He went southward and launched his boat on one of the rivers that flow into the Ohio. It is not certain which stream he traveled, but it may have been the Allegheny (ǎl'lě ga'ni), the Scioto (sīō'tō), or the Big Miami (mī ăm'i). (Find these rivers on the map on page 130.) He discovered the 

Ohio, made it known to the world, and claimed it and its shores for France.

He described the new country, which he said was quite different from Canada. In Canada, he said, the forests

LA SALLE 

139 145 FIL 

TAX The ell 20 er were dense, the soil was rocky and barren, the climate was harsh, and snow covered the ground for half the year. It would be very hard to build up colonies there. 

In the Ohio country there was more summer. Here the 

T. land was beautiful and fertile, full of meadows, brooks, and rivers, abounding in fish, game, and venison. Food was plentiful and easy to obtain. The soil would produce everything raised in France. Flocks and herds could be left out in pasture all winter. La Salle gave a glowing account of the Ohio country, perhaps too glowing.

By 1673 La Salle had returned to Montreal. From what he heard of the explorations of Marquette and Joliet, he was convinced that the Mississippi flowed into the Gulf of Mexico. He thought that he would explore that great river to its mouth and thus extend the power of France to that far-away country. 

2. TRYING TO COME TO AN UNDERSTANDING WITH THE INDIANS 

Upon his return to Montreal, La Salle persuaded Frontenac to attend a council with a large number of Indian chiefs. This council was held on the shore of Lake 

Ontario where Kingston, Canada, now stands, and where the lake narrows into the St. Lawrence River. La Salle saw that there was no better place for a fort. 

The Governor came in royal splendor. He landed from his boats in bright array, with drums beating and banners waving. There were one hundred and sixty Indian chiefs at the council. The simple redmen of the forest had great respect for the glittering uniforms of

EARLY AMERICA 

the Governor and his bodyguard. Governor Frontenac made generous gifts to the Indians-tobacco, guns, fruits, clothing, and wampum (wom'pum). He addressed them as "Children" - the Mohawks (mō'hawks), Oneidas (ō ni'dáz), Onondagas (on on dâw'gáz), Cayugas (kā yū gázs), and Senecas. These were the fierce "Five 

Nations" of the Iroquois. 

Frontenac sought to win their friendship. He told them that the French came among them not for war but for peace. At the same time his officers were marking out the lines of a fort. They wished to fortify the place and be able to hold it against Indian attacks, if the Iroquois refused to be friends. The fort built in that spot is known as Fort Frontenac.

The Governor then urged the Indians to listen to the teaching of the French missionaries and to accept the Christian religion. He told them of his powerful King across the sea and warned them of the punishment the King could inflict upon them if they were disobedient. 

He urged them to listen to the advice of La Salle. In this way Frontenac was fairly successful in winning the good will of the Iroquois. The Indians sent a number of their children, both boys and girls, to Quebec to be educated.

In the fall of 1674 La Salle returned to France, after eight years of wilderness life in America. He saw the

King and was made a noble in reward for his daring deeds. He returned to New France in the summer of 

1675 and began preparations for another and greater voyage. He built a large number of canoes and four good-sized vessels for trade on lake and river. He

LA SALLE 

141 ne alle Wa raised wheat, peas, and beans. He had become a seignior (sen'yer), or lord of an estate, owning much land and having many workers and servants around him. 

3. LA SALLE, FATHER HENNEPIN, AND TONTY 

On the return trip to America in 1675, La Salle met two remarkable men. One was Tonty (ton'tē), who became the faithful friend of La Salle, supporting him loyally in all his hardships and trials. The other was Father Louis 

Hennepin (hĕn'ně pin), a Franciscan (från sis'kăn) priest. The Franciscans were of a different order from the Jesuits of whom you have already learned. 

Tonty was an Italian. He was a brave soldier with only one hand. He had lost the other hand in battle, and in its place he wore a hand made of iron. For this reason he was called "Tonty of the Iron Hand." He was a strong man, and he could strike a "knockout blow" with his iron hand. We shall see more of Tonty as we follow the brave deeds of La Salle. 

Father Hennepin was born in France about 1640. As a boy his greatest desire was to listen to the stories which sea captains told of their long voyages. He often hid behind tavern doors to hear the sailors tell of their travels. He listened eagerly to their adventures at sea, and to the stories of dangers encountered in foreign lands. He says he would have passed whole days. and nights in this way without eating, for he wished to learn of life in foreign countries, and of the beauty and riches of other lands. Here was a man whose soul was on fire with longing for adventure. No danger could hold him back.

EARLY AMERICA 

Father Hennepin first went to Holland where he nursed soldiers wounded in battle, and poor people who were sick with diseases that other people were afraid of catching. He fell ill himself and came "within an inch of the grave." After coming to America he served for a while as a missionary around Quebec. From Quebec he often went into the woods alone with his dog-sled. He tried to learn all about the country and to do good to the Indians whom he met.

Soon he went up the river to Ft. Frontenac to meet La 

Salle, for he had been selected to join La Salle's party. 

There he explored the surrounding country, going as far up the Niagara River as his canoe could take him. When he came to the plunging rapids below the falls he climbed the steep bank and went on afoot. He heard the roar of the falls and soon the wonderful view of Niagara burst upon his sight. Before his gaze was one of the wonders of the world.

In 1677 La Salle sailed again for France, this time to obtain from the King the right to explore the Mississippi. La Salle agreed to bear the expense of the expedi tion, expecting to pay for it by the furs he obtained. He wished to claim for France the whole of inland America. 

He knew the English were looking with longing eyes on the western country, hoping to seize it for themselves. 

The King gave La Salle the right to explore the western part of New France, and to build forts to defend it against enemies and any others who might claim the territory. He was given control of all the buffalo hides which could be had in the country.

LA SALLE 

143 10 དྲ། fa 

4. LA SALLE'S FIRST ATTEMPT TO REACH THE MISSISSIPPI 

When La Salle returned to America, he began to build a big boat, about two miles above Niagara Falls. He he wanted a vessel that could sail the Great Lakes. Roundabout was plenty of timber and the wood-cutters, carpenters, and ship-builders were kept busy. The Indians looked in wonder on the wooden monster with its giant ribs.

In the spring the vessel was ready for launching. All the provisions, firearms, and powder had to be carried up the steep banks of the Niagara and around the falls. It was hard, slow work. On the prow of the boat a griffen he was carved and Griffen became the name of the vessel, or Griffon in French. A griffen is an imaginary creature, half-bird, half-beast, with the head and wings of an eagle and the body of a lion. It stood for swiftness and the strength. So La Salle thought Griffen a good name for his new vessel. The Griffen had five cannons on board. When she was launched, the cannons were fired, a hymn was sung, and there was loud cheering by both the whites and the redskins. The vessel was forced by sails and tow-ropes against the current of the Niagara until it floated out on Lake Erie, the first sailing vessel ever seen on that freshwater inland sea.

The Griffen sailed through Lake Erie, on through the Detroit (dē troit') River, through Lake St. Clair, and the 

St. Clair River, and at last into Lake Huron. Here it

EARLY AMERICA. 

AMERICAN MUSEUM OF NATURAL H FIRST SAILING OF THE Griffen ON LAKE ERIE 

was met by a terrific storm, so fierce that the vessel had to lie by for a night. Then it sailed on, passed through 

Mackinac Strait, and on to Green Bay. Here La Salle heard that enemies in Montreal were telling false stories about him and were seizing his property. He sent the 

Griffen back with the valuable cargo of furs that had already been collected, believing that the furs would satisfy the people to whom he owed money. He would wait for the return of the Griffen at the lower end of Lake Michigan. But the Griffen never returned, nor was it ever heard of again. In one of the lakes it went down with all on board and with its rich cargo of furs. 

After sending the Griffen back to Montreal, La Salle proceeded on his journey with four canoes and fourteen men, and all the supplies and guns that his canoes could carry. Gales and storms tossed them about; two of their canoes were wrecked, though the supplies were saved. 

It was hard to row in the daytime and hard to drag their canoes up the steep banks at night.

LA SALLE 

145 the Her re 

LA SALLE AND HIS MEN NEAR THE PLACE WHERE CHICAGO IS NOW 

Once the party took shelter in a cave for five days. 

They had pumpkin and Indian corn for food, and once a fine, fat porcupine which they caught and ate with great pleasure. They started out again, but soon they were driven for shelter to a little island where they shivered with cold for two days around a driftwood fire. At times they nearly starved, for they had only a little boiled corn to eat. Some of the men ate some wild haws from hawthorn trees, which made them very ill. Such were the hardships of their journey along the Wisconsin shore of Lake Michigan.

In spite of such dangers and difficulties La Salle and his men pushed on to the southern end of Lake Michigan. 

They crossed the south end of the lake to the mouth of the St. Joseph River where they built Ft. Miami. They waited for a time for Tonty, who had gone in search of the Griffen. At last he returned, but he had no news of

EARLY AMERICA 

the ill-fated vessel. Yet La Salle was not yet ready to give up the Griffen as lost.

The explorers went up the St. Joseph to the place where South Bend, Indiana, is now. From there they carried their canoes and provisions over the portage to the Kankakee (kǎn'kǎ ke'). Down this sluggish stream they glided into the Illinois and on to the spot where Peoria is today. Here they built another fort, which La 

Salle named Crevecoeur (krâv'kûr'), or Broken Heart. (See map, page 130.) By this time La Salle may have felt heartsick.

Here six of the men deserted, joining the wood-rangers. 

Two of these were his best carpenters and builders. 

The wood-rangers were French adventurers and wanderers who gave up civilized life and roamed from place to place in the woods, at times joining the Indians, marrying Indian squaws, raising half-breed children, and living in Indian fashion.

From Crevecoeur La Salle sent Hennepin and two companions to explore the upper Mississippi. They went six hundred miles up the river. One day while the two men were cooking a wild turkey and Hennepin was patching the canoe, a band of Indians came upon them. These were the fierce Sioux (soo), or Dakotahs (dá kō'táz).

They were out on the warpath against another tribe of Indians. Their bodies were painted in bright colors from head to foot. They had straight, coarse, black hair, decorated with red and white feathers. When these savage warriors landed from their canoes and rushed upon Hennepin and his men, the explorers expected to have their heads split open at once. Instead they were

FATHER HENNEPIN AT THE FALLS OF ST. ANTHONY 

The 11. taken captive and were carried off to the far north into Minnesota (min e sō'tá). Here they lived for eight months among the Sioux before they were set free. On their journey back Hennepin discovered the Falls of St.

Anthony (sant ǎn'tó ni) near Minneapolis (min ẽ ăp'ó lís).

Hennepin returned to Europe and wrote an account of his journeys. No living man could tell more than he about the life and customs of the Indians. He died in 1701.

Meanwhile La Salle had left Tonty with a small force of men to guard the fort at Peoria. Then, with five companions, he had started back to Montreal, on a long, dan-

EARLY AMERICA 

gerous journey. He wanted to secure supplies and to see if he could learn something of the Griffen. 

Never was there a journey of greater hardship and suffering through a wilderness of more than a thousand miles. "The thaws of spring," said La Salle, "increased the difficulty of the way. Everywhere there were marshes and rivers. There were hostile Indians. We must suffer all the time with hunger; sleep on the open ground, and often without a fire; watch by night and march by day, loaded with baggage, such as blankets, clothing, kettle, hatchet, guns, powder, lead, and skins for moccasins; sometimes pushing through thickets, sometimes climbing rocks covered with ice and snow, sometimes wading whole days through marshes where the water was waistdeep. Though I knew all this, it did not prevent me from resolving to go on foot to Fort Frontenac, to learn for myself what had become of my vessel and to bring back the things we needed."

La Salle overcame all difficulties and reached Montreal. 

He learned nothing of the Griffen, but he obtained supplies and started back. He had not gone far when a messenger met him bringing sad news from Tonty. The men at Broken Heart had deserted, destroyed the fort, and had stolen the supplies. La Salle's expedition seemed wrecked.

In the face of this discouraging news La Salle and his men pushed on over the long, hard journey to rescue his friend. He found no trace of the fort or of Tonty, nothing but desolation. 

This was enough to break the stoutest heart, and La

LA SALLE 

149 Salle was downcast, but he did not give up. He pushed on down the Illinois to the place where it joins the Mississippi; but he could not go down that big river with so few men. In the hope of finding Tonty and other helpers, he started back to Ft. Miami, in southern Michigan. There he learned that Tonty was at Mackinac. La 

Salle went the whole length of Lake Michigan to find him, and there he heard from Tonty's own lips the story of the disaster at Broken Heart. 

5. LA SALLE REACHES THE GOAL 

Together La Salle and Tonty returned to Canada for supplies, and in the fall of 1681 were on their way back to the Illinois country. In the dead of winter they reached the lower end of Lake Michigan. The streams were frozen over, but they made sledges and placed their canoes upon them. They went up the Chicago River, passed over the portage to the Des Plaines and then on to the Illinois River-the stream Marquette and Joliet had paddled up on their return from the south. Over the frozen course of the Illinois they dragged their sleds till they came to open water near the place where their fort, Crevecoeur, or Broken Heart, had stood.

They were now on their way to their goal. They floated along the Illinois to the Mississippi, where they found large blocks of ice drifting down the river that forced them to remain in camp for a week, until the river was safe for their canoes. Then they floated down the "Father of Waters" without mishap, passing the mouths of its many branches. Where the city of Memphis (měm'fis) now stands, they built another fort. Push-

EARLY AMERICA Copyright 1903 by The Card Courtesy Singer Manufacturing Co. 

LA SALLE AT THE MOUTH OF THE MISSISSIPPI, 1682 

ing on once more, they finally came to the mouth of the Mississippi where it flows into the Gulf. 

La Salle had reached his goal at last. He had discovered the mouth of the great river. He now took possession of the country in the name of France and gave it the name of "Louisiana," (loo e'zē ăn'á) in honor of the

King, Louis XIV. He claimed for his country "all the nations, peoples, provinces, cities, towns, villages, mines, minerals, fisheries, streams, and rivers" from the Ohio to the sea and on to the Rio Grande (ri'ō grän’dē). 

6. LA SALLE'S EXPEDITION TO THE GULF 

La Salle returned safely to Quebec, and then to France. 

Two years later he made another expedition to America,

LA SALLE 

151 ד but this was his last. Along with him went a company of colonists with the purpose of planting a settlement in 

Louisiana. He planned to go straight by sea to the mouth of the Mississippi. During this voyage La Salle was ill much of the time with a raging fever. He missed the mouth of the river, going too far to the west. He landed his force on the coast of the present State of Texas, not far from where Galveston (găl'věs tón) is today, about four hundred miles west of the mouth of the Mississippi.

Here he found himself in a wild and unknown land, with a company of about two hundred people, many of whom could not be trusted. He started overland to find the Mississippi. He had to build rafts to cross the swollen rivers. Some of his men deserted; others were killed by Indians. They were marching over vast and trackless plains, and no one knew how far they had to go. Five of the men planned to kill La Salle. They carried out their evil plot by firing upon their leader from ambush. He died instantly. "Such was the end of one of the greatest men of the age," said Tonty, and no one knew La Salle better than Tonty.

La Salle died while still a young man, in the midst of his labors, without having seen their full success. No monument marks the place of his death. The exact spot is not known, but it was somewhere on the plains of southern Texas.

Hard luck seemed always to have dogged his footsteps. His first mishap came when a disloyal pilot wrecked one of his boats on the Genesee River, with a loss

EARLY AMERICA 

of all its supplies. He lost the Griffen, with its rich cargo. This was a blow from which he never fully recovered. His enemies stirred his men to disloyalty. 

One of them tried to shoot him; others tried to poison him. Some of his canoes were wrecked on Lake Michigan. Storms and hurricanes and pelting rains seemed to pursue him. In his absence from the fort at Broken 

Heart, near Peoria, his men deserted, destroyed the fort which they were left to guard, and stole the supplies. 

He had to endure the severest hardships of cold and hunger on long journeys to and from Canada, in a wild country, always in danger of Indian attack. He triumphed over all these difficulties only to meet an early death at the hands of traitors among his own men. 

But in spite of hardship, disappointments, and cruelty, La Salle had discovered the Ohio, had gone to the mouth of the Mississippi, and raised there the colors of France. 

The French had built forts in the interior, at Kingston and Niagara (in Canada); at Detroit, Mackinac, and 

St. Joseph (in Michigan); at La Fayette (lä’fā ĕt') and 

Vincennes (vin senz') (in Indiana); at Memphis (in Tennessee), and at other important places. They finally reached the headwaters of the Ohio and built a fort where 

Pittsburgh (pits'bûrg) now stands. Here they met English settlers who were beginning to come across the Allegheny Mountains. Then and there began a struggle for the possession of North America. We shall later see the outcome of that struggle.
