16. THE FRENCH AND THE INDIANS 1. HOW THE FRENCH TREATED THE INDIANS 

When the first French fur traders came to America, they had no homes of their own. They found the Indians living in crude wigwams, very different from the fine dwellings which the Spaniards found among the civilized Aztecs, Incas, and Mayans, about whom we have read. 

The Indians in the north were not nearly so civilized as those in Mexico and farther to the south. 

The French knew that they could carry on fur trading and fishing more successfully if they treated the natives well. At first they had no thought of staying in America and making homes here. All they wanted was the furs and fish to sell in France.

In this way it came about that the Indians and the French were usually on very friendly terms. The newcomers tried to learn the native language. The Indians did not feel that the French looked down on them or on their way of living. Many a fur trader married an Indian girl, and such marriages helped to increase the friendliness between the two peoples.

The Jesuit missionaries also had a large part in bringing about friendly relations. On their trips they went far back into the lands of the Indians and by their good 154

THE FRENCH AND THE INDIANS 

155 lives and their many kindnesses won the love and respect of the natives. This helped to pave the way for a lasting friendship between the French and the Indians. 

The most powerful Indians friendly to the French during all the years of settlement were the Algonquins. 

Champlain, you remember, joined forces with them and

S urged them to make war on the Iroquois, or "Five Nations," who lived farther south. (See map, page 130.) 

The Iroquois, as we have seen, were not a single tribe of Indians, but a powerful union of tribes-the strongest group in America. There came a time when France suffered a great loss through the hatred of the Iroquois. 

But that story will come later. the

As 

2. HOW THE INDIANS LIVED 

The Algonquins lived in long houses of bark, several families and many dogs in each house. They cooked indoors over the many fireplaces that lined one side of the building. Fish and deer, called venison (věnʼĭ săn), or bear meat was the usual meal. Corn was the chief article of food, but there were beans, squash, pumpkins, and melons besides. The women, of course, did all the cooking.

Among the other household duties of a squaw (skwâ) was the making of clothes for the entire family. This was not really an easy task, for she had first to dress the animal skins, then smoke them, and finally saw and trim them. You would have liked the trimmings of beads and the gay borders.

The Indian woman also did the family gardening. She took care of the little vegetable patch, using such simple

EARLY AMERICA 

Courtesy Museum of the American Indian, Heye Foundation 

DECORATED BIRCH BARK BASKET AND SAP BUCKET MADE BY ALGONQUINS 

IN QUEBEC tools as a sharp stick and a stone spade or a hoe. She knew no better garden tools than these until the incoming whites brought them from Europe.

In fact, the squaw knew no other kind of life than the dull one she had always lived, and she seemed satisfied. 

It was no hardship for her to gather firewood and build the fire, to make the cooking vessels and prepare the food. 

Her mother had done all these tasks and she had been taught to do them when she was a girl. In time she would teach her own daughters how to do them, too, for they must know how to do a woman's work when they grew up.

In the Indian family the man was the food-getter and the protector. He did the hunting and trapping and fishing. He early learned to handle bow and arrow and tomahawk; for he did the fighting, as well. He saw to it that his boys became good swimmers, runners, and trappers. He showed them how to track an enemy to his hiding-place, and how to kill him, if necessary.

THE FRENCH AND THE INDIANS 

157 The Indian brave, or warrior, built the swift birchbark canoe.

He made the wampum beads from shells. 

These were highly prized, for they had many uses in an Indian tribe. They were used for money and for decorations, and also for the keeping of records. The Indians had no written language, and patterns in wampum often told what the tribe was doing. 

The pleasures of the Indians were few and simple. 

They smoked, gambled, and feasted, and enjoyed long silences together. They had strange dances, for which they painted their faces and made a great deal of noise. 

It is said that Governor Frontenac once painted his face red, and, tomahawk in hand, joined a war dance to please the Indians and to show them that he, too, enjoyed their pastimes.

Through the good work of the Jesuits many of the Indians became friendly to the French.

17.  1. DIFFERENT CLASSES OF PEOPLE IN NEW FRANCE 

The most important man in New France was the one whom the king named for governor. As the chief officer living among the French settlers and their Indian neighbors, he had to govern the colony and let the king know what was going on in the region. 

Other officers, usually favorites of the king, helped the governor to rule the settlers. These officers, of whom there were only ten or twelve, were members of a group of men who formed what was called the Council. 

It was the duty of the members of the Council to meet together each week, talk over the needs of the settlers, and send long reports to the king. The governor and the officers usually lived in Montreal or Quebec. 

When the king decided to send settlers to America, he made it known that he would pay the traveling expenses of any French people who cared to go and also give each man a piece of land for himself. 

The men who received the largest tracts of land often had estates that extended more than a mile along the St. Lawrence and several miles back into the wilderness. 

Such men, who were called seigniors, gave small parts of their land to soldiers and poor farmers. In Europe the poor farmers were known as peasants, but in French 159

EARLY AMERICA 

Canada they received the name of habitants (hăb'itănts). The seigniors collected a small rent and a few days' work each year from the habitants. 

If you take an automobile trip some summer and drive through the old French part of Canada, you will see the same small, narrow farms along the St. Lawrence, some of them with only three or four acres, with Frenchspeaking habitants at work in the well-kept fields. 

Farther back from the towns and villages lived the wood-rangers. Many of these had begun life in good 

French families and had fought as soldiers in Europe. 

Coming to America, they had chosen to live in the wilds, roving among the Indians, and seeking furs in parts of the interior never before visited by white men. They lived very much as the Indians lived, and many of them married Indian girls.

In the wilderness, too, dwelt the French missionaries, most of whom were Jesuits, like Father Marquette. 

They lived much like the Indians, ate the same coarse food, and went by canoe and on foot, deep into regions where for months they might see no white person. 

One other class of people lived in New France-the merchants, who grew rich through trading in furs. For the most part their homes were in the towns near the 

St. Lawrence River. 

2. HOMES 

The officers and the merchants, of course, had the best homes. Usually these were built in imitation of the feudal castles in France, but were much more simple. 

Protection from attack was provided by a drawbridge

31 328 ܐ܂ ம்!

From Sulte's Canadiens Français CARTIER'S HOME IN CANADA 

and a ditch, gun platforms, and a place to store arms and iammunition. Deeply-sloping roofs came down to the second story. Several chimneys were needed to carry away the smoke from the wood-burning fireplaces. A gallery or balcony ran around the second floor of the house. 

Windows and doors were few. Outside a small garden furnished a few vegetables and fruits in season. ער IS The homes of the seigniors were not so large as those of the officers and merchants. Usually the seignior lived out in the country on the large tract of land given to him by the king or the king's officers. Near his house were the rough little cabins of the habitants, a fort, a chapel, and a mill. These were all built of logs or stone. They huddled close to each other and close to the street, a cluster of small buildings forming a village such as may still be seen in some parts of Quebec.

EARLY AMERICA 

The seignior's house was the best dwelling in the vil lage, but even so it was not a handsome structure. On the first floor were the living room and kitchen; over it was the bedroom with its outside gallery. Rough, homemade furniture, hand-woven woolen rugs, pictures of saints, cooking kettles, guns, candlesticks, and spinning wheels might be seen in the living room of any seignior. 

Only a few had furniture from France. 

In the bedrooms hard mattresses lay upon the floor. 

There were no closets. A home-made chair or two and a rough chest of drawers were the only other furnishings. 

To the west of Quebec, where the country was wild, the houses were rough and very much like the rude dwellings of the Indians. Here lived the wood-rangers and their families and a few soldiers. Their log houses were built close to the forts and trading posts, forming tiny villages in wilderness clearings. Such villages were not at all like the villages and cities in France, but more like the neighboring Indian villages. 

3. FOOD AND CLOTHING 

In New France the food, like everything else, was simple and without much change from day to day. For meat the settlers usually had venison, salt pork, and beef. Fish was plentiful in the rivers and lakes of the interior, as well as along the coast. 

The colonists raised their own potatoes, peas, beans, and cucumbers. Melons and wild fruits in season served as desserts. Wheat and oats were also planted on the small farms, and the settlers did their own grinding.

Milk and cheese were used in the homes of the settlers 

From France came their coffee, sugar, who kept cows. and liquor.

The French chose a cold region in America in which to settle, and for many months of the year they had to dress warmly. Furs and wool made up most of their clothing. ha! At first the furs were easy to get; beaver, otter, mink, bear, and deer roamed the forests near the 

Atlantic coast. But as the villages sprang up, most of the animals within easy reach were killed, and the few that were left proved much harder to find. Then the villagers near the coast had to buy their furs

CANADIAN PACIFIC RAILWA 

A VERY OLD MILL IN QUEBEC 

from the Indians who came from great distances, once a year, carrying the winter's catch.

The wool from which the homespun was made meant a great deal of work to the settlers. the sheep and, when the season was 

They had to raise right, cut off the wool. The women must ther clean the wool and spin it into yarn, which looked ugly and dirty until it was cleaned

EARLY AMERICA 

and dyed. The French were very fond of color and used bright dyes for most of their wools.

The habitants wore baggy trousers of homespun, topped with woolen coats. Around their waists gaycolored scarfs were tied. Woolen caps and long, handknit stockings gave another splash of color to the costume, although the stockings were almost entirely covered by long deer-skin moccasins that often came up to the knees.

The women and children, too, had home-made clothes of wool and fur. Long dresses, plainly cut, with tight waists and full, roomy skirts, bright caps and scarfs, and colorful mittens and stockings were fashionable in every village.

Among the officers and merchants in the towns, clothes were worn which had come from France. Fine woolens and velvets in beautiful colors, long silk stockings, buckled shoes-all marked the wearers as above the common people. 

4. MAKING A LIVING 

Between 1607 and 1680 the French in America were interested chiefly in exploration and the fur trade. Only enough farming was done during this period to provide a few articles for home use.

When the King of France saw that the settlers cared only for fur trading and wanted to buy their food from France, he declared that food must be raised by the settlers themselves. After that, some farms produced more than enough oats, peas, and beans for themselves, and sent to France whatever they had left over. But farm-

FRENCH, LIFE IN AMERICA 

165 Te 10

THE 

the ing never became the chief way of making a living in the 

French colonies.

The fur trade was the important business of the French in America. It was carried on chiefly by commercial companies and by favorites of the king. Only a few merchants became wealthy through fur trading, but it was the chief business, and most of the settlers and Indians had some part in it. 

In the summer of each year great fairs were held in towns like Quebec, Montreal, and Three Rivers on the St. 

Lawrence. At these fairs the Indians exchanged their winter's supply of furs for blankets, guns, cooking vessels, liquor, and gay trinkets, which had come from France.

The skins were taken over by the French merchants, and most of them shipped to France. were 

The towns were gay, in 

A WOOD-RANGER 

Hundreds of canoes, deed, during the great fairs. manned by two or more Indians, and heaped high with furs, would come swiftly along the St. Lawrence after a journey of several hundred miles. Even from the re-

EARLY AMERICA 

gions of Green Bay and Mackinac they came, led by wood-rangers. A strange mixture of French and Indian tongues could be heard as the canoes were pulled up on shore.

Just outside of the town the Indians pitched their tents and unloaded their goods. They knew that they would be there for ten days or more. After they unpacked, they held council meetings with the Frenchmen; the pipe of peace was smoked; and a promise of friendship for the next year was solemnly made. 

On rough counters, hastily put up, not far from the river, the French merchants showed their wares. They tried to make them look as attractive as possible. Of course there were guns and ammunition, cloth, axes, knives, and kettles, all of which were useful. Besides these, rings and chains, bells and tin mirrors glittered in the sunlight and awakened longings in the hearts of the Indians.

Then, too, there was always liquor, too much of it, at the great fairs. Many an Indian trapper exchanged the work of a whole year for enough liquor to keep him in a drunken stupor for two or three days.

Beaver was the fur the white people liked best. It was so soft and warm and it wore so well that it was in greater demand than any other furs the Indians might have to sell. Thousands of beaver skins were sent to 

France each year.

When the trading was over, the wood-rangers led the procession of canoes back into the interior for another season of hunting and trapping to get ready for the next year's fairs. And once more the wood-rangers would

seek out other Indians, farther back in the wilderness, and try to persuade them to bring their furs to the French trading posts and to join the next group of traders on the long journey to the towns on the St. Lawrence the following summer.

For many years the wood-rangers were poorly paid for their hard work. The Indians also received goods of very small value in exchange for their furs. The keepers of the trading posts, too, were poor, although they handled great quantities of supplies from France and furs worth a great deal of money. 

Such conditions naturally made these people angry. 

They did not like to see the fur merchants in the towns growing wealthy, while the men who did the real work 

B received barely enough to live on.

For this reason the wood-rangers began to do their own trapping and trading. Their action was against the law, to be sure, but the wood-rangers had grown tired of being poor. Besides, the King of France was very far away.

He had sent officers to America to make laws for the settlers, and the people were not allowed to say what the laws should be. It was all quite unfair, thought the wood-rangers, and therefore they would do as they pleased for a change. Many of them became successful fur traders.

When the Dutch and the English came from Europe and began to trade with the Indians for furs, the French found that their own fur trade fell off. They learned also that the Indians were often able to get more for their furs, when dealing with the Dutch and the English, than when dealing with the French. 1

EARLY AMERICA 

In spite of the rivalry that followed, the fur trade continued for many years to be the main interest of the French settlers. Their farms were small, as we have seen, and produced little that could be sold in France. 

There was no manufacturing. Every village had its carpenters and blacksmiths and a few tradesmen, as had the French villages in Europe. But in the fur trade alone the settlers believed riches might be found. 

5. TRAVEL 

Traveling, as most of us think of it today, means covering long distances by train or bus, by steamship or airplane or automobile. But in the days of the early French settlers travel was much less rapid and far more difficult than it is today.

The explorers, as we have seen, traveled on foot, by canoe, and by sail-boat. At the portages between two rivers they had to carry their goods and their canoes across wild and tangled country to the next stream. 

From the Indians the explorers learned to make the birch-bark canoes which they used wherever possible.

In a few years horses were brought to New France. 

Then travel on horseback took the place of walking, and loads of goods were shifted from men's shoulders to the backs of horses. Of course there were no hard roads, and most travel was uncomfortable. 

6. PLAY AND AMUSEMENT 

The French settlers had to provide their own good times. They worked hard, to be sure, but whenever they could they made play of their work. When there was

corn to be husked or a building to be put up, the neighbors were asked to come and help. Often the women and girls got together to knit and spin and to grind corn. good meal and singing and dancing made the time pass happily.

For the officers and the merchants in the towns there were dances and plays such as they had enjoyed in France, but there were no theaters nor large places of entertainment such as we know today. They acted their own plays, for no companies of actors came over from 

France to perform for them.

On holidays, and while a fair was being held, everyone wore his best clothes and joined in the general gaiety. 

The children, too, enjoyed these good times. The fair gave the townspeople a chance to meet the French people who came from far inland to engage in the trading. 

Indeed, the season of the fair was the only time they saw anybody but their own families and neighbors. 

Can you imagine what it must have been like to live in a rough little French village with such simple interests and pleasures? 7.

EDUCATION 

In New France there were few educated people. The priests and the officers, of course, were educated men, but among the settlers not more than two in every thousand people could read and write.

Schools were started by the priests, and after a few years nuns came from France to help them with the teaching. The chief subject was religion, for both boys and girls. The boys learned reading, writing, and arith-

EARLY AMERICA 

metic, but the girls spent their school day in learning to read and sew.

The villages were so scattered, and some of them were so far back in the country that schools were very few 

WAYSIDE CHAPEL IN QUEBEC, PROBABLY HUNDREDS OF YEARS OLD 

and were separated by wide distances.

When New France was a hundred and fifty years old, it contained only thirty schools for boys and very few for girls.

These were all private schools, of course. 

There were no newspapers in New 

France, and the only books were on relig ion. The French did not start to to print their own books shortly after settling in America, as did the Spaniards, far to the south. 

The first college in New France was started in Quebec. Today part of the old college still stands. You may see it, if you visit Laval (lä vǎl') University, where many French Canadians now receive their education. In Quebec, too, a convent school was opened

PART FOUR ENGLAND SENDS COLONISTS TO AMERICA

ENGLAND SENDS COLONISTS TO AMERICA 

While the Spaniards and the French were trying to get what they could of the New World's treasure and lands, the English people were not napping. 

The Cabots, in search of a northwest passage to the Indies, discovered land along the eastern coast of North America and claimed it for England. Sir Francis Drake, fighting favorite of Queen Elizabeth, whipped the Spaniards and drove them to shelter in harbors along the shores of the Pacific Ocean. 

Thus Drake claimed the western coast of the new continent for his Queen and country. 

These discoveries and claims gave the English people a place to go and live when they became dissatisfied at home. 

Poor English farmers, men without work or homes, adventurers seeking gold but finding none settled in Virginia and the Carolinas. Pilgrims and Puritans, unwilling to worship 

God as the King commanded, came to the Massachusetts region. Maryland offered religious freedom to persecuted 

Catholics, and Pennsylvania's first colonists were Quakers. 

Poor debtors found a refuge in Georgia. 

Dutch fur traders in New York and New Jersey, and Swedish fur traders along the Delaware River, were too few in number to hold out against the English, and in 1664 their territory fell into the hands of the Duke of York and became a part of the English colonies.

Busy, well-behaved, taking part in the making of their own laws, and not afraid of hardship and danger, the settlers in the first thirteen English colonies laid the foundation for the United States of America. They were independent colonies, each with its own work, trade, and government, but they were settled chiefly by the English, and naturally there was a bond of sympathy and understanding among them.

By Percy Moran. From a Copley Print copyrighted by Curtis and Cameron, Inc. 

SIGNING THE MAYFLOWER COMPACT

18. HOW ENGLAND ENTERED  1. THE CABOTS DISCOVER THE EAST COAST OF NORTH AMERICA 

The Pope's Line of Division, you will recall, divided the newly-discovered lands between the Spanish and the Portuguese. Have you wondered how the other nations of Europe felt about this? Kings in wide-awake countries were unwilling to sit back with folded hands and accept the Pope's decision. They wanted their share of the new lands, and they decided to find out what that share was.

The King of England, Henry the Seventh, was one of the rulers who was interested. He had had a chance to help Columbus, but had refused. Then Columbus received help from Spain. King Henry had "turned a deaf ear and lost a new world.”

Columbus had already sailed west, seeking a short cut to the East for Spain. Diaz had rounded the Cape of 

Good Hope, and soon Da Gama would open a new southern and eastern route to India for Portugal. The King of England must send his sailors to seek the East by a wholly different way. "A route to the northwest would be good," he thought, "for then we should not be interfering with the Span- 179

EARLY AMERICA 

iards and the Portuguese. Besides, if we owned a northwest route, our ships would pass through a cold climate, and there we should be able to sell the woolen goods made in England and secure the furs that we so much desire." 

In the English seaport, Bristol (bris'tol), there lived a merchant named Cabot (ka'bot). At least he was called 

Cabot in England, but his real name was Caboto (kǎ bō'tō), for he was an Italian, like Columbus. He was also an excellent sailor, and had little trouble in persuading King Henry to allow him to set out on a voyage to India by way of America. 

King Henry gave permission, but he did not furnish the money for the voyage. 

Some English merchants gave that help. With one small ship and a crew of eighteen men John Cabot and his son set sail from England in 1497.

They took a more northerly route than Columbus had taken. They landed on the coast of Labrador. Of course Cabot and his men did not know where they were. Some of them thought they had reached the coast of China. 

JOHN CABOT 

Cabot was the first European, after the Northmen, to reach North America, for Columbus did not touch the continent until the following year (1498), when he made his third voyage. A royal welcome awaited Cabot on his return to England, and King Henry, who was never very

generous, even gave him ten pounds (about fifty dollars) for finding a "New Isle."

A year later Cabot again set out for the New World. 

This time he explored the coast of North America, probably as far south as South Carolina, but of this no one has ever been certain. While on this voyage John Cabot disappeared and "was lost in the gloom of the western ocean."

His son Sebastian (sẽ băstyăn) then took command, and his stories of their expedition have always been accepted with some doubt. He seemed to recall a great many things that sounded impossible, and some people believe that they never happened at all. 

The Cabots failed to find a northwest passage to the land of gold and spices; and, although they had found some new lands, King Henry was disappointed. What did he care for bleak coasts and howling forests and dark savages? What he sought was the spices and the riches of the East.

Nevertheless the voyages of John Cabot are very important. He discovered the continent of North America, which gave England her claim to the land now occupied by the United States and Canada. 

2. HOW DRAKE CLAIMED THE WEST COAST OF NORTH AMERICA FOR ENGLAND 

Although the Cabots had discovered North America in 1497, for eighty years England did nothing to follow up his work. They were busy years for England, for she was having trouble with Spain. Spanish treasure ships from the New World were often stopped and stripped of their gold by English pirates. In those days piracy, or

EARLY AMERICA 

robbery on the high seas, was not against the law as it is today. Even the English ruler, Queen Elizabeth, encouraged the attacks on the Spanish vessels. 

One of the boldest of the English pirates was John Hawkins, who made several voyages to the Spanish colonies with African slaves. You will remember that

Negroes took over the work of the Indian slaves when Spain put a stop to slavery among the Indians. John 

Hawkins became a rich man, for slave-trading and piracy paid very well.

Such ways of making a living were exciting and dangerous. In 1568 Hawkins' small fleet was attacked and beaten by the Spaniards near the coast of Mexico. 

One of his captains at that time was Francis Drake, who had been a sailor since he was ten years of age. 

Hawkins and Drake succeeded in escaping from the Spaniards; the two men swore that they would get even with their enemies, and they at once began to plan for revenge.

Queen Elizabeth greatly admired young Francis Drake and helped him to make ready for his next voyage to America. Away he sailed, in 1577, happy in the thought that at last revenge was at hand. He had a most exciting expedition, which brought him immense wealth, honor, and reputation.

Sailing south around Cape Horn and then north along the western coast of South America, he plundered Spanish ships and towns by the sea, gathering large quantities of gold, silver, jewels, and other goods of great value.

Spaniards on a return trip to England across the Atlan-

DRAKE'S VOYAGE AROUND THE GLOBE 

tic, he kept on to the north, and finally reached a harbor near the present city of San Francisco, California; some historians believe he sailed as far north as Seattle 1 (se'atl) or Vancouver (văn koo'ver). After repairing 

P his ships he struck out boldly to the west across the Pacific.

On the map you will see that he sailed through the 

Philippines and the Spice Islands, following Magellan's route around the Cape of Good Hope, and so back to England in 1580. He was the first Englishman to sail around the world. 

And what a welcome he received upon his return to

England! He was a rich man now, for he had captured treasure worth about three million dollars. Queen Elizabeth visited his ship to show her delight at his success and to thank him for the jewels and gold he had brought her. She honored him with knighthood, and he was then known as Sir Francis Drake.

EARLY AMERICA QUEEN ELIZABETH HONORS DRAKE WITH KNIGHTHOOD 

Drake's voyage again reminded Europe that America was a new continent instead of a few islands off the coast of Asia, as had been supposed. England now claimed the western coast of North America, which Drake had explored.

The King of Spain was furious. Drake had plundered 

Spanish ships and towns and he should have been punished, the Spaniards thought, instead of being honored. 

The King of Spain saw his power in the New World slipping away.

Eight years later (1588) a great Spanish fleet attacked England, and Sir Francis Drake was among the bold English fighters who destroyed the dreaded Spanish

0 Armada (är ma'dá). The way was now open to establish English colonies in America. 

3. How RALEIGH TRIED TO PLANT AN ENGLISH COLONY IN AMERICA 

Before the days of Walter Raleigh many English and Spanish people thought of the New World as a good place in which to seek gold and to carry on trade. They were eager to come to America and return to Europe as soon as possible with all the wealth they could gather. But Raleigh had a different plan. He thought it was not enough to claim land in the New World; a country must make sure of its claim by sending people to live on the land. Raleigh gave his fortune and the best years of his life in trying to carry out his plan. 

SIR HUMPHREY GILBERT 

Like Drake, Raleigh was a brave young fighter. When only seventeen, he had fought in France, and later had served as a soldier in Holland and in Ireland. He, too, attracted the attention of Queen Elizabeth and was knighted by her. She became very fond of him and was proud to have him at her court. Gifts from the Queen-money, a London palace, and extensive lands in Ireland-gave him great power. 

Sir Walter Raleigh's half-brother, Sir Humphrey Gil-

EARLY AMERICA 

bert, had a charter, or written permission from Queen Elizabeth, to found a colony on new lands not yet settled by Englishmen. Of course, the Queen expected some return for her generosity, and asked that she be given onefifth of whatever gold and silver might be found in the colony. 

Raleigh and Gilbert set out for Newfoundland in 1579, but their ships were lashed by storms and were attacked by Spanish vessels which forced them back to England. 

They lost one ship in the attack, but they did not lose courage.

In 1583 Gilbert made another effort. This time he succeeded in reaching Newfoundland, but everything seemed to go wrong. The climate was cold, and farming was out of the question. Those who knew how to stand the hard life, and were strong enough, could make a living at fishing off the coast. But Newfoundland was a poor place for a colony. 

Troubles heaped upon the luckless Gilbert. His own ship was dashed to pieces upon the rocks, and a hundred of his men went down with the vessel. He decided to give up the attempt to found a colony in Newfoundland and to return to England at once. He boarded a tiny ship named the Squirrel, a vessel of only ten tons, and the Squirrel and the Golden Hind set sail together.

Fierce storms played with the small ships and tossed them about like egg-shells upon the waves. But Gilbert was not afraid. Sitting quietly in his ship, with a book in his hand, he said: "Heaven is as near by sea as by land." Presently the little ship went to the bottom with all on board.

dis 8 Ex

After Gilbert's death, his charter from the Queen passed to his half-brother, Sir Walter Raleigh, who would not give up the idea of planting a colony in the New World. He decided to try the milder climate of the south. Three different times he sent colonists across the 

Atlantic, but not one of his attempts proved successful. 

In 1585 Raleigh sent a hundred men to Roanoke Island, g in the present State of North Carolina, although Raleigh called it Virginia. The colonists were a worth- 

E less lot; and proved a to be too lazy to do farm work or any other kind of labor.

The colony was a misterable failure. Huntegry and homesick, the 200 

RALEIGH AT ROANOKE 

nd settlers gladly weltcomed the arrival of Sir Francis Drake with his fleet. th His coming saved their lives, for he provided food and return passage to England. When Raleigh came with a large store of supplies, a little later, he was surprised and greatly disappointed to find the place deserted. sea

EARLY AMERICA 

Two years later (1587), Raleigh tried a third time. A group of 150 men, together with 17 women and 11 children, was sent to Roanoke. Raleigh thought they would find the fifteen men he had left there on his last visit, but no trace of them could be discovered. The fort lay in ruins and deer were quietly grazing near by. 

It was not long before these settlers had trouble with the Indians. Then, too, food became scarce and they urged their leader, Captain John White, to return to England for more. He did not like to leave the colonists, but he could not let them starve. So he went back to

England for supplies. War between England and Spain delayed his return to Roanoke. Three years later, when he was able to go back to America, he found that the colony had been deserted. From that time to the present not a trace has been found of these people. Their complete disappearance has remained a mystery. Whether they were killed by the Indians, or went away with friendly natives and made their homes a long way off, is unknown.

Gilbert and Raleigh failed in their efforts to found an English colony in America, but they paved the way for others that were to follow. Their failure showed the 

English that it was very hard to plant colonies, and that more money and supplies of all kinds were needed than 

Gilbert and Raleigh were able to provide. Then, too, planting colonies was too big a job for one man to handle alone.

After his failure, Raleigh's life was full of unhappiness. Queen Elizabeth turned against him, partly because he married one of her maids of honor without ask-

P ing her consent. He was sent to the Tower-the old 

London prison-to think it over. 

The death of the Queen in 1603 brought a new ruler, James the First, to the English throne. King James was not a strong ruler and he was rather friendly towards Spain. He accused Raleigh of plotting against him. We do not know all the facts, but it is likely that 

Raleigh knew something about the plots against the King, for he usually knew what was going on. After a most unfair trial, Raleigh was sentenced to death. But the sentence was not carried out at once, and Raleigh awaited his doom in the Tower for thirteen long and weary years. 

After his imprisonment he was released, but not pardoned. He was permitted to go on another expedition to the northern coast of South America. Again he failed; one of his captains attacked a Spanish settlement and killed several Spaniards, thus disobeying King James's orders, and Raleigh was beheaded upon his return to England in 1618.

Raleigh was calm in the face of death. Mounting the scaffold, it is said, he took the executioner's axe in his hand. As he passed his thumb along its edge, he said, ""Tis sharp medicine, but a cure for all diseases."

19. LIFE IN OLD ENGLAND 1. RELIGIOUS DIFFERENCES 

England was once a Catholic country, and acknowledged the Pope as the head of the church. But King 

Henry the Eighth, father of Elizabeth, quarreled with the Pope and declared that the King should be head of the church in England. Most of the monasteries that had been in England were closed and Catholic buildings and lands were seized by the King. Some of them he gave to his friends and others were used for schools to take the place of the Catholic schools in which English children had been taught. This was a time of deep religious interest and bitterness.

When Elizabeth became Queen, she too declared that England should not be a Catholic country. She formed what was called the Church of England. But this did not satisfy all the people. The Catholics were of course unhappy in a country that had become Protestant, and many of the Protestants themselves were not pleased with the Church of England. 

Some years before Elizabeth became Queen, several religious leaders had gathered groups of followers around them, each group with its own ideas. Among these leaders were Luther (lu'ther), in Germany, Calvin (kǎl'vin), in Switzerland, and Knox (nox), in Scotland, 191

EARLY AMERICA 

of whom you will learn more as you go more deeply into the study of history. The Church of England did not satisfy the followers of these and other leaders. The most important of the discontented groups were the Puritans, the Presbyterians, and the Friends or Quakers. 

And, unfortunately, the Protestants of those days were as unfriendly to other Protestants as they were to the Catholics. 

2. UNSATISFACTORY GOVERNMENT 

After the death of Queen Elizabeth in 1603, James I became King. He was not well liked by the people. He made up his mind to enjoy royal power at any cost. He declared that the people were not allowed to question what the King might do, for he was all-powerful. 

The English people were glad to have Queen Elizabeth use her royal power while there was danger from Spain, but after the defeat of the Spanish Armada they wanted a share in the government. 

At that time they had elections in England to choose men to go to London to make the laws, much as Americans today elect senators and representatives to go to Washington for that purpose. In England the group of law-makers is called Parliament (păr lá měnt), while here it is called Congress (kon'grès). 

Although the members of Parliament were elected by the people to make the laws, King James always expected Parliament to agree with him. If they did not, he dismissed them, for he wanted all the power in his own hands. Many a man who displeased him was sent to the Tower, and some were beheaded there.

LIFE IN OLD ENGLAND 

193 E There were mutterings and grumblings all over England. Less power for the King, and more power for the people through Parliament, was what many people wanted. By the time Charles I, son of James I, became 

King, the grumblings had given way to real action. 

Oliver Cromwell became the leader of the group who were against the King. To show that they were not friends of the long-haired King Charles I and his favorites, he and his followers cut off their hair and so received the nickname of "Roundheads." They formed an army which successfully fought the King's army of Cavaliers (kǎ'vá lērz'), and Charles I was beheaded. 

Then Parliament ruled England for many years, determined that never again should any king have more power than the Parliament.

Cromwell and the Roundheads took the government out of the hands of the King, and then used as much power themselves as the Kings had had. They did not always act for the good of the people. They killed 

Catholics and Protestants who disagreed with them, and destroyed and ruined many churches. 

Could the English be happy under such a government? 

The unrest caused by such conditions lasted for almost a hundred years. During this time thousands of dissatisfied people left England and took up life in America where they could worship God and govern themselves as they thought right. 

3. TRYING TO MAKE A LIVING 

Besides being dissatisfied with conditions in religion and government, many people were unhappy in England

EARLY AMERICA 

for other reasons.

The country contained many classes of people-some rich, but most of them poor. 

Favorites of the King owned large grants of land, some of which had been taken from the Catholics during the reign of Henry VIII. One writer of that day (1589) said of them: "Who can live idly and without manual labour, he shall be called master, and be taken as a gentleman." Their lands were used for farming, but they did none of the work themselves. 

Some men who were not favorites of the King also became rich. The people in the towns, especially the merchants and the traders, made a great deal of money through foreign trade. Sailors and pirates brought to

England spices, jewels, silks, and rugs to sell to the merchants and traders, who sold them to eager English buyers for much more than they had paid for them. Soon the traders had enough money to buy lands from the gentlemen, or nobles, and sometimes they even bought from the King such titles as "Lord," "Duke," and "Sir."

Others who became wealthy in England were known as "yeomen" (yo'měn). These were the landowners who worked the farms of the nobles. Sometimes they worked so hard and so successfully that they were able to buy the lands of the "gentlemen."

Wealth made these people interested in bigger and better homes, with glass windows, chimneys, silk-covered beds, and other comforts not enjoyed by many people in England. Garments of silk and velvet and costly jewels were worn by both men and women who could afford such fine things. Better foods, too, were served.

LIFE IN OLD ENGLAND 

195 But most of the English people were not nobles, merchants, and successful yeomen. The country contained thousands and thousands of laborers and poor tenant farmers who had to pay high rents for the land they used. When the owners decided to enclose large tracts of land for sheep-raising, which required fewer laborers than food-raising, large numbers of farm workers found themselves with no means of making a living. 

Extensive sheep-raising was brought about by the making of woolen cloth in many English towns. The unemployed farmers flocked to the towns to look for work as weavers or spinners. Not all of them were able to learn the new trade; and many of them lived in poverty and hardship.

Sir Thomas More, in a book called Utopia (ū tō'pē á), which you will enjoy reading when you are older, wrote that the meek and tame sheep had driven farmers "out of their own houses, finding no place to rest in. And when they have wandered abroad, what can they do else but steal, or go about begging? And then also they be cast in prison as vagabonds, because they go about and work not, whom no man will set to work." 

The numbers of such people grew until there were thousands of homeless, unemployed farmers in the towns. 

In 1574 Sir Humphrey Gilbert suggested that they be taken to America, where there would be plenty of work in establishing a colony. Then England would be rid of the idle, unhappy people who might soon cause trouble at home.

This, then, was life in England, full of unrest and unhappiness. The kings were losing power. The Protest-

EARLY AMERICA 

ants did not agree. the rich merchants.

Land was owned by the nobles and Raising sheep instead of food led to the manufacture of cloth and the growth of towns, but it drove thousands of poor farmers from their homes and made beggars of them.

20. ENGLISH COLONIZING BEGUN IN AMERICA 1. THE LESSON TAUGHT BY RALEIGH'S FAILURE 

As we have seen, England had many restless and unhappy people in the reigns of Queen Elizabeth, James I, and Charles I. These people longed for the day when they could try a new life in a new country. Among them were nobles with little money, religious groups who wanted to get away from other religious groups, poor farmers out of work, beggars and persons in prison for not being able to pay what they owed. Such people believed that happiness awaited them in America. 

Sir Walter Raleigh had once been wealthy, but he had died a poor man. He had sought his happiness in trying to make colonies in America, but had lost his fortune in his attempts to establish settlements in the New World. 

Other Englishmen were not so ready to spend all their money for the glory of England, as Raleigh had done. 

They saw that no one person could afford to start a colony in America, no matter how rich he was. 

Gradually it became clear that men with money to spend on colonies in America must work together, each putting in what he could afford, but no one risking the loss of all he had. If the colony failed, then all would share the loss; if it succeeded, all would share in the gain. 198

ENGLISH COLONIZING 

199 -།, LO ODE 

2. THE LONDON AND PLYMOUTH COMPANIES 

Several Englishmen became interested in such a plan. 

They agreed to work together and formed the London and Plymouth (pli'muth) Companies, the first with members from London and the second made up of rich men who lived in Plymouth, England. Other companies were formed later.

Both the London and Plymouth Companies were made up of "Knights, Gentlemen, Merchants, and other Adventurers," who furnished the money for the new colonies. Most of them did not expect to go to America themselves.

Each company received from King James a paper called a land grant, which told them what territory they might have in America. Each also received a charter in which the King laid down certain rules for them to follow. Any rules not in the charter might be made by the company for the colonies.

Then the companies advertised for settlers. What a strange crowd of people came to answer the advertisements! There were men who had long been out of work, soldiers without jobs now that the wars with Spain were over, farmers whose lands the nobles had seized, gentlemen in search of adventure. "What is there for us to do in America ?" they eagerly asked. "Just till the soil, look for a passage to India, hunt for gold, and trade with the Indians," was the reply. "And what should we get for our work?" came the next question.

EARLY AMERICA 

"We pay your way over the Atlantic. We give you clothes and enough food to last till harvest-time. We provide you with houses, tools, sheep, cattle, and horses. 

For a few years you will be servants of the company. 

It is only fair that part of the food you raise and part of the gold you find should go to the Englishmen who provide the money to pay the cost of the plan." 

For people who were homeless and hungry, with no jobs in sight for them in England, the plan held out hope. 

At least, they thought, America cannot be a worse place to live in than England, and perhaps it is much better. 

Some of the people were willing to join in the plan, and in 1607 both companies founded colonies in the New World-the London Company at Jamestown, on the James River in Virginia, and the Plymouth Company at the mouth of the Kennebec (kěn'é běk) River in Maine. 

The colony in Maine was not successful; it lasted only a few months. Jamestown, however, lived through disease, starvation, and Indian attacks, and became the first permanent, or lasting, English settlement in America.

21. HOW SOME COLONIES WERE SETTLED TO GAIN WEALTH 1. VIRGINIA (1607) 

With high hopes the first colonists sent over by the London Company set sail from England in December, 1606. The three small ships battled storms and rough weather, but their captain, Christopher Newport, did not turn back.

It was May, 1607, when they reached Virginia, with little food, for the four months' voyage had almost exhausted their supplies. A good place had to be found for the settlement, one with mild climate and fertile soil, and not too far from the coast so that it would serve as a trading post.

Leaving the sea, the ships sailed up a broad river for thirty-five or forty miles.

They called the river James, after their King, and for him, too, they named the colony Jamestown. The place they chose would be easy to defend, they thought. But in picking out the location, they paid little attention to the company's order: "Neither must you plant in a low or moist place because it will prove unhealthful." 

And unhealthful Jamestown proved to be. It was both low and swampy; the air was damp; the drinking water SE 202

SETTLEMENTS TO GAIN WEALTH 203 

1 alm 

ART 

and!

THE LANDING AT JAMESTOWN 

was bad; mosquitoes and fevers became deadly enemies of the colonists. The newcomers soon realized that they had made a poor choice for their settlement. 

One hundred and twenty had come from England to live in Virginia. But only a handful of these knew how to do the kind of work that had to be done in the new land. There were only six carpenters, two bricklayers, one blacksmith, and one stone mason among them. Besides these, there were adventurers who came to get rich and had no idea of doing any real work. In the little band were also a number of gentlemen who had never had to work and did not know how. They apparently

EARLY AMERICA 

expected to be able to pick up gold with as much ease and in as great quantities as we pick up pebbles and shells on the beach. Before long they found that they were mistaken.

The colonists made a clearing and built a fort immediately. Then they provided for a church-a very simple one, with a sail spread out for a roof and a board wedged between two trees for a pulpit. On the board they placed the Bible and the English Prayer Book. 

Houses were needed, but building took time and hard work. Hunting for gold was much pleasanter, so the gold hunt claimed more attention than anything else. 

The colonists did manage to collect enough wood and sassafras to send back to England when the ships returned, but on the whole they were lazy, untrained, quarrelsome, and homesick. And they were always hungry, too.

The woods had to be cleared before food could be planted, and then crops were a long time coming. There was a common storehouse, with food supplied by the London Company, but there never seemed to be enough to satisfy the settlers' appetites. Often the rats ate the grain and the salted meat that had been brought from England, and the daily portion of each colonist grew smaller.

Yet many of the hungry settlers saw no real reason to work, since they had neither houses nor land of their own, and were fed whether they worked or not. Besides, when they did plant corn, the Indians often cut it down. 

Within sixty miles of Jamestown there lived about five thousand savage and warlike Indians. They did

SETTLEMENTS TO GAIN WEALTH 

205 not like the idea of having strange neighbors with pale faces, queer-looking clothes, and big ships. Whenever they had the chance, the Indians made it unsafe for the colonists to hunt, fish, and work in the fields. It is easy to see why the settlers kept out of the woods and tall grass. York R. Chickahominy

NTIC 

A N The colony needed a strong leader, and after a few months secured one in 

Captain John Smith, the ablest of the early leaders in Jamestown. Smith saved the colony from starvation and death, by making everybody work and by getting corn from friendly Indians. A busy man himself, he would not permit idleness. When he was not acting as overseer, or boss, he was out exploring and making maps of the coast. Returning from one of his exploring trips, he was injured by an explosion of gunpowder in his boat. His burns were so serious that he had to return to England—and the colonists' troubles began all over again. 10% White James Jamestown amsburg River 

Cape Charles 

Cape Henry

THE VIRGINIA SETTLEMENT 

ос Cold weather came, and with it pneumonia (nū mō’nyá) and fevers. The food supply ran low and the colonists had to kill their own animals for food, even the horses

EARLY AMERICA 

and dogs. When these gave out, they ate rats, mice, and roots and herbs. These days were called "the starving time." Well people could not keep well and sick people could not get well with such food. Captain John Smith had left four hundred colonists in Jamestown, but by spring only sixty were left alive. Fever, Indian arrows, and starvation had almost destroyed the colony. 

In the spring a new governor, Lord Delaware, arrived with fresh supplies and a number of good laws. Then the colonists really began to work with a will. The woods rang with the sound of axes as trees were chopped down. 

New houses were built and more forts sprang up. Crops were planted and the future looked bright. 

When Lord Delaware returned to England, Sir Thomas Dale was sent by the London Company to take his place. He was a harsh governor and insisted that his strict laws be obeyed. Punishments for disobedience were severe, and lawbreakers suffered the death penalty for any one of twenty different crimes; today the death penalty is usually given only when one person has killed another. One writer tells us of a colonist who "had a bodkin thrust through his tongue, and was chained to a tree."

It was a common sight to see disobedient colonists being whipped on the streets.

But Governor Dale, in spite of his harshness, did something that greatly strengthened the colony; he gave to each person a tract of land to farm for himself. By this plan every man worked harder, for he knew that the harder he worked, the better his farm would be. 

Tobacco-raising took most of the time of the settlers, as years went on. England offered such a good market

for tobacco that plantations grew and spread in Virginia, and by 1619 there were eleven settlements and towns miles apart. This was a year of three important changes in the colony. 

A new plan of government in 1619 allowed the colonists to choose representatives to make their laws. In each of the eleven towns the people chose two men to go to Jamestown to represent them and talk over their needs and their difficulties. This body of twenty-two men who made the laws for Virginia was called the House of Burgesses (bûr'jes ěz). They passed laws against drinking, gambling, and idleness. They also decided what taxes the colonists should pay. Since they met or assembled to represent the people in Virginia, they were a representative assembly, the first in America.

EARLY AMERICA 

In 1619 Negroes were brought to Jamestown by Dutch slave traders and sold to the plantation owners. The 

Negroes were used to the hot climate of Africa and could stand the heat of Virginia summers better than the white men. At first only a few Negro slaves were brought, but as more and more tobacco was raised, more and more of these African laborers were put to work. Later you will see how the use of the Negro slaves brought on trouble between the North and the South which led to a war in America.

The third important change in Virginia in 1619 was the coming of a shipload of women from England. Each colonist who married one of these women had to pay the company for bringing her over. It was not long before they were all married, and later ships brought more women to Virginia. This made a big change in the colony, for now real homes were started. Men who had been homesick for England settled down and decided to stay in Virginia, for they now had families to support. 

As the years passed the colony grew larger and stronger; the suffering and the starvation of the early years no longer threatened them. 

2. THE CAROLINAS (1670) 

You will remember that Roanoke, Sir Walter Raleigh's colony, was not in what we call Virginia, although he called it Virginia. It was in what later came to be called South Virginia, and still later, Carolina. 

As the Virginia settlements grew and the plantations spread, some of the farmers moved south from Virginia in search of rich lands for planting and broad pastures

SETTLEMENTS TO GAIN WEALTH 

209 for cattle-grazing. They found that tobacco grew well in that soil, and that two crops of corn could be raised in one season, so long was the season and so mild the climate.

The settlers from Virginia had to work hard in the new region. They built rough cabins in the wilderness and lived by farming, hunting, and fishing. Sometimes they traded with the Indians; it is said that they had no laws whatever. Their homes were too far apart for them to enjoy any neighborhood life. 

In this part of the country, later called North Carolina, tobacco was the chief product. The colonists also raised cattle and sold the meat to traders from the West Indies. There was little or no money in the colony, and the people had to use tobacco, pigs, and turpentine to buy what they needed.

As early as 1650 the Virginians had begun to push into this fertile territory. Their nearest neighbors on the south were the Spanish in Florida, but that was a long way off. The King of England claimed the broad strip of land between Virginia and Florida, stretching from the Atlantic to the Pacific.

Charles I, the English King, as we have seen, was not to be trusted, and his people rose up against him. 

Oliver Cromwell and the Roundheads defeated the King in battle and in 1649 had him "put to death by the severinge of his head from his body." You can easily guess the meaning of that. The son of the ill-fated Charles I, also named Charles, fled to France where he remained for eleven years while Cromwell ruled England.

After Cromwell's death, Charles II was called back

EARLY AMERICA 

from France to take the English throne. Although he had little money to spare, he wanted to reward his friends for their loyalty. Thus it happened that he gave to eight of his followers the land between Virginia and Florida. These men were called proprietors, for they were now the owners of the property. 

Charles II had other reasons for giving the land to the eight proprietors besides wishing to reward his loyal followers. He did not want the Spanish or the French to settle in the region and the presence of the English colonists would be likely to keep them away. 

We have said that Charles II had little money. He hoped to build up his fortune by trade with the new colony. He thought Carolina, as it was called, might raise such warm-climate products as silk, wine, and olive oil, and other goods which England then had to buy from foreign trade rivals. But he was mistaken; such products could not be raised there.

Among the eight lords to whom Charles gave the property the best known were the Earl of Clarendon (klǎr'en don), Lord Albemarle (ǎl'bé märl), Sir George Carteret (kär'ter ět), and Sir William Berkeley (bûrk'li).

The King provided for high-sounding titles for these proprietors and for the division of the territory into counties and colonies. The settlers paid rent for their lands, and had a share in their own government. 

The first settlers from England, after Charles the Second's grant of land to the eight proprietors, arrived in Charleston, as they called the place, in 1670. In two years the settlement had 400 colonists, and by 1685 this number had grown to 2500. (See map, page 283.)

SETTLEMENTS TO GAIN WEALTH 

211 1 bay uch the eir

In this southern territory, which became South Carolina several years later, the soil was low and swampy, and seemed just right for the growing of rice. As a result large rice plantations sprang up. Before long many of the planters had fine homes in Charleston as well as homes on their plantations, and had Negro slaves to do the work. The more rice they grew, the more slaves they brought in, and it was not long before there were more 

Negroes than whites in South Carolina.

But rice was not the only product of South Carolina. 

Indigo (in'di gō), from which blue dye is made, was also raised there, and this, too, brought wealth to the colonists. 

A lively trade also sprang up with the West Indies. 

The proprietors welcomed people from other countries than England and of different religious beliefs from that of the Church of England, although they wanted to keep the Catholics out. Puritans from a New England colony in the north, French, German, and Swiss Protestants,

Scotch and Irish came to enjoy the benefits of climate, soil, trade, and government. As in Virginia, there was a representative assembly in the Carolinas, and the people took part in their own government. 

There were troubles with the Indians and the Spaniards, however, and the colonists were not pleased with the way the land was divided nor with the rents they had to pay. They found some of their governors were greedy, dishonest, and eager for too much power. 

Finally the proprietors grew weary of the grumbling and discontent, and gave Carolina back to the King. It was the King who divided the territory into North and South Carolina.

22. HOW THE PILGRIMS TRIED TO FIND FREEDOM OF WORSHIP 1. THE PILGRIMS 

You have already heard of the pilgrims who traveled thousands of miles from their homes in Europe to the Holy Land in the days of the Crusades. 

Four hundred years later another body of Pilgrims (and this time it is spelled with a capital P) traveled a long, long way for religious reasons. These Pilgrims were English people who wanted to worship God in their own way, without interference from the King or his soldiers.

At first they lived in the little town of Scrooby (skrū'bi) in the north-central part of England. From there they went to Holland, and from Holland to America. 

Now you might ask, "Why were these people so hard to please? Why did they not settle down somewhere and be contented ?" We all know that good homes might have been found in England and in Holland - better even than in America at that time. We shall see why the Pilgrims were so restless.

Everybody in England, except the Jews and the Catholics, was expected to belong to the Church of England. 

People were not allowed to have other churches or even to hold religious services of a different kind in their own 214

PILGRIMS AND FREEDOM OF WORSHIP 215 

FO ! 1 homes. The King, too, was strict in enforcing the law. 

He ordered all ministers to use the service of the Church of England without change. He said that he would make the people worship in the Church of England or he would drive them out of the country.

This was a very serious state of affairs. There were some things about the Church of England which the Pilgrims did not like. They wanted to set up a church of their own and have nothing to do with the King's church. 

They said that they had a right to worship God in their own way, using the Bible as their guide. They wanted to separate from the Church of England, and so they 

E were known as Separatists.

In Scrooby they had no church building, but they met in the home of one of their members. This man was 

William Brewster, the village postmaster. Their pastor was the beloved John Robinson, who later went to Holland with them. They had to hold their meetings in secret, of course, because they feared the King's soldiers. 

They did not enjoy this secret worship and the fear of being caught; they wanted to be free and open. But they would not give in to the King; nor would he give in to them.

They now thought of going to some other country. 

They had heard that in Holland people might have their own church and worship God in their own way without interference, and they decided to go there.

They had to be very secret about their plans because the King would not have let them go if he had known of their intention. Some of them had already been arrested and imprisoned because they tried to escape from

EARLY AMERICA 

the country. Others had been thrown into jail, beaten, or even hanged for holding services against the King's orders.

The Pilgrims made their plans secretly and slipped away a few at a time. By the end of the summer of 1608 about one hundred of them had made their escape. 

They remained in Leyden (li'den), a queer old city in Holland, for eleven years. In some ways the Pilgrims were very happy in their Dutch home, with their old friends John Robinson and William Brewster as their leaders. No one disturbed them, but they had troubles of another kind.

Try to put yourself in the place of these poor wanderers. Do you think that you would like to go to a foreign country and stay there for the rest of your life? 

Do you think that you would really be happy? The Pilgrims felt that they were strangers in a strange land. 

They found it very hard to make a living. In England most of them had been simple farmers and gardeners. 

In Holland they had to work as clothmakers, mechanics, and printers. Working indoors was hard for them and their health was suffering. Their children also labored in factories and shops, where they had no chance to grow healthy and strong.

The boys and girls were also learning Dutch ways of living, and they were speaking Dutch instead of English. 

Their parents did not want them to become Dutch. They had been driven out of England, but they still loved their mother country and wanted to remain Englishmen. 

They also wished their children to remain English in every way.

PILGRIMS AND FREEDOM OF WORSHIP 217 

30 fE O୮:' ob el 

DEPARTURE OF THE PILGRIM FATHERS FROM HOLLAND, 1620 

For these reasons, then, the Pilgrims were eager to get away from Holland. But where should they go? They could not go back to England, and they could not make their homes in any other foreign country. Finally they decided to go to the English part of the New World. 

William Brewster was sent back to England to make arrangements for their journey. From the Virginia 

Company the Pilgrims received a large piece of land in America, and several merchants lent them $35,000. By the arrangement the Pilgrims became partners with the merchants of the company, agreeing to work for them for seven years. The merchants were to get back their money from the labor of the colonists, "trade, traffic, trucking, working, fishing, or any other means." The

EARLY AMERICA 

products were to be put into a common storehouse, as in Virginia. Meanwhile, the colonists should have food, clothing, and whatever else they needed from the common storehouse.

When all these things had been settled, the Pilgrims left Holland on board the Speedwell, in the spring of 009 

1620, bound for England. Here another and larger vessel called the Mayflower joined them for the voyage across the Atlantic. 

The Speedwell was not well named. 

Soon after starting, she sprang a leak and was forced to turn back into port. The 

Mayflower, with one hundred and two

THE Mayflower persons, including twenty-one women and twenty-eight children on board, sailed on and, after nine weeks on a stormy sea, reached the bleak New England coast. 

Men were sent ashore in small boats in search of a good place for a settlement. They found that the country was farther to the north than the land given to them by the Virginia Company, but they decided to make a landing anyway. This northern territory was under the con-

trol of the Plymouth Company in England. (See page 199.) 

2. THE FOUNDING OF PLYMOUTH 

The Pilgrims had set out for America with no form of government to follow. Before leaving the Mayflower, therefore, they held a meeting in the cabin, and drew up a plan by which they should be governed. By this plan all the colonists should have equal rights, they should make and obey any laws that were needed, and they should help one another. This plan, which was called the Mayflower Compact, was signed by all the men on board.

Then the Pilgrims landed at a place which they called 

Plymouth, on December 21, 1620. They named it after the seaport in England from which they had sailed many weeks before. (See map, page 239.) 

Four days after landing, on Christmas, they began to build. Trees were cut down to be used in building. A storehouse was put up to hold their supplies. Houses were built for some of the colonists, while others lived on the Mayflower until the next spring.

The colonists' cabins were quickly thrown together. 

The first ones were rude huts, the cracks of which were filled with clay. The floors, too, were of clay. There were no stoves nor furnaces. A fireplace was built in the middle of the room, and the smoke escaped through a hole in the roof.

Windows were simply holes in the walls which were boarded up in stormy weather. Sometimes oiled paper was used, for they had no glass. Many of the people

EARLY AMERICA THE LANDING OF THE PILGRIMS

PILGRIMS AND FREEDOM OF WORSHIP 221 

slept on piles of straw thrown on the floor with logs for pillows. 

The Pilgrims who remained on board the Mayflower during the winter were a little more comfortable than those who lived on land. But the air was stuffy and many of them became sick. Nearly half of the colonists died before spring, Governor John Carver among them, and only four women lived through that first winter. 

But when spring came, the fortunes of the colony took a turn for the better. A fort was built, the lower part of it being fitted up for church services. Streets were laid out. All the men were busy building their own houses and planning their gardens. Land was cleared and corn was planted. ernor.

When Carver died, William Bradford was chosen gov- He served the colony for more than thirty years. 

Governor Bradford's right-hand man was Captain Miles

Standish, the soldier. He was the strong defender of the Plymouth Colony, a man for whom the Indians had great respect.

Miles Standish was small in stature, but he was strong and brave. He never lacked courage. The Indians said of him at first, "He is a small man; let him go to work with the women!" They changed their minds a little later. Standish could look an unfriendly Indian squarely in the eye and tell him what he had to do. On Sunday when the Pilgrims went to church, they always felt safer when Standish was with them.

The Indians muttered and grumbled when they saw the white men taking their hunting grounds, but they seemed unable to do much about it. Sickness broke out among

Some of those who lived thought that the sickness had been sent upon them by the whites because they had killed two white fishermen the year before.

One day the Pilgrims were surprised to receive a visit from an Indian who spoke English. Samoset (săm'óset), for that was his name, had learned the white man's language from English fishermen along the coast. A friend of his, Squanto (skwon'tō), also spoke English. 

These two Indians were kind and helpful to the colonists. They taught the newcomers to grow better corn

THE FIRST THANKSGIVING DAY IN AMERICA 

by the use of fish for fertilizer. Three or four fish in each little hill made the soil richer.

Sometimes treaties, or understandings, were made with the Indians, which helped to keep the peace. One day Massasoit (măs'sá soit'), one of the Indian chiefs who lived near Plymouth, came to the log cabin of Governor Bradford. With him were about twenty warriors, all decked out in paint and feathers. They squatted down on the dirt floor of the cabin and began to smoke the pipe of peace. There in that rude hut a treaty was made which was kept in good faith for more than fifty years.

After the crops of the first year had been gathered, the Pilgrims ordered a time of thanksgiving and prayer -the first Thanksgiving in America. For three days the white men and the Indians, whom they had invited,

EARLY AMERICA 

feasted on deer, fish, and wild turkey, and gave thanks for a plentiful harvest.

In the summer of 1623 the common labor and land plan was given up. The older men of the colony did not like to work in the gangs, and the younger men found that they were no better rewarded for their labor than were those who would not work hard. That year each man received his own piece of land. 

In 1627 the seven-year partnership between the Plymouth colonists and the Virginia Company came to an end. 

By that time the Pilgrims had established a colony, had defended themselves from Indians, had governed themselves, and by their own hard work and wisdom had been able to earn a living. And they had earned enough to pay their debt to the Virginia Company, of which the 

Plymouth Company was a part.

23. HOW THE PURITANS SETTLED IN NEW ENGLAND 1. THE PURITANS 

While the Pilgrims were building homes in Plymouth, their success was being watched with great interest by people in England who were dissatisfied and unhappy. 

Like the Pilgrims, these people wanted religious freedom, but they found it impossible to secure their desire so long as the King kept on breaking up their church meetings and throwing their ministers into prison.

These people were called Puritans. They wanted to "purify" the Church of England and to remain members of it. The Pilgrims had gone farther; they had left the Church of England altogether and were therefore known as Separatists.

The Puritans, like the Pilgrims, were weary of the King and his ways. For many years they had argued with him about government and religion. They felt that they should have something to say about how they were governed. They also wished to make changes in the services of the English Church. But the King wanted things done his way. As a result, thousands of the Puritans at last left their homes in England to build new homes in America.

They chose Massachusetts Bay for their first settle- 226

PURITANS IN NEW ENGLAND 

227 

THE PURITANS' FAREWELL 

Leaving their friends and relatives on shore, the Puritans set out in a small boat for the little sailing vessel which brought them to America. ment. Already a few adventurers and traders had visited its shores. In 1623 a group known as the Dorchester Adventurers had founded a fishing station at Cape Ann. There were only about fifty or sixty in this 

I little colony; the inhabitants made their living by fishing, hunting, fur-trading, farming, and raising cattle. 

John Endicott was their first governor. Under his leadership in 1628 some of them had settled at Salem (sā'lem), which means peace. There was a friendly feeling between this colony and the Plymouth Colony, and when many of the Salem settlers fell sick their Plymouth neighbors came over to help them. With the exception

EARLY AMERICA 

of these tiny settlements - Cape Ann and Salem-the 

Massachusetts Bay territory was a wilderness. 

2. MASSACHUSETTS BAY COLONY 

Meanwhile a number of wealthy English Puritans, who called themselves the Massachusetts Bay Company, received a charter from the King, allowing them to land in the New World and found a colony north of the Plymouth settlement. Almost all the members of the company came to America in 1630. For the first time a company itself formed a colony. 

Most of the leaders of the Puritans were rich and well-educated, and they did not have the difficulties of the poor Pilgrims who had founded Plymouth Colony.

It was not hard for them to get together seventeen ships and a thousand people, together with food, clothing, two hundred and forty cows, sixty horses, gunpowder, furniture, and other needed supplies. They took their charter with them.

They elected John Winthrop their first Governor. He was one of the best and wisest of men. Although against the Church of England, he was a very religious man. 

Part of his plan in coming to America was to bring 

Christianity to the Indians.

He did not like the way people lived in England. He said that they ate and drank too much and enjoyed too many luxuries. He wanted a purer and simpler life. 

He thought his people would live better lives in America than if they remained in England.

He also knew that England had too many people for so small a country, that the cloth business was not doing

PURITANS IN NEW ENGLAND 

229 well, that many people were out of work and would be better off in America. He saw clearly, however, that this change of homes would bring "some pinch to them at first," and they felt the pinch of hunger and cold before the first winter was over and for several years plater. ma In the summer of 1630 John Winthrop and a thousand of his fellow-Puritans landed in the harbor of 

Salem. They put up some tents and built a few log houses. When a number of the new colonists died, Winthrop was afraid that the water in Salem was not pure, and he decided to look somewhere else for a place to settle, finally choosing land along the tcoast to the south. Here he founded Boston. 

The first winter in Boston was very hard. The weather was terribly cold. 

A driving blizzard struck

Hungry, cold, and homesick for friends in England.

More and more settlers continued to come from England, and by the end of the first winter several new towns were formed near by-among them Roxbury, Watertown, and Dorchester. (See map, page 239.)

The Indians were friendly and helped the colonists in many ways. They brought corn and beans and traded them to the white men for knives and trinkets.

In spite of cold and hunger the colony grew. When it was four years old, Governor Winthrop said in a letter to a friend that there were four thousand persons in 

Massachusetts Bay. By this time there were sixteen towns.

The colony made very strict rules for its government. Governor Winthrop and the other leaders said that no man should vote who was not a church member. 

This meant, of course, a member of the Puritan Church. 

Some of the people did not like this, for they had come to America not for religious freedom, but because they thought they could improve their way of living. 

At first all the members of the company had a direct share in the government, but as the colony grew and spread other persons were also given a voice in the way the colony was ruled. After the settlements spread, it was decided to send men to Boston to represent the settlers. This was the beginning of the first representative assembly in Massachusetts.

During the first ten years (1630-1640) about twenty thousand Puritans left England to make their homes in

Massachusetts Bay Colony. More than twenty towns were started and were doing well, but Boston grew to be the greatest of them all. In sixty or seventy years Massachusetts Bay and Plymouth joined to form one colony, and both were called Massachusetts.
