The Puritans settled in Boston in 1630. 

They had more money than the Pilgrims had had, and their colony grew much more rapidly. 

After sixty or seventy years Plymouth and Massachusetts Bay joined together and were called Massachusetts.

24.  BAY COLONY 1. RELIGIOUS FREEDOM AND RHODE ISLAND 

The Pilgrim and Puritan colonies were the humble beginnings of the great State of Massachusetts. They were founded to give the people religious freedom which they could not enjoy in England. 

But the Puritans in Massachusetts insisted that everybody be a Puritan. They had come over from England to have their own church and they would not allow any other church to be started in the colony. They said that people who did not like the ideas of the Puritans did not belong in the colony and should go elsewhere. 

Laws were passed so that those who did not go to the Puritan Church or spoke against its ideas were fined or put in prison. Besides this, the Puritan Church was supported by taxes collected from all the colonists. 

Many of the settlers thought that this was not fair. 

A young man named Roger Williams was pastor of the church at Salem. He was educated in college in England. Some of the things he believed and preached were very disturbing to the Puritans. He declared that the government had power only over men's bodies and property, not over their thoughts and consciences. He also said that the King had no right to give the land of the 232

Indians to the colonists; that the Indians were the true owners of the land and the settlers should buy it from them.

These ideas seemed so dangerous that the Pilgrims thought it best to send Roger Williams back to England. 

Learning of their plan, he bade his wife and children goodbye, wrapped himself in his cloak, and fled under cover of darkness into the wilderness in the midst of winter.

Williams was always friendly with the Indians, visitting them in their wigwams and helping them whenever he could. Thus he learned to speak the Indian language and he taught the savages to speak English. They liked him and were always glad to have him come for a visit. 

When he was driven from Massachusetts, he made his way through the wilderness, fighting snow and hunger, and sought out the wigwam of Massasoit. He had taken fourteen weeks to travel the seventy or eighty miles from Boston, a distance which can now be covered by automobile in about two hours. 

With five friends Williams now began the settlement of Providence (prov'i dĕns), in 1636. He named it 

Providence, for he believed God had watched over him and brought him there safely. He and his companions bought land from the Indians and drew up an agreement somewhat like the Mayflower Compact. Their government did not come from the King, but was free and independent, like that of the Plymouth Colony.

Another Massachusetts colonist, whose teachings were thought to be dangerous, was Mrs. Anne Hutchinson. 

She was a proud, restless, active woman with a very

EARLY AMERICA 

Hassan

ROGER WILLIAMS WELCOMES MRS. HUTCHINSON TO RHODE ISLAND 

unusual mind. During the week she used to hold meetings in her own home, at which she would comment on what the minister had said in his Sunday sermon. But that was not all; she often found fault with the sermons and gave opinions of her own. She had about a hundred followers who believed she was right and tried to get others to go to her meetings.

Governor Winthrop, who lived just across the street from Mrs. Hutchinson, was much disturbed by the power she had over many people in the colony. He and other 

Puritans thought it best for her to leave Boston at once, and she was driven out as "a woman not fit for our society."

FERCE

Mrs. Hutchinson and several of her friends moved to a place they called Portsmouth (pôrts'muth), on Narragansett (năr'á gănʼsĕt) Bay, not very far from Providence. (See map, page 239.)

Other small settlements were soon started in the neighboring wilderness. Roger Williams had invited all people who had trouble in other colonies to come to this place where they could enjoy freedom of worship. 

There were no religious tests for the right to vote and to hold office. The settlers were not punished for their religious beliefs. The Indians, who sold them the land, remained at peace with them.

As the years passed, the settlers in Rhode Island decided to ask the King of England for the right to have their colony. They knew that it was not enough to have bought the land from the Indians, for the King claimed all that part of America and would expect to have something to say about it.

In 1643 they sent Roger Williams to England to get the King's permission for the three towns of Providence, Portsmouth, and Newport to rule themselves and others in their territory by whatever form of government seemed best to them. The King agreed. Twenty years later these towns together received a charter from the King as "Rhode Island and the Providence Plantations."

2. How CONNECTICUT WAS FOUNDED 

As the good lands in and near Boston were taken up, some of the settlers moved into other territory. They thought that if they went away where they could find better land, they could make a better living. Besides, many of them were growing restless and displeased with the kind of government they had in Massachusetts. 

There was too little freedom for them in the Puritan colony. 

About five or six years after Governor Winthrop came to Boston, some of his colonists moved west to the valley of the Connecticut (kon nět'i kŭt) River. They had heard that the soil in this region was much better and

THOMAS HOOKER AND HIS FRIENDS GIVE THANKS FOR A SAFE JOURNEY TO CONNECTICUT 

more fertile than that along the sandy shores of Cape Cod. 

Thomas Hooker, a minister in Newtown, now called

Cambridge, was the chief leader among the colonists from Massachusetts who moved to Connecticut. With a hundred of his church members he trudged over a hundred miles, through forests so dense that they often had to take their axes to chop a passageway for their oxen and their wagons. The journey required two weeks of hard travel before they reached the Connecticut valley. 

The colonists from Massachusetts Bay were not the only people to seek a new life in Connecticut. A few 

Plymouth settlers had explored and traded with the Indians there for three years. Dutch traders, too, had

EARLY AMERICA 

found their way into Connecticut and had built some small forts and a trading post where they bought furs from the Indians.

In this way Hartford and Wethersfield were started. 

If a census, or count of the people, had been taken in Connecticut after the third town, Windsor, was settled, it would have shown eight hundred colonists that first year.

The Connecticut settlers had no charter and therefore had no lawful or legal right to the region. Some of them bought land from the Indians, but most of them took what they wanted. For two years they were governed by Massachusetts, but in 1638 they decided to have their own government.

You all know what a foundation is for a house or a building; the colonists in Connecticut did, too. They wanted a good foundation for their government. So it came about that they drew up a set of papers which they called the Fundamental Orders of Connecticut. If you remember that the words fundamental and foundation belong to the same word family, you will understand what this means.

By the "Fundamental Orders" the people were to elect a governor and six assistants and four representatives from each town, to make the laws, to decide what taxes should be collected, and to dispose of the land. 

In this way Connecticut became the first colony in America to have a constitution, for that is what the 

Fundamental Orders might be called. You will learn more about constitutions as you go more deeply into the study of history.

Davenport (dăv'ĕnpôrt) was the leader of the New Haven colonists. They agreed to make their laws like the laws of the Bible. 

They declared that "the worde of God shall be the only rule to be attended unto in ordering the affaires of the commonwealth." That is how New Haven came to be called the Bible Commonwealth.

3. MAINE AND NEW HAMPSHIRE 

As early as 1603 France and England were rivals for the Maine region, which was not far from the French settlements in Canada. The King of France granted land in Maine to a Frenchman for fur-trading. 

A few years later the English King's grant to the Plymouth Company covered the same region. You remember the colony started by the Plymouth Company at the mouth of the Kennebec River the same year the 

London Company founded Jamestown (see page 201).

The leaders of the Maine colony were Gorges (gôr'jěz) and Popham (pop'åm). They made a brave attempt to succeed, but sickness and attacks by the Indians caused many deaths among the colonists the first winter. In writing about their suffering, Gorges said, "Our hopes were frozen to death," and the colony could not escape "the malice of the Divell." Thus the first English settlement in Maine proved a failure.

Captain John Smith of Virginia explored the coast of all the region in 1614, made a map of the coast, and called the territory New England. He did some lively trading with the Indians while he was there and took away fish and furs worth about $75,000. After his expedition there were a few fishing and trading stations in Maine, but they were small, scattered, and unimportant.

Then, in 1631, Gorges and his friend John Mason received a grant of land from the King of England, and another attempt was made to establish a colony. Fifty men were brought in to fish and farm along the Piscataqua (pis ká tä’kwá) River. This was the beginning of 

New Hampshire.

Another settlement along this same river was started at Exeter (ěks'ě těr) by thirty-five men, under the leadership of the Reverend John Wheelwright. This minister had been driven from Boston because his religious teachings ruffled the peace of the Puritans. 

Since Massachusetts considered these neighboring settlements a part of herself, she took over Mason's New 

Hampshire in 1641 and Gorges' Maine in 1652. To gether for many years they all formed the powerful New 

England colony of Massachusetts. 

1. Make a list of the people named in the story of Maine and 

New Hampshire, and write a sentence about each. 

2. Draw a simple map. On it show the different settlements in Maine and New Hampshire about which you have just read. 

Be sure to show the Kennebec and the Piscataqua Rivers. 

Maine and New Hampshire were once a part of Massachusetts.

25. MARYLAND AND THE CATHOLICS 1. LORD BALTIMORE AND HIS PLAN 

In England, you will remember, while Charles I was King, all Englishmen were expected to attend the services of the Church of England. To escape this requirement the Pilgrims and the Puritans fled to America where they could enjoy religious freedom, but the Catholics, who seemed to have no place to go, remained in England and suffered. No welcome awaited them among the Puritans of Massachusetts Bay, and in Virginia the colonists wanted only people who were members of the 

Church of England. 

The fines for not attending the Church of England were so heavy that many Catholics were unable to pay them. They were sorely punished for their religious beliefs, but they would not change them. Something had to be done for the persecuted Catholics. 

Sir George Calvert, the first Lord Baltimore, was a

Catholic of wealth and education. He wanted to found a colony in America. As a member of the London Company, and as one who had tried to found a colony in Newfoundland without success in 1623, he was keenly interested in establishing a refuge for his fellow Catholics in America. He was a good friend of the King, and in return for many favors the King gave him a tract of 242

MARYLAND AND THE CATHOLICS 

243 ።

From a painting by Leutze.

THE LANDING OF LORD BALTIMORE 

es -1 land in the New World. He received the grant in 1632, but died before he could realize his hopes. 

Cecilius (sē sil'yus) Calvert, his son, who was the second Lord Baltimore, then received the land grant which had been made to his father. He, too, was eager to provide a place where persecuted Catholics might go and find religious freedom. He was willing, if necessary, to spend a large part of his fortune to establish such a colony. Before his hopes were accomplished he is said to have spent $200,000 on the plan during the first two years alone.

Lord Baltimore himself did not come to America, but sent his brother Leonard (len'árd) in his place. Two ships, the Ark and the Dove, with more than two hundred

EARLY AMERICA 

English families and their household goods, tools, and food supplies, left England in 1633, bound for America. 

Among them were twenty Catholic "gentlemen," two Jesuit priests, and a great many Protestant laborers. 

2. THE FOUNDING OF MARYLAND 

Near the mouth of the Potomac (pō tō'măk) River the colonists made their settlement in the winter of 1634, calling the place St. Mary's, out of respect for the Virgin 

Mary. The land itself they named Maryland, after the wife of Charles I, Henrietta

Maria, who was a French princess and a Catholic.

From the beginning there were more Protestants than Catholics in Maryland, but the colonists lived and worked happily together, with few quarrels. Lord 

Baltimore did not allow anyone

EARLY SETTLEMENTS IN AND to attack the religion of others. 

He gave full religious freedom to all Christians, and welcomed Puritans, Quakers, members of the Church of England, and others who believed in Christ. You will notice that he kept out Jews and people who had no religion who might have wished to come to the colony. 

Upon reaching St. Mary's, the colonists found the land already cleared for planting. You can imagine what a great help this must have been. Father White, one of the Jesuit priests, wrote: "We bought from the King of the Indians thirty miles of that land, delivering in ex-

MARYLAND AND THE CATHOLICS 

245 change axes, rakes, and several yards of cloth." The colony made a good start. 

The whole town of St. Mary's was carefully planned before the colonists began to build their homes. At first some of the settlers lived in the wigwams of the Indians, and Father White had an 

Indian hut for his Catholic chapel.

The Indians from whom they bought the land were friendly. Some of them became Christians. They not only worked with the newcomers, but also furnished them wild turkeys and other food. The Indian women, who were called squaws (skwôz), taught the white women. how to plant corn and how 

ST. MARY'S, MARYLAND 

school in America. to make corn bread. In This was the first English Catholic 

Maryland the settlers lived at peace with the Indians for many years. 

The King had given Lord Baltimore not only the land in Maryland, but also the right to rule the colony, to tax the people, and to have his own court to try all lawbreakers. The colonists, however, were to be allowed to help make the laws. Thus Maryland, too, had a representative assembly.

It was hoped, at first, that large estates would be formed. An "adventurer" who took five strong, healthy

EARLY AMERICA 

men to Maryland and supplied them with whatever they needed in the new country was made "lord of a manor" (măn'ôr) of a thousand acres. He paid a small rent each year to Lord Baltimore for this estate. The five men, for their part, worked as servants for four or five years for the lord of the manor. At the end of that time they each received their freedom and fifty acres of land of their own.

When it was seen that the colony could not grow very quickly under this plan, a new arrangement was tried. 

Each free man (that is, each man not a servant) who paid for his fare on the ship and for his necessary outfit, which cost him about one hundred dollars, received one hundred acres of land for himself, a hundred acres for his wife, and other hundreds for each of his servants, whose fares of course he would also have to pay. Each colonist was to pay rent, usually in wheat, to Lord Baltimore. Can you see how much better this plan was than that first used in Virginia and Plymouth?

Maryland was a successful colony from the very beginning. The settlers knew how to work and they were willing to spend long hours in planting and building. 

They raised so much corn the first season that they were able to sell some of it to New England in exchange for salt codfish. It was not long before they were sending corn and tobacco to England. 

In a few years the Maryland colonists were living on broad plantations like those in Virginia. The climate and the soil of the two colonies were much alike, for they were next-door neighbors. There should have been peace and friendliness between these neighbors, but un-

MARYLAND AND THE CATHOLICS 

247 fortunately a quarrel kept them stirred up for several years.

Both Virginia and Maryland claimed Kent Island in Chesapeake (chěs'á pēk) Bay. The island had been bought from the Indians by William Claiborne (klā'bûrn) of Virginia, who had a trading post there. The 

King of England had given him the right to the fur trade all along that coast. Lord Baltimore's grant of land also included Kent Island, which shows how careless the King was about important things like boundary lines in those days. Finally Lord Baltimore won his claim and 

Kent Island was declared a part of Maryland. 

In 1642 the people of England were at war with one another. Some of them, as we have seen, were on the side of the King, and some on the side of Parliament. 

At that time many Puritans, who had settled in Virginia, moved into Maryland. Lord Baltimore insisted that all in his colony be treated alike. The Governor of Maryland, William Stone, who was a Protestant, influenced the legislature not to punish anyone because of his religious beliefs. This feeling of fairness toward all people is called "tolerance." The "Toleration Act" of 

1649 thus became the first law in America to provide religious freedom.

After 1688 the people of Maryland drove out Lord Baltimore and changed the law, so that the rights of Catholics were taken away. They could no longer vote or hold office, buy land, or teach school. They were not allowed to hold church services except in their homes. This was a big disappointment to the Baltimores, for they had always clung to the idea of religious freedom.

EARLY AMERICA 

Maryland kept on growing, although only one or two towns were established. The colony consisted mainly of large plantations and small farms, producing corn, tobacco, and wheat. Many of the planters became rich. 

They had no such difficulties as the settlers in the other colonies and they lived at peace with the Indians.

of M 26. PENNSYLVANIA AND THE QUAKERS 1. WILLIAM PENN 

In England, as we have seen, what was called the "Established Church" was the Church of England. It was the King's church and was supported by law. Many of the English did not wish to belong to this church; they liked simpler services better. 

None of the groups of objectors to the King's church is more interesting than the "Society of Friends." 

Their very name tells us something of what they were like. The Friends were sometimes called "Quakers."

It is said that one of them was brought before an English judge for disturbing the peace. The man held his head high, and told the judge that he should "tremble before the Lord." The judge did not tremble or quake, but after that the Friends were often called "Quakers." 

The first leader of the Quakers was George Fox, an

English shoemaker, who was sent to prison for his religious teachings. After he got out of prison, he and his fellow Quakers went about the country preaching and doing many new and strange things. 

They refused to take off their hats in the presence of ministers, or judges, or other high officers, or even in the presence of the King. They taught that all men were equal in the sight of God, and that God loved them all 249

EARLY AMERICA 

alike. Why, then, should they not all be equal in the sight of men?

They called war a sin in which no Christian should take part, either by becoming a soldier or by paying 

A QUAKER TRIAL 

taxes for the support of an army. They said that they would not kill their fellow men. 

Now it must be said that the Quakers were very good people, peaceful, quiet, and usually very orderly. 

They loved their fellow men. They would not fight and kill people.

They were against imprisonment for debt when hundreds of the poor were dying in jail because they could not pay what they owed.

The Quakers would not take off their hats They preached against before the judges in court. idleness, waste, and lies. They were fair and honest, and wanted all men to live good lives.

In the Quaker church there were no paid ministers. 

Their religious services were simple and their church buildings were plain. They met in the church in silence so that they might hear God's voice. They believed in

PENNSYLVANIA AND THE QUAKERS 251 

the "inner light," or conscience. They believed that the "inner light" would show them how to understand the

Bible and would guide them in their daily life, in their laws, and in their government. 

It was, indeed, a good thing for America that many of the Quakers came as settlers in the early days. 

One of the strongest leaders in the colonies was William Penn, who has been called "the father of Pennsylvania." He was the son of a great sea-fighter who was an admiral in the British navy.

While William Penn was a college boy at Oxford, he became interested in the Quakers and their beliefs, and attended several of their services. For this he was sent home from college to face his angry father, who had no love for the Quakers.

The Admiral then sent his son to Ireland to manage his estate there. While in Ireland he heard another 

Quaker sermon, and from that day to the end of his life 

William Penn was a Quaker.

When he came home, he refused to take off his hat in his father's presence. He called his father "thee" and "thou," as the Quakers did, and was again turned out of his home.

But he did not give up his belief. He became a preacher at Quaker meetings and soon the officers of the law put him in prison for what he wrote and said on religion.

In 1680 he gave the King a list of the sufferings of the Quakers. This showed that ten thousand of them had been held in prison and more than two hundred had died from cruel treatment. Their lands had been taken away

EARLY AMERICA 

from them by the English government, and the payment of heavy fines had brought many of them hardship and suffering.

After his father died, William inherited his estate and became a rich man. This did not spoil him nor make him lose interest in the sufferings of the Quakers. He now wished more than ever to do something for them. 

He planned to find homes for them in America, where they might have religious freedom and live in peace. 

This is exactly what the Pilgrims had done.

King Charles II owed Ad miral Penn about $80,000, and this debt was now due to 

William, the Admiral's son.

The King was very wasteful, and there was little chance that he would ever pay this debt to Penn. Then Penn asked the King if he would pay the debt in land. "Certainly," replied Charles, "you may have land in America." 

WILLIAM PENN 

Thus William Penn became the proprietor, or owner, of a vast territory in America. All the laws he made for the region, however, had to be approved by the King, who claimed all the land in America. As the land cost the King nothing, he was willing to give Penn a very large tract. The grant lay north of Maryland and south of New York, and stretched away toward the west for

PENNSYLVANIA AND THE QUAKERS 

253 three hundred miles. It was later found to contain 

45,000 square miles. (See map, page 258.)

Penn named this land Sylvania (sil va'ni á), meaning woodland, because its hills and plains were covered with forests. But the King called it Pennsylvania, in honor of Admiral Penn, he said. William feared that people might think him vain for allowing his name to be so honored.

In 1681 Penn advertised for colonists to go to his new land. He wanted hard-working farmers, carpenters, stone masons, weavers, shoemakers, and other mechanics who wished to live under a fair government among plain, peace-loving people.

A few Dutch and Swedish fur traders were already living on Penn's territory, but he did not try to drive them out. He thought there was plenty of room for all comers. 

2. PENN VISITS AMERICA 

In 1682 Penn left England to join the colony, taking with him about a hundred of his friends and neighbors, mostly Quakers. They sailed on the good ship Welcome. 

On reaching America they went up the Delaware River to the mouth of the Schuylkill (skool'kil), where Penn found a town that had already been laid out by some of his colonists who had come to America a year before. 

This town he named Philadelphia (fil'á děl'fi á), which is made up of two Greek words meaning brotherly love.

He chose this name for the town to show that he wished his people to live together in love and helpfulness toward one another. Philadelphia is also called the "Quaker

EARLY AMERICA 

City" since Penn and its first settlers were mostly Quakers.

Penn was delighted with his new home. The soil was fertile and well-watered; the climate was mild; and the low, level land was covered with forests. In order to build their houses the people had to cut down great trees of oak and elm, chestnut and walnut, spruce and pine. 

Penn planned the streets of Philadelphia like a checker board. The cross streets were numbered in plain Quaker style, "First," "Second," "Third," and so on, as the Quakers also call the days of the week. The streets crossing the numbered ones were given the names of trees-chestnut, spruce, walnut, pine, oak, and the like.

The Pennsylvania colonists were careful, hard-working people. They began to build homes at once, but some of them had to live in caves and huts till their own dwellings were ready. By summer eighty houses had been built and three hundred farms were ready for the first crops. Supplies brought from England kept the settlers from being hungry until their crops grew. 

Through their general assembly, or legislature, the Pennsylvania settlers made laws forbidding horse-racing, alehouses, gambling, card games, stage plays, and duels. 

They provided that their children should be taught useful trades in their schools and that they should also learn something about the laws. They made laws about caring for the poor and teaching the prisoners how to earn an honest living. There were no laws to provide for forts, an army, or religion. Can you tell why?

By 1685 Pennsylvania had eight thousand colonists,

PENNSYLVANIA AND THE QUAKERS 

255 π. half of whom were English. The rest were Dutch and 

Germans, for the most part. By that time Philadelphia had nearly four hundred houses and was the largest town in any of the American colonies, and continued to be till 1810. Most of the settlers lived on farms, as the land was plentiful and cheap.

Eager for his colony to grow, Penn advertised in Germany and other countries for settlers. As he promised religious freedom to all, many Lutherans, members of the Reformed Church, and Mennonites 

Mennonites (měnʼonīts) flocked into the colony from Germany. The Mennonites believed very much as the Quakers did. Welsh Quakers were also attracted to Pennsylvania from Wales, just west of England. In time there were Irish and Scotch people in Pennsylvania, as well as English, German, Welsh, Dutch, and Swedish. No other colony in America had settlers of so many kinds of religious belief. You may be sure that Penn was glad to welcome them all to his colony. 

3. PENN AND THE INDIANS 

The Indians, too, were treated well by William Penn. 

He believed in the Golden Rule, and tried to do unto the Indians as he would have had them do unto him. In this way he made the Indians friendly toward the newcomers.

When he arranged for a meeting with the red men, the tribes came swarming through the woods and laid down their bows and arrows at the feet of the peaceful Quaker.

Although Penn had received the land from the King of

EARLY AMERICA 

Copyright by M. G. Abbey;

from a Copley Print Copyright by Curtis & Cameron, Publishers, Boston. 

PENN'S TREATY WITH THE INDIANS

PENNSYLVANIA AND THE QUAKERS 

257 England, he was willing to pay the Indians, too. This he did as he smoked the peace pipe with them under the shade of a great elm tree. He gave them beads, as well as knives, axes, saws, and other things that would help them to build houses and aid them to live a more settled life.

There under the old elm Penn and the Indians promised friendship to one another and agreed to be brothers as if they were "of one flesh and blood." Penn said to the Indians: "We meet on the broad pathway of faith and good will; no advantage shall be taken on either side, but all shall be openness and love." (Can you put this into your own words?) 

One of the Indian chiefs replied: "We will live in love with William Penn and his children as long as the sun and moon shall endure."

This promise was kept; not a drop of Quaker blood in those days was ever shed by an Indian. 

Long after Penn died, there were Indian troubles in Pennsylvania, because the people had forgotten the promise of friendship which he had made. But Penn's famous treaty has always been remembered. 

4. MASON AND DIXON'S LINE 

Although Penn was at peace with the Indians, he had trouble with his white neighbors in Maryland about the boundary line. As we have seen, English kings sometimes gave two people grants of land that overlapped. 

The quarrel was not settled until the King sent two men from England to draw a boundary line between Maryland and Pennsylvania. Since their names were Mason

EARLY AMERICA 

and Dixon, the boundary was called Mason and Dixon's Line. Many years later, when the States of the North and the South were in a quarrel over slavery this line was part of the boundary between the slave States and the free States. 

When Penn saw the need of a stretch of seacoast, he bought land along the Delaware Bay, already settled by 

Dutch and Swedish traders. This land

Penn had wanted to prove that a colony could have religious freedom and be successful in other ways at the same time. He had called the colony his "Holy Experiment." The experiment proved that Penn was right. From the very first it was successful; its people were steady workers who lived good lives. They made wise laws and obeyed them. 

In time Penn's "home for the new faith" grew into the great State of Pennsylvania.

27. HOW THE DUTCH SETTLED A COLONY WHICH THE ENGLISH CONQUERED 1. WHAT HENRY HUDSON DISCOVERED 

Over in western Europe, on the shores of the North Sea, is a little country called Holland. In this land the 

Pilgrims lived for a short time, you remember, before coming to America.

Holland is sometimes called the Netherlands, which means low lands. Much of the land is lower than the sea, and the water is kept from overflowing the fields by means of dikes or dams. 

In early days when America was being settled, the Dutch (for that is what the people of Holland are called) were great sailors. Many of them were wealthy, too. 

They had made money by trade and by manufactures. 

These wide-awake people wanted to share the riches of the New World. They also clung to the idea that there must be a short route to the East-perhaps around the northern part of Europe, or perhaps northwest around the northern part of North America.

In 1609, only two years after the settlement of Virginia, an English sea captain, Henry Hudson by name, offered his services to the Dutch West India Company. 

In a small Dutch ship, the Half Moon, Hudson and a crew of sixteen or eighteen men tried to find a northwest 260

The ! 

20

THE LANDING OF HENRY HUDSON 

passage from the Atlantic to the Pacific Ocean. You remember that the Cabots also hoped to find such a route more than a hundred years earlier, and failed. 

Henry Hudson failed, too. He explored the coast of 

North America between Newfoundland and Virginia, sailing into bays and rivers, always hoping to come upon a northwest route. At last the Half Moon slipped past a hook of sand into what is New York harbor. After going some distance beyond this point, now called Sandy Hook, Hudson discovered the great river to which his name has been given.

Thinking that this river might be the long-looked-for northwest passage, Hudson turned his ship into it and sailed north. About one hundred and fifty miles from the sea the river became so narrow that the Half Moon

EARLY AMERICA 

could go no farther. Some small boats were lowered, and sailors went ahead to see what the river was like farther north. They brought back a disappointing report; the stream grew smaller and smaller; it could not be the northwest passage after all.

There was nothing to do but to turn back. Hudson

Hudson and his men

This friendly exchange led to a profitable fur trade for the Dutch in America, and out of it grew the Dutch colony in the valley of the Hudson River. 

2. EARLY LIFE IN NEW NETHERLAND 

The Dutch did not come at once to settle in New Netherland. The first people who came arrived in 1614, interested only in the fur trade and the riches they could

take back to Holland in a short time. The fur traders were rough men for the most part, strong and fearless, and fond of drinking liquor. They shared their "fire water," as the Indians called it, with the thirsty savages from whom they bought their furs. 

Unwilling to spend time and money on building houses which they would leave when they returned to Holland, the traders were content with rude huts and shelters. 

Their trading kept them moving from one place to another.

Some of them sailed up the Hudson River and built a fort where Albany is now. This they called Fort Orange. Others went south and built a second Fort Nassau on the Delaware, about where Camden, New Jersey, is I now located. Still others went over to Long Island. 

Some went east to Connecticut, where they were found by Hooker and his friends who had come from Massachusetts Bay to found a colony of their own. 

It was soon clear to the members of the Dutch West India Company that a permanent colony could not grow out of such scattered groups of fur traders. New Netherland, with its rich soil and fine forests, offered a good living to farmers, but so long as fur trading was so profitable, farm work did not attract the newcomers. 

3. THE PATROON SYSTEM 

A new plan for settlement was then tried out, providing for a colony of farms. The Dutch West India Company promised that any man who paid the traveling expenses to America of fifty adults (over fifteen years of age) should receive a large tract of land extending six-

EARLY AMERICA 

teen miles along the Hudson River and as far back as he wished. If he preferred eight miles on each side of the river, the company would permit that. He had to provide his settlers with farm land on the tract, and with houses, tools, cows, and horses. As his position was like that of the father of a family, he was called a patroon (på troon'). The word patroon comes from the Latin word pater (pä'těr) which means father.

The settlers were to pay land rent to the patroon and work for him for ten years. Then they were to be free to become independent farmers. They were required to take their grain to the patroon's mill for grinding.

When they had farm products to sell, they had to offer it first to the patroon. If they wanted to hunt or fish, they had to ask his permission. But not even the patroon could allow them to engage in fur trading, for the company had forbidden that. 

The patroon system was meant to encourage farming alone. 

Free colonists, able to pay their own way over to America from Holland, received from the company all the land they could take care of, and for ten years were not required to pay any taxes.

The settlers brought over by the patroons saw the success of the free farmers and the fur traders. They thought that they themselves were being unfairly treated, and became dissatisfied. Quarrels were frequent among the settlers, and between them and the patroons. 

At last the company saw that the patroon system had failed, and it was given up. It had been the means, however, of bringing many settlers to New Netherlands, even though it had not been a success.

SHE THE INDIANS SELL MANHATTAN ISLAND TO THE DUTCH 4. THE PURCHASE OF MANHATTAN ISLAND 

New York City, which was once called New Amsterdam (ăm'ster dăm') by the Dutch, is now one of the great cities of the world. It stands on the island of Manhattan. In 1626 Peter Minuit (min'u it), one of the early governors, bought the whole island from the Indians for twenty-four dollars' worth of trinkets. This was at the rate of ten acres for one cent.

The 

The Dutch at first lived on the south end of Manhattan. 

In order to protect themselves from the Indians, they built a wall at the north end of their settlement. street which runs where this wall used to be is now called Wall Street. It is a short street, but is one of the best known in the world. It is a center of great banking houses. Through an opening or gateway in the old Dutch wall, the people could go out into a broad highway when

EARLY AMERICA 

it was safe to do so. This open passage toward the north is now known as Broadway, one of the important streets of New York City. 

5. PETER STUYVESANT, THE LAST DUTCH GOVERNOR 

While New Netherland was a Dutch colony (16141664), a very interesting man among its rulers was Peter Stuyvesant (sti'vě sănt), the last of the Dutch governors. 

Stuyvesant was proud and gruff. Many of the colonists did not like him. They made fun of his big nose and his small eyes. But they had to admit that he was a brave old soldier. He wanted his own way and he enjoyed giving orders to others. He had lost a leg in battle with the Portuguese, and after that he had had a wooden leg.

Stuyvesant came to New Netherland to rule like a king. 

He said that he was going to be a father to his children. 

But he was ruling over people who were grown up, and he was so foolish as to allow them no voice in their own government. 

Old Peter used to say: "This is what I think. If any one has any objection, let him speak out." Then if anyone dared to speak or to object, Stuyvesant would fly into a rage, stamp his wooden leg on the floor, and use very harsh words.

When the Quakers came into the colony, Stuyvesant refused to allow them to hold meetings. He also fined and punished them, sometimes whipping them upon their bare backs.

Was it any wonder that Stuyvesant had few friends among the people? That was one reason the English

6. NEW NETHERLAND BECOMES NEW YORK 

From the very beginning the English had said that New Netherland belonged to them. The Cabots had explored the eastern coast of North America, and for more than a hundred years England had claimed all that part of the New World. They warned the Dutch to keep away, for they wanted the valuable fur trade for themselves. 

The Dutch were stubborn, however, and would not leave their little settlements. 

New Netherland lay between the English colonies in

New England and those to the south. Charles II did not want his colonies to be separated by a group of Dutch posts and farms. Thus it came about that in 1664 he sent a fleet of four ships and about four hundred men commanded by his brother, the Duke of York, to demand the surrender of the Dutch colony. The English promised that the Dutch should be treated fairly if they would give up without fighting. Governor Stuyvesant raged and refused to surrender, but when the Dutch heard that they would be well treated they refused to stand by their governor and Stuyvesant had to give up New Amsterdam without a struggle. The Dutch flag was hauled down and the English flag took its place. 

Thus New Netherland failed to be a permanent Dutch colony, but became an English colony named New York after the Duke of York. The Dutch had given their attention largely to the profits from fur trading. Homebuilding seemed to them less important than getting rich

Th 

PETER STUYVESANT STANDS HIS GROUND 

and going back to Holland. The company had treated both the settlers and the Indians unfairly. As a result, the colony was a hopeless tangle of dissatisfied Dutch and raiding Indians. By the failure of the Dutch and their surrender to the English, the colonies in the north and south were linked together under the King of England. 

Today in many of our States there are people who can trace their families back to the early Dutch settlers who came to New York, New Jersey, and Delaware. These

Dutch families have helped to establish schools, colleges, and churches. They have helped to build up America and to make our country what it is. President Franklin D. 

Roosevelt comes from one of these early Dutch families, and President Martin Van Buren came from another.

28. HOW THE SWEDISH COLONIES WERE TAKEN BY THE ENGLISH 1. FUR TRADERS IN NEW SWEDEN 

While the Dutch were settling New Netherland and the English were making homes in other colonies, the 

Swedes did not intend to be left out. King Gustavus

Adolphus (gus tä'vus á dŏl'fus) wanted the Swedes to have a share in America's riches, too. 

The Swedish West India Company was therefore formed, and in 1638 fifty Swedish settlers came to America to engage in fur trading. They chose a spot on the Delaware River, then known as the South River, where they built a fort, naming it Christina (kris tē'ná). 

Now it happened that the Dutch had already built Fort Nassau on the same river, not many miles from Fort 

Christina. For fifteen years the Dutch had been buying beaver skins from the Indians, and they did not like to have rivals come into the same region. The Dutch, however, were few in number, and more and more Swedes kept coming to New Sweden. It looked as if the Dutch would soon be forced back to New Netherland or even to Holland. (See map, page 276.) 

2. PETER STUYVESANT IS WORRIED 

Peter Stuyvesant, you remember, was the last Dutch

Governor of New Netherland. For nearly twenty years 271

EARLY AMERICA 

he was in trouble of one kind or another. He quarreled with the patroons along the Hudson River, with the English in the New England colonies, and with the Swedes on the Delaware River. Jealous of the Swedish strength, and anxious that the Dutch should not lose the fur trade south of New Netherland, he sent a man down to New Sweden to look after the Dutch interests. 

The Swedes had planned to build a fort near the mouth of the Skuylkill River. The Dutch learned their intention from an Indian who was unfriendly to the Swedes. 

The Dutch lost no time in building a trading post on the same spot. The Swedes watched their chance, and when no Dutch were on guard, they stole up to the new post to see what damage they could do. Whang! Whang! went their axes, and down fell the trees near by. 

When Governor Stuyvesant heard of this attack, he decided to go to New Sweden himself to have a talk with the Swedish Governor Printz. Stuyvesant showed 

Printz the Dutch title to the land along the Delaware and asked Printz to show him the Swedish rights. Printz replied that the papers were in Sweden. 

In a day or two several Indians told Stuyvesant that the Swedes had no papers to show their right to the territory, for Printz was even then trying to buy the land from the Indians. They called Stuyvesant "the Grand 

Sachem (sa'chem) of the Manhattans," and made him a present of the land in question. All they wanted in return was to be given a little corn and to have their guns repaired when necessary. Stuyvesant returned to New 

Netherland, satisfied that all would be well. 

But New Sweden did not pass to the Dutch without

THE SWEDISH COLONIES 

273 11 bloodshed. A new governor, who came to the colony after Printz returned to Sweden, brought with him three hundred Swedes. They attacked the new fort near Christina, and drove out the Dutch. Stuyvesant took his revenge by capturing a Swedish vessel in Manhattan harbor. A little later Swedish rule came to an end with the capture of Fort Christina. 

3. NEW SWEDEN BECOMES ENGLISH TERRITORY 

The Dutch held New Sweden for only eight years. 

Then the Duke of York in 1664 took possession of all the Dutch territory in America. Within a few years English settlers moved into the Delaware valley where they were joined by colonists from New England. Thus it happened that William Penn found almost a thousand people living in the region when he came to America in 1682.

29. HOW NEW JERSEY AND DELAWARE CAME TO BE 1. NEW JERSEY (1664) 

The early history of New Jersey is bound up with the history of New Netherland and New York. 

In 1614 a number of Dutch colonists crossed the Hudson River and tried to settle what is now Jersey City. 

They built several forts to protect themselves from the Indians, but no plantations were started at that time. 

Fur trading, which was the chief interest of the Dutch, was carried on by them in different parts of the present State of New Jersey as well as elsewhere. The region was then thought to be part of New Netherland, although no one bothered much about the boundaries. 

Then in 1664 came the overthrow of Dutch rule in New Netherland when Charles II gave his territory to the Duke of York. The Duke was generous, and was willing to share the land with several of his friends. Almost immediately he gave what is now New Jersey to two of his favorites, Sir John Berkeley and Sir George Carteret (kär'ter ět). These men became the owners or proprietors of East and West Jersey as they called the region.

Dutch and Swedish fur traders were already in the territory, but Berkeley and Carteret wanted New Jersey 275

EARLY AMERICA 

to be an English settlement. The proprietors therefore invited English people to come to their colony, where they might have religious freedom, a share in making the laws, and land for very little money. Puritans from 

Connecticut, Scotch-Irish settlers from North Ireland, and Quakers from England accepted the invita-

In 1666 many of the people in the old New 

Haven colony (see p. 239) led by Robert Treat and 

Matthew Gilbert, sailed up the Passaic (pá sã'ik) 

River and made a settlement which they called 

Milford. Later the name was changed to Newark (nū´ărk). 

Settlements were also made at Elizabethtown,

Woodbridge, and other places. The newcomers bought land from the Indians and started towns much like those they had come from in New England. 

The Puritans among them forced people to go to the Puritan Church. Some towns were wholly Puritan, some Dutch, some Swedish, some Quaker. Since they had been promised religious freedom, they did much as they liked about religion. Town meetings were held, as in New England, where the colonists decided what they needed and how they should get it.

CARTERET'S ARRIVAL AT NEWARK, NEW JERSEY 

This picture is taken from a painting by Howard Pyle in the Essex

Berkeley and Carteret had provided for a government under a governor, a council, and a representative assembly with power to make the laws. But the settlers in the different towns paid little attention to any laws they themselves did not make. They were always objecting to the rents they had to pay. 

The proprietors grew tired of trying to settle the difficulties of the dissatisfied colonists. Berkeley was glad to sell his share in New Jersey - the western part of the territory-in 1674 and a year later William Penn and two of his Quaker friends bought it. This was the beginning of Penn's real interest in America as a place to plant colonies. Carteret's share, East Jersey, was sold in 1682 to Penn and eleven other Quakers. 

Then the peace-loving Quakers, too, had trouble with the colonists in New Jersey. They were happy to give the colony back to the Queen of England in 1702. After that it was governed by England and became a royal colony.

EARLY AMERICA 

2. DELAWARE (1682) 

The Swedes had been in the Delaware region since 1638. You will recall that the Dutch conquered the 

Swedes and claimed their land, only to lose it again to the Duke of York when he took New Netherland in 1664. 

From then on Delaware, too, was controlled by the English. 

After the settling of Pennsylvania, William Penn saw that his colony would grow more rapidly if he owned the Delaware River and land along Delaware Bay out to the Atlantic Ocean. Can you tell why he wanted a strip of land next to the ocean? 

Fortunately, Penn was a rich man. In 1682 he was able to buy from the Duke of York the land along Delaware Bay. This was known as the "Three Lower Counties," and, although it was really a part of Pennsylvania, it was held separate for several years. Thus the Lower 

Counties, or Delaware, belonged to the Penn family, and its history is much the same as that of Pennsylvania. 

In Delaware, as in New Jersey, the soil was good and most of the colonists made their living by farming. 

They suffered no real hardships, like the earlier colonists in other parts of America, and they lived at peace with the Indians.

30. HOW GEORGIA WAS SETTLED BY POOR DEBTORS 1. GENERAL JAMES OGLETHORPE 

While the Puritans, the Catholics, the Quakers, and other groups of English people were starting a new life in the American colonies and seeking freedom from the hardships they had suffered in England, another large class of people would have liked to come also, but they did not have the money. They had to stay in England and endure punishments which many of them did not deserve.

In those days England's chief interest was to protect people's land and houses. It seemed as if the lives and liberty of the people, and their health, were not cared for so much as their property. If a man stole something, he was sent to the gallows and hanged. A person who could not pay what he owed was put in prison. As many as four thousand people were imprisoned for debt each year in England.

Many of the prisons were a disgrace. The jailers were often cruel to the prisoners. Starvation and disease took many lives in the dark, dirty dungeons. 

General James Oglethorpe (o'gel thôrp)-college man, army officer, and Member of Parliament-became a friend to the prisoners. He set about helping them. He 280

GEORGIA 

281 visited many prisons and then told Parliament about the horrors of prison life. A committee was appointed, with 

Oglethorpe at its head, to correct these evils. As a result many of the jailers were punished and dismissed from their offices and the jails were improved. 

Then Oglethorpe began to plan better things for the people who could not pay their debts. As William Penn had planned a new colony for persecuted Quakers, he wished to provide new homes and a place of safety for poor debtors and other sufferers. He decided to plant a colony in America.

Oglethorpe selected the best among them for his new colony. He was rich enough to pay the debts of many of these prisoners, and he agreed to do this if they would go to America and begin life over again. There, in a new land, they could make an honest living with their own hands.

Besides taking the poor debtors of England, Oglethorpe was ready to take people from the continent of Europe who were being persecuted because of their religion. He also planned to locate the colony far to the south to protect the Carolinas from the Spanish settlers in Florida. For this reason the King was ready to help him. In time the colony became a wall of protection against Spain. (See map, page 283.) 

2. GEORGIA, THE LAST OF THE THIRTEEN COLONIES (1733) 

With these purposes in mind, Oglethorpe and twenty of his friends formed a company called the Georgia Trustees. They received from the King a grant of land in America between South Carolina and Florida. They

EARLY AMERICA 

accepted the land "to use for others," for that was their motto.

In January, 1733, the good ship Anne landed thirtyfive families, one hundred and fourteen persons in all, at Beaufort (bu'fôrt), South Carolina. Oglethorpe then led his settlers on to the mouth of the Savannah (să văn'á)

GEORGIA 

283 Oglethorpe did not allow the settlers to govern themselves or to make their own laws. His colony was something like an army. He was their leader, their judge, their defender. He was a wise and good man, and did his best for the welfare of his colonists. He granted them lands in small amounts and would not permit them to own slaves. For this reason plantations could not grow up in Georgia as they did in South Carolina. He also forbade the people to buy or sell rum, for he thought liquor was not good for them.

EARLY SETTLEMENTS IN THE SOUTH 

The people planted mulberry trees for the silkworm to feed upon, and several Italians came over and showed the colonists how to weave silk. The mulberry tree also grew wild in

Georgia. It was hoped to make silk the most important product of the colony, but the plan failed. 

By this time there were a great many persons in Georgia besides the debtors. These men, with their sons and daughters, laid the firm foundation of a great State. 

Some very well known men also came for a while. 

Among these were the Wesleys. John Wesley was the

EARLY AMERICA 

founder of the Methodist Church and his brother Charles was the writer of many beautiful hymns which are still sung in our churches.

After Georgia had passed from the control of Oglethorpe and had become a royal colony (1752), the people were allowed to have slaves and rum as in the other colonies. Planters came from South Carolina and from 

England. The soil was good and the climate warmjust right for the raising of rice and tobacco. Large plantations took the place of small farms, and black laborers did the hard work in the fields. Georgia then grew in population and in trade.

31. HOW THE INDIANS LIVED IN THE ENGLISH COLONIES 1. THE INDIANS IN VIRGINIA 

When Captain John Smith came to Virginia, he found the natives dressed in the skins of wild beasts. In the winter the hair was left on the skins, but when warm days came, it was scraped off. Then in the autumn new skins had to be provided for winter wear. In this way the Indians were sure of having at least one new suit a year only we should remember that the snit did not look at all like the clothes we are used to seeing.

The Indians in Virginia painted their bodies red so as to make themselves look frightful. They had a queer way of doing their hair; they left it long on one side of the head, and shaved it off on the other side. They thought this very beautiful, for their ideas of beauty differed from ours. For jewelry they had chains of copper hanging from their ears and around their necks.

The homes of the Indians were shelters made of trees and bark, called wigwams. In winter they were able to keep snug and warm, for they had fires inside the shelter. The smoke found its way out through a hole in the top. If you had tiptoed into one of these simple homes under cover of darkness, you would have seen the 285

EARLY AMERICA 

whole family sleeping on the ground, close to the fire. 

Mats and skins were used for bedding. 

The Indians ate corn, roots, berries, fish, and the flesh of the animals they killed. They tried to keep as much of their food as they could for winter use. Their food was all they owned, besides their clothes and a few simple tools, for they knew nothing of owning land and other things, as we do. 

2. THE INDIANS IN THE NORTH 

In New England and New York the Indians whom the colonists met were of different tribes from those in the South. They had their own languages and habits, and their own peculiar ways of dressing and living. 

The northern Indians were bedecked with paint and feathers. Around their necks were strung wampum beads from which dangled bags of tobacco. Their heads. and faces glistened with oil. Skins of animals wrapped around them kept out the winter's cold. 

No one knows, of course, how many Indians were living in different parts of America when the white men first began to come over from Europe. From what historians have been able to find out, however, it is believed that about 200,000 Indians lived east of the Mississippi River.

The Indians made their living by hunting, trapping, and fishing, and usually moved from one place to another to get more and better food. As they believed that all the land was theirs to use, they felt free to roam as they pleased.

By tangled trails and deer paths through the woods or

MI 

A LONG HOUSE 

This home was made of wide strips of bark fastened to a frame of poles. The Indians did not move it from place to place as they could the tepee. across the plains, they traveled on foot. When snow hid the ground, they wore snowshoes made of a maple frame, with strings of deerhide forming the network. On snowshoes the Indians were able to travel as much as forty miles a day.

On rivers and streams they used canoes and dugout boats. Perhaps you have seen an Indian canoe in a museum. If so, you will remember that it was made of strips of wood fastened by roots. Over this frame were stretched pieces of bark sewn together. To make the canoe watertight, the seams were greased and covered with pitch. If you have seen pitch, you know that it is thick and dark, and smells like tar and turpentine, from which it is made. 

A hollow tree trunk was used for the dugout boat; its inside was made smooth and clean by scraping it with shells.

The Algonquins lived in long houses or round houses, sometimes in villages. Others lived in tepees (tē'pēz), as they called their tent-like dwellings made of skins. 

You have seen pictures of tepees and you may have ex-

EARLY AMERICA V. M. H. 

A TEPEE A tepee was the Indians' home which could be moved from one place to another. See the small door, the open space at the top, and the decorations on the side. amined one in a museum. Do you remember the poles, planted in the ground and tied together at the top to form the framework for the skins, bark, and bushes? Like the 

Indians in Virginia, they left a hole at the top for the smoke to get out. The bare earth was their floor. 

The mother of an Indian family was called a squaw. 

She did the cooking, of course, but her work was very different from our way of preparing food. She had no gas stove, oil stove, nor kitchen range, such as we use, nor did she have a kitchen with kettles and frying pans and the other utensils that we think are necessary. To heat water she first heated stones over a wood

1 

AN INDIAN SQUAW GRINDING CORN 

/fire and dropped them into a pot. This must have taken her some time.

The squaw was used to slow and unhandy ways of working. She was the family gardener. With simple tools - a pointed stick or a home-made spade of stoneshe tended the beans, the pumpkins, and the corn for the family. She bent her back beneath the weight of the firewood she gathered. She made the fires, dressed the animal skins, wove the baskets, and made the clay pottery -all this she did besides taking care of the baby, for there was usually a baby in every Indian family. 

When it was time to move, the squaw had to carry whatever of the household goods the family took along. 

Her husband carried only his tomahawks, bows and arrows, or his new gun (after the white man provided him with one), for he had to be ready to protect his family from enemies.

mother, as boys do today. His boyhood was spent in preparing to be a warrior and a hunter. For this reason he early learned to use the bow and arrow, to swim and dive and run, to set traps and kill animals for food. He had to learn to follow the tracks of an enemy and to hide his own tracks as well as he could. He learned to be brave, cunning, patient, and to stand pain, as all Indian men were expected to do. 

We have described the Algonquins of New England. 

To the west of them, in New York, lived the Iroquois. 

They were not so wild as the other Indians, for they

VIOLET MOORE HIGGINS - AN INDIAN BRAVE MAKING FIRE

EARLY AMERICA 

lived in large villages and enjoyed neighborhood life. 

Long houses, instead af wigwams, were their dwellingplaces. (See page 287.)

Among the Iroquois the wise men were chosen to serve in the Council. There each man was permitted to give his own opinions on questions of importance to them all. 

As they had no written language, they could keep no record of their Council meetings. They had to remember what they decided; sometimes they made notches on a stick to help them remember. 

3. WHAT THE INDIANS BELIEVED 

Before the English colonists in America taught the Indians to be Christians, the Indians worshiped the power or force in the sun, the moon, the trees, the streams, and the animals. These were thought to have minds, just as people have. This power or force the 

Algonquins called the manitou (măn'i too). The happy hunting-ground was their idea of heaven. 

The Indians believed in giants and monsters, and took delight in telling stories about their power and their tricks. These stories were told by the older men around the campfire. The children learned them, and when they grew up, they told them to their children. In this way the stories lived for hundreds of years, being told over and over again. In recent years some of these stories have been written down and we are now able to find them in many of our school readers and in books in the public libraries. You will enjoy reading them.

The Indians believed in the power of the evil spirits to make them sick or to cause them to suffer in different

ways.

The tribes had medicine men, as they were called, who were supposed to drive off the evil spirits. They painted their bodies and faces so as to make themselves look terrible; sometimes they wore frightful masks to scare the spirits. Wild songs and dances, too, were used for this purpose. By magic and charms they expected to get help from the gods. 

4. THE INDIANS AND THE WHITE MEN 

It was only natural that the Indians should not welcome the white men to 

America with open arms. 

Do you think you would greet with pleasure a strange race of men who came with their families to settle on your lands? 

The white men were

hunting grounds and fishing places away from the red men without even so much as a "thank you. At first, when only a boatload of the whites came, the Indians probably did not think much about them. Possibly they expected the whites to go away again. But when more and more strangers arrived and showed no signs of leaving, the Indians became alarmed.

EARLY AMERICA 

The Indian was sometimes a friend, sometimes an enemy, and sometimes a nuisance to the white settler. 

He was a friend when he gave the white man food and taught him the secrets of forest life; he was an enemy when he raised his

Courtesy Field Museum of Natural History MASK AND WOODEN GLOVES WORN BY A SAVAGE TRIBE OF INDIANS 

tomahawk to drive the newcomer from his hunting-ground; he was a nuisance when he began to drink and steal and spend his time loafing about the settlement and frightening the women and children. 

On the other hand the white man was sometimes a friend, sometimes an enemy, and sometimes a pest to the Indian. He was a friend when he paid for the land he used or made a treaty with the Indian or taught him to be a good Christian. He was an enemy when he burned the 

Indian village and killed all who lived in it. He was a pest when he brought disease and liquor to the wigwam. 

When the Indians and the white men first met, several questions arose that caused difficulty. One was the Indians' right to keep their lands and to fight all who might try to take the region by conquest or by purchase.

Another dispute arose over the way the fur trade was to be carried on.

PART FIVE THE ENGLISH KEEP BUSY IN AMERICA

THE ENGLISH KEEP BUSY IN AMERICA 

Homes to build, food to provide, roads to make, enemy attacks to guard against, laws to pass, taxes to raise - all these things, and many more, kept the English colonists awake in America.

All but two of the first thirteen colonies had been settled by English people. The Dutch and the Swedes were not powerful enough to hold their own settlements and they were taken over by the English. Thus an unbroken line of English colonies extended along the Atlantic coast from Maine to Georgia. 

Meanwhile the French along the St. Lawrence and down the 

Mississippi aroused their Indian neighbors against the English settlers, and made it necessary for the English colonies to plan for some sort of union. 

England and France were at war in Europe for about a hundred years, and usually such wars extended to America. 

Other European nations took sides and in the end England came out on top, defeating one by one Spain, Holland, and France. As a result, the Spanish were left in control of the territory west of the Mississippi and the French were able to keep only two small islands off the coast of Newfoundland. 

All the rest of North America came to England.

When the French and Indian War had been fought and won, the French were no longer feared. Busily, then, the colonists in the North earned their living by farming and fishing, lumbering and shipbuilding, and in the South by raising tobacco and rice on the large plantations. 

Like all mother countries, England expected her colonies to provide her with raw materials to manufacture in England and to boost English trade by buying goods in England. Believing England's laws to be unfair, the colonists found ways of disobeying them. If colonial products were to bring wealth to anyone, the colonies wanted to enjoy that wealth themselves.

Little by little, the thirteen separate colonies came to think of themselves as one group, with interests of their own quite apart from those of the Mother Country three thousand miles away.

© JOHN WARD DUNSMORE GEORGE AND MARTHA WASHINGTON ENTERTAINING GUESTS AT MOUNT VERNON

32. THE PEOPLE LIVING IN THE ENGLISH COLONIES 

From the first settlement at Jamestown in 1607 to the founding of Georgia in 1733, one hundred and twentysix years slipped away. During that time three groups of colonies were formed-the Northern, the Middle, and the Southern.

In the Northern group were the New England colonies 

New Hampshire, Massachusetts, Rhode Island, and

Connecticut. 

New York, New Jersey, Pennsylvania, and Delaware were known as the Middle Colonies. 

The Southern group was made up of Virginia, Maryland, North Carolina, South Carolina, and Georgia.

The Middle Colonies grew more slowly than the others. 

The Dutch and the Swedish fur traders who came to New 

Netherland, New Jersey, and Delaware, as you have read, did not care about building homes. They wanted only to make as much money as they could in America, and then return to their homes in Europe. There were only a few hundred of these fur traders, moving from one trading post to another, and without any family life. 

The fur traders were followed by Dutch and Swedish farmers, but their numbers grew so slowly that in 1664 there were only about 7000 people in the Middle Colonies. 305

EARLY AMERICA 

At that time New England had more than four times as many people.

There are several reasons why the Middle Colonies grew more slowly than New England and the South. In 

New Netherland the people had no share in making the laws. They did not have educated leaders in their church and their government, as the other colonies had. Schools were few and newspapers were almost unknown.

In New England and the South the colonists had representative assemblies. All the people could take part in making the laws and in deciding what taxes they should pay and how the tax money should be spent. They had schools and colleges, and well-trained teachers, ministers, and governors. Through their newspapers they could read what was going on in their own colony and in England, which was called "the Mother Country."

After New Netherland became New York, and after Pennsylvania was founded, the Middle Colonies began to grow. By 1689 there were about 45,000 people living in the Middle Colonies. They had left Europe for different reasons, but all of them wanted to start life over again in America.

For thirty years the countries in the central part of Europe had been at war. Crops and homes were destroyed; men lost their jobs entirely or had to work for low wages. They were poor and unhappy in their homeland. When they read the advertisements for settlers in 

Pennsylvania and in other parts of America, they thought they saw a way out of their troubles. 

So it happened that Pennsylvania and New Jersey

PEOPLE IN ENGLISH COLONIES 

307 welcomed people from Germany and Switzerland, Sweden and Denmark, Scotland and Finland, as well as from England. Besides these came groups of Irish, Scotch, Welsh, and French Protestants to make their homes in the Middle Colonies. 

Philadelphia, northern New Jersey, and New York were the big centers to which most of these people came. 

New York was the chief port after 1664; it was a city of ship-owners, sailors, traders, inn-keepers, manufacturers, and workers of all kinds. 

In the early days of the colonies Indian slaves were used, not only in the Spanish settlements, but also in several of the English. But the Indians were poor workers and Negro slaves soon took their places. There were very few Negroes in the Middle Colonies and New England. The kind of work they could do best was to be found in the South, where there were large plantations for the raising of rice, cotton, and tobacco. 

The colonists, for the most part, were hard workers, careful with their money, neither very rich nor very poor, but sure of making a good living through the work of their hands. They were the first Americans and they laid the foundation for our United States.

33. HOW THE COLONISTS LIVED IN NEW ENGLAND 1. HOMES AND FURNITURE 

Most boys have helped to build a hut, perhaps in a field, or at the edge of a wood, or in someone's back yard. 

Each does his share in finding boards, nails, and the necessary tools. If materials are hard to get, the hut may be rough and crude, but the boys who make it enjoy it as much as they would enjoy a better one. Of course boys do not have to eat and sleep in the huts they build, although they may have had picnics in them occasionally. They have good, warm homes to go to at night, with beds and chairs and closets and windows. 

The hut is something they have made for fun. 

The first homes of the early settlers in New England were not much better than the huts boys might make. 

Building materials and tools were scarce, and the colonists had to use whatever they found near by. But they did not have the good fortune to find nails and boards, canvas and tar paper, such as the boys may have used. 

The first task of the colonists, as we have seen, was to make a clearing in the woods, to cut down trees, bushes, and weeds, and to smooth out the ground for their floor. 

They stripped the branches from the tree trunks and used the logs for the sides, the roof, and the doors of 309

EARLY AMERICA 

From an old print 

PLYMOUTH IN 1623 

their cabins. Instead of nails they used wooden pegs of their own making. Chinks between the logs were filled with moss or bark to keep out as much cold as possible. 

Clay was used for plaster. The fireplace was clay, and so was the inside of the log chimney. Oiled paper served instead of glass for the window. The cabin was plain and rough, but it was the chief shelter of the early New Englanders until they could provide a better home. 

They tried to make the inside of the house comfortable. 

Again they had to use whatever they found near by, for they brought very little furniture to America except, perhaps, a chest of drawers to hold their clothes and their linen. This chest usually stood in the kitchen, not far from the home-made stools and benches, and the table made from the stump of a tree. Along one wall were

cupboards and shelves to hold their few wooden and pewter dishes.

The fireplace was the most important part of the cabin; it was used for both cooking and heating. As it was big enough to burn logs six feet long, you can imagine how much larger it was than the fireplaces in most houses today.

The good housewife in New England never let the fire go out if she could help it. In those days a light was struck with steel and a hard stone called flint, for there were no matches. Usually it took longer to light the fire this way than to send one of the children to a neighbor's house to borrow some live coals on a shovel or in a pan. 

Before the fireplace there might be a settle or settee with a high back of plain boards-not a very comfortable seat, to be sure, but the high back kept out the cold from other parts of the kitchen. In winter the cabin was always cold, except very close to the fire. That is why the spinning wheel stood near the chimney.

Hanging over the fireplace from a "back bar" of green wood were the cooking utensils-usually a large pot, smaller kettles, and a frying pan. These were made of iron or copper. Long iron chains from the back bar dropped the kettles close to the fire. Whenever the back bar burned away, as it often did, a new one had to be put in its place. If it broke off while the meal was being cooked, the family probably went to bed hungry. In later years iron bars were brought from England to be used instead of the wooden ones.

For lights the early colonists had no electricity nor gas. 

The Indians taught them to use torches of pine knots,

EARLY AMERICA A CORNER OF A COLONIAL KITCHEN 

If you visit the Paul Revere House in Boston you will see this kitchen with its old furniture. Which articles can you name? What do you suppose the white tags are for? but these were smoky and dirty. Candles were made when there were enough animals in the colonies to provide the tallow and fat. 

From whales there came a fat that made better candles than tallow; and, when many of the colonists made their living by whale fishing, whale oil was found to be useful in lamps. But this light, like that from the pine torches, was smoky and poor.

The best candles were made from the berries of the bayberry bush which grew in damp places near the sea. 

The New England housewives used boiling water to melt the grease from the berries. Then the grease was

This is a picture of a bedroom in the Paul Revere House in Boston. 

What sort of bed is it? What can you say of the bedspread and the rug? skimmed off and placed in another kettle to cool. A second boiling made the grease a clear, beautiful green. 

Candle wicks were put into this hot grease, and then the bayberry candles were ready for use on special occasions. It took so long to make them that they could not be used every day.

In the bedroom, which was usually much smaller than the kitchen, the simple beds were made from logs, split along the center, and fastened to the wall. Of course there were no mattresses and springs, as we know them. 

A feather mattress and long feather pillows, handmade patchwork quilts, and covers of hand-woven wool were used in nearly every home. 

In later days, when the colonists had more money and could buy furniture, they used beds called four-posters,

EARLY AMERICA 

draped with heavy curtains. These were wool in winter to keep out the cold, and linen in summer. 

2. FOOD 

We have already spoken of the food of the first colonists-fish and game and wild turkeys and corn. 

As much of their work was out of doors, the settlers needed hearty meals. Meat was their main dish. The housewives made big stews of deer, rabbit, or squirrel. 

Besides wild turkey they had roasted wild geese and ducks, pigeons, and quail. In later years they enjoyed pork and beef from their own farms. 

The colonists did their own butchering and preserving. 

As they had no ice, they had to put their meat into a mixture of salt and water to make it keep. Lard was made from the fat and tallow was saved for candles. 

Fried fish and lobsters, oysters and crabs were also used. There seemed to be plenty of these in the sea and streams. Codfish were caught along the coast all the way from Long Island up to the Grand Banks of Newfoundland. The "sacred codfish" later became the emblem of Massachusetts, for a large part of the success of this colony came from catching and selling this fish. 

The corn which the New England colonists raised provided them with corn meal mush, hominy, and corn bread. 

A mixture of corn and beans, called succotash (suk'ótǎsh), was a favorite dish. Then, too, they had baked beans and pumpkins. Some of those who could not afford to buy sugar used dried pumpkins for sweetening; wild honey and maple sugar also served for this purpose. 

At first there were no potatoes in New England. The

1 1. 

I THE JOHN HANCOCK HOUSE IN BOSTON 

Virginians raised them and sent them to England. From there they were sent to Ireland, where the Irish people enjoyed them very much. Then, when the Irish began to come to New England, they brought potatoes to plant in the new country.

In summer the housewives dried fruits and vegetables for winter use. They had no cans nor Mason jars in those days, or we may be sure the busy New England women would have had many shelves well filled with preserves of all kinds. Instead, there hung from the kitchen ceiling dried apples, pumpkins, and peaches, as well as beans and peppers. Later these were stored in the cellars or in the ground for winter use. 

When they had cows enough, milk, butter, and cheese were used. The women made their own cheese, of #course, and churned the butter. Churning was not easy 

I work, but the housewives did not complain. 

Only the wealthy colonists could afford to buy the tea,

EARLY AMERICA 

coffee, and spices that came from Europe. In their homes, too, liquors and wines were served, and the puddings and cakes were flavored with brandy. 

3. CLOTHING 

The New England colonists usually made their own clothing from materials they wove themselves. On their farms they raised flax from which they spun linen thread. 

The wool from their sheep gave them woolen yarn. The linen and woolen cloth were hand-made, and so were the garments they put together. Their dresses and suits were simple, and very much alike. Black, blue, and gray were the usual colors. Ruffles, ribbons, and puffed sleeves were not allowed. The Puritans were plain people and wanted no frills. 

Those who were rich enough had silks, velvets, and laces from Europe and cotton from the West Indies. In those days cotton cloth was much more expensive than linen.

The colonial woman wore a very full dress, cut plain and long, untrimmed except for a narrow piece of lace at the neck. It was of woolen homespun in the winter and of linen or cotton in warmer weather. The Puritan, as you have often seen him in pictures, wore knee-length breeches of homespun or leather, long woolen stockings, high cowhide boots, and a beaver hat. His shirt was usually linen.

At first all the spinning and weaving were done at home. 

By a Massachusetts law, as early as 1640, all girls and boys were ordered to learn to spin. Small classes were formed, each in charge of a teacher. A few years later

ce
