Dee stod

TEACHING, COLONIAL CHILDREN TO SPIN 

a new law ordered each spinner to spin thirty pounds a year or else pay a fine. In this way the colonists could st be sure of having plenty of thread and yarn for the cloth they needed.

After a while weavers began to come from England. 

Some of them traveled from one home to another and with a hand loom they wove the cloth for clothing and household use. Others went to towns where cloth-

EARLY AMERICA 

making was the chief way of earning a living. But, for the most part, this work was done at home. 

Woolen stockings and mittens were made by hand.

The women and the girls did the knitting, often working in beautiful patterns of shells or leaves. They also made the lace, that trimmed their bonnets and aprons. For summer wear they made hats of braided straw. To be a good housewife in colonial times, a woman had to know how to do many things that are not done in homes today. 4.

OCCUPATIONS 

Perhaps you have met the word occupations before. 

Or you may have come upon the word industries instead, which means much the same. Occupations or industries are ways of making a living.

We have already learned that farming and fishing were the industries of the first New England colonists. There was some fur trading with the Indians, too, but not so much as in the Middle Colonies. 

The forests of New England had a great deal more lumber than the forests of England. The Mother Country needed lumber, especially for ships, and the New England settlers were able to send large quantities to England. The forests gave work to thousands of colonists, for it took large numbers of men to cut down the trees and haul them from the woods and make them into ship timbers at the sawmills. Next to farming and fishing, the lumber industry was of most importance in New England.

But not all the ship timbers were sent to England. 

When the colonists were able to catch more fish than they

A COLONIAL SHIPYARD 

could use themselves, they decided to sell them to the other colonies and to the West Indies and Europe as well. To do this, they needed ships of their own. In this way shipbuilding became one of the leading industries in New England. It should be remembered, of course, that these were the days of wooden sailing vessels, for the steamship had not yet been invented. 

Rum-making was another industry in New England in colonial times. The ships that carried fish and lumber to the West Indies brought back molasses from which rum was made.

At first the colonists had to send to England for whatever things they could not make themselves. But they soon found that they could make their own glass and china, furniture and clocks, as well as cloth and shoes and many other things.

In New England there were many towns and villages.

EARLY AMERICA 

Each was rather complete in itself, with enough different kinds of workers to take care of the town's own needs. 

In Newbury, Massachusetts, in 1634, there were only ninety-one families, but among them were two ministers, two or three storekeepers, one doctor, one teacher, one sea captain, one mate of a ship, one dyer, one glove maker, three or four tanners, two linen weavers, two wheelwrights, two blacksmiths, one saddle maker, one lawyer, two or three carpenters, and eight "gentlemen." If all the other towns and villages were like Newbury, it is easy to see why each one could very well make what it needed. 

The occupations by which the colonists made their living were the industries of the men of New England, for in those days women did not work outside the home. 

They did not collect wages for their work, to be sure, but like housewives today they earned their living by taking care of the home and the children. You have already seen how much work, and how many different kinds of work, the New England woman had to do. 

Even the children had to learn to work. It was necessary for them to know how to do everything their mothers did. Little girls learned to sew and knit and put patches together for the quilts. Sometimes several girls or women would meet at one house for a quilting bee. Then they would sit around the quilt and stitch pretty patterns upon it, their tongues often as busy as their needles. 

For the boys there was always plenty of wood-chopping and wood-gathering. They became skillful with their knives and whittled tools and dishes. They helped at 'sugaring-off" time when the maple trees were tapped for their sap, from which maple syrup and maple sugar

were made. They learned to hunt, fish, and handle a boat. 

They could milk the cows, tend the sheep, and drive the horses. They helped with the farm work, using the simple hoes, sickles, and scythes which their fathers owned. They had no farm machinery such as is used today to make the work easier. 

5. TRAVEL AND COMMUNICATION 

At first the Pilgrims and the Puritans walked along Indian paths, for they had brought no horses to America. 

But when they began to make money in the New England 

Colonies, they bought 

E horses from the West Indies and from European traders. Horseback riding made the paths wider and 

I led to the building of roads. 

Let us try to keep in mind that there were no hard roads, even in England, until 1790. The colonial road was of dirt, deep with dust in the summer, and with mud in the winter. Sometimes tree ハリー ​いいいいいい 

​TRAVEL IN THE COLONIES IN 1660 

trunks were laid side by side across the muddiest places. This would make a "corduroy road." None of us would have enjoyed traveling over such rough roads, but they were the best the colonists had, and they had to use them or stay at home.

EARLY AMERICA 

inmL. ༤ཅ 

A COLONIAL FERRY ABOUT 1700 

As early as 1639 there was a law in Massachusetts which ordered each town to make a road to connect with the road in the next town. This made it easier for horseback riders and for the wagons and carriages that came later.

The rivers and streams in New England were dangerous to cross. The colonists were slow in building bridges, for they knew very little about such a task. Instead, they crossed at fords, where the water was not deep, or they used ferries.

When we think of a ferry-boat today, we usually picture a steamboat that will carry two or three hundred people and twenty or thirty automobiles. The ferries of colonial days were canoes, sailboats, or flatboats. Sometimes a wagon or a carriage had to be taken apart before being carried on the ferry. The horses were expected to swim the streams.

About the year 1700 stagecoaches came into use. By that time the roads were better, and people could travel greater distances - that is, of course, if they could afford to pay the high prices for travel. Some people found colonial travel so expensive that they lived their whole lives without going twenty miles from home.

The early stage wagons or coaches did not offer the comforts of travel such as we now enjoy in an automobile or on board a train. The passengers sat on hard benches; rain or snow could easily beat through the curtains and give them all a drenching. When the coach was stuck in the mud, as often happened, the passengers had to help the driver lift it out. 

The coach made regular stops at taverns, just as trains and buses make regular station stops today. There the passengers bought their tickets and took the coach. In the tavern stables were kept fresh horses ready for the arrival of the coach. Travelers were able to buy food and drink at the tavern and to chat with the people of the town who came to get news of other towns and cities in the colony. 

Goods were first carried on men's shoulders, for there were no horses nor oxen to bear the load. Whenever possible, the colonists took their products by boat, for it has always been cheaper to carry goods by water. 

There were no daily visits by the postman nor boxes where letters might be put and later collected by the postman. Only the most necessary letters were written, for the cost of postage was high. At first travelers carried the mail from a person in one town to a person in another. Later there were post riders who took the mail by horseback if there were enough letters to pay for the journey. The tavern often served as post office, and the tavern keeper was postmaster.

Few newspapers were published in the colonies. The - first appeared when the city of Boston was seventy years old. The newspapers were small and had more adver-

EARLY AMERICA 

tisements or paid notices than news. They usually came out once a week. Daily papers were not published until some time after the colonies became the United States. 

Thus it was hard for the colonists to learn what was M going on outside their own towns or to send information to others. This "give and take" of news is called communication, for in this way people share with each other whatever they think to be of common interest.

We ourselves find it easy and cheap to mail and receive letters, to procure daily newspapers and weekly or monthly magazines (mag'á zēnz') with news from all parts of the world, and to hear the day's news over the radio. We can therefore scarcely imagine what it must have been like in the colonies when there was very little communication. 

A COLONIAL MAIL CARRIER 6. EDUCATION 

The Puritans in New England believed that every child should learn to read the Bible and the Catechism (kǎt'ě kizm) and other religious books, and to write carefully and beautifully.

Manners were taught at home, for politeness was important to the happiness of all. Children learned to be thoughtful for others, to do little kindnesses whenever they could, and to respect their parents and teachers and old people. They were not allowed to talk at table nor

to attract attention when grown-ups were present. Co- 

Elonial children did not have the freedom which American boys and girls now enjoy. 

The first schools in America were in Massachusetts, the colony in which there were ministers and elders who had been well educated in England. The people of this colony had a great deal of respect for learning and were willing to pay for their children's education, even if they had to do without something else.

The little girls attended a "Dame's School," which was usually held in the teacher's home. Here she taught them their ABC's and the first lessons in reading. The little boys became the pupils of the minister in his home. 

These were private schools, of course, for public schools were not opened until 1647. 

In those days there were no Primers nor First Readers with brightly-colored pictures and interesting stories such as you enjoy. Instead, there was what was called a Hornbook, which was not really a book at all. It was a thin piece of board, smaller than the printed page you are now reading, with a handle at one end. Through a hole in the handle a string was drawn for tying around the pupil's neck.

On this board was a printed sheet of paper bearing the ABC's, such syllables as am, an, em, en, and others like these, and the Lord's Prayer. The paper was held to the board by a thin sheet of horn. That was how this colonial "reader" got its name.

After 1690 the children learned from the New England Primer, the first real school book to be published in America. Here again the ABC's, the syllables, and the

EARLY AMERICA 

Lord's Prayer were given, in addition to the Apostles' Creed, Bible questions and answers, a few bits of poetry, and lists of names. In later years the Cathechism and

some short stories were added.

The Puritans gave much importance to religious training.

They combined school work with what the church taught. For a long time the only books the colonial child had to read were those that told of duty and death, sin and punishment. These were read over and over again. 

The boys went to the Master's School after the minister had taught them the first steps of reading and writing. Here they had more reading, a great deal of writing, The problems in aritharithmetic, and Catechism study. metic were long and hard. Only the schoolmaster had a book, and the boys had to puzzle out most of the problems without help.

The pupils gave close attention to their writing, for it was thought to be an important subject. Today boys and girls sometimes think that good writing is quite unimportant, but they are mistaken, for it is very important. 

From the Master's School some of the boys went on to the Latin Grammar School, where they had more work in reading, writing, and arithmetic, with English grammar and Latin besides. They had to study Latin if they expected to go to college and learn to be ministers or lawyers. The Latin Grammar School was the high school of colonial times.

The teachers in the colonial schools were held in great respect by both pupils and parents, but they received small pay for their services. In some schools the teachers were paid less than a dollar a week. Many of them lived in the homes of the pupils, and had to find other work during the long vacation periods, for in those days the schools were open only a few months in the year. 

The first college in America was Harvard, established in 1636, and named for the man who gave his library for the use of students. Yale College, in New Haven, was another of the early colleges, founded in 1701. 

Massachusetts not only had the first college in the English colonies, but it also made the first law to compel children to go to school. In 1647 the first public school system was founded; before that, the schools had been private, supported by the families of the pupils. By 

1689 nearly all of New England had laws which compelled the children to go to school.

The early public schools were about as rough and plain as the early homes of the colonists. A log schoolhouse

EARLY AMERICA 

with a dirt floor and hard benches without backs was a familiar sight in nearly every town. There were no blackboards nor maps, no bulletin boards nor colorful posters, no borders around the walls, no plants on the windowsill, such as we see in classrooms today. The 

A COLONIAL SCHOOLHOUSE 

How is this different from the country schoolhouses of today? light came through a window of oiled paper, and the fireplace was the source of whatever warmth was in the room. 

The colonial teacher was permitted-and expected to whip pupils who were ill-behaved or lazy. The birch rod hung in plain sight near the schoolmaster's desk, as a reminder to all mischiefmakers and wasters of time. Dunce caps were often used, too, and some children hated to wear a dunce cap more than they hated being whipped before the class.

Teachers were more severe with the boys than they were with the girls. In those days it was thought that too much study was bad for girls. People believed that they had such delicate brains that they should study only music and painting, fancy sewing and good manners. It was enough that they be able to read and write a little, and if they spelled badly it did not seem to make much difference. Girls did not go to college, and they did not expect to be educated so well as their brothers.

197. PLAY OR RECREATION 

The children in New England had to help so much at home that there was little time left for play. Their strict Puritan parents saw no need for fun, except the pleasure to be had in religious life and the duties of the family. h But the boys and girls managed to have a good time whenever -they could. Games of hide-and-seek, tag, London Bridge, baseball, and football were played then as they are now. In the winter sleighing, coasting, and skating were their favorite sports. 1 

Courtesy of the Metropolitan Museum of Art COLONIAL DOLLS 

Toys were simple in colonial times. 

Girls had dolls, but no doll carriages, wardrobe trunks, and beautiful dresses. Jackknives, tops, and marbles gave the boys many happy hours. Sometimes they whittled their own toys. They made as much fun for themselves as they could with their simple playthings. 

The one on the left has a wax head, wood hands and feet, and a kid body. 

The one on the right has head, hands, and feet of painted wood. The body is jointed.

Their parents found it a great pleasure to go to church on Sunday with the family and listen to a sermon that

EARLY AMERICA SABBATH IN A NEW ENGLAND VILLAGE AFTER 1700 

sometimes lasted two hours. After church there was always a good time as the worshipers ate their lunch together before the afternoon service. The Sabbath was a great visiting day for all these good people. It was interesting to talk over the sermon and to exchange the news of the week, but no laughter was allowed. There were no games, no running around, no "sinful" pleasures. 

Not only did the colonists meet on the Sabbath for church and lunch, but they also came together on such holidays as Thanksgiving and Election, college Commencement (graduation), and when there were lectures during the week or military training on the village green. 

In other colonies there were dancing and card-playing and horse-racing. But in New England these pastimes were considered sinful and were not allowed. The Puritans preferred the milder and more useful forms of pleasure in which all the neighbors helped one of their number, such as barn-raisings, corn-huskings, appleparings, and quilting bees.

Thus the New England colonists had little recreation

(rěk're a'shun). If this is a new word, you need not be afraid of it. Just remember that it means amusement or entertainment or whatever makes one feel fresh in body and in mind. In other chapters we shall read of the recreations of New Netherland and the Southern Colonies.

34. HOW THE COLONISTS LIVED IN THE SOUTH 1. HOMES AND FURNITURE 

The first homes in the Southern Colonies were much like those in New England in the early days. Huts or shelters of logs, bark, and moss later gave way to log cabins built more strongly.

When the colonists had sawmills and were able to make boards, frame houses were built. Brick houses came in when the settlers began to make their own bricks. In parts of the country where stone was plentiful, stone was used as building material. In the Southern Colonies, therefore, many different kinds of houses were built. 

The homes of the plantation owners are of special interest. These were built in the early years of the 18th century. They were mansions of three stories, with a pitched roof over the attic. Broad and beautiful doorways at the front opened into wide halls, running through to the back of the first floor. At each end of the house was a large chimney. In the rear was a kitchen, separated from the rest of the house so that the cooking noises and smells might not annoy the family. 

The rooms were large, with many windows. One or two bedrooms were on the first floor and the others, on the second, were reached by a beautiful, broad stairway. 333

EARLY AMERICA EWING GALLOWAY, N.Y GATHERING FOR A LAWN PARTY AT MONTICELLO 

This is the famous old colonial mansion of Thomas Jefferson. It is one of the show-places of the South. 

Courtesy Metropolitan Museum of Art COLONIAL FURNITURE

The beds were four-posters, high up from the floor, usually with a low trundle bed underneath. Some of the colonial four-posters and low trundle beds may still be seen in Southern mansions today. 

Artistic chests of drawers were used instead of closets. 

The heavy tables and chairs were much deco- rated, for such furniture came from Europe and was not plain like that which the early New Englanders made for themselves. "Grandfather" clocks ticked away the hours. China ornaments, pottery vases, and silver or brass candlesticks stood on the mantels over the fireplaces.

Courtesy Metropolitan Museum of Art Tea tables, set with CORNER OF A ROOM IN A SOUTHERN HOME 

silver and china, were always ready to serve the family and the many guests. 

From early days Southerners have been very gracious to their guests, giving a hearty welcome to friends and strangers alike. The plantations were so far apart that visitors were rare, and when they did come they were treated well indeed. 

Near the mansion were many smaller buildings, for the plantation was like a small village of several hundred

EARLY AMERICA 

people. Most of the plantations had a thousand acres or more, and many servants and slaves were needed to do the work. For this reason there were slave quarters, storehouses, tobacco houses, barns, dairies, coach-houses, and other similar buildings besides the owner's mansion. 

2. FOOD 

The food of the Southern colonists was prepared and cooked over the open fire, just as in New England, in big kettles and pots like those already described. 

In the South there was plenty of meat and vegetables, butter and cheese, bread and wine. White potatoes were rare, but sweet potatoes were a favorite dish, not only as a vegetable, but also in puddings and bread. Corn was used in as many ways as in New England. Apples, pears, and peaches grew on every plantation. Tea and coffee came to the Southern Colonies from Europe. 

Cows were brought to Jamestown soon after its settlement, and by 1639 there were 30,000 head of cattle in Virginia. Thus the colonists had plenty of beef. Hogs as well as cattle were allowed to run at large in the forests, and even in those early days Virginia hams and bacon were much desired. 

3. CLOTHING 

Most of the colonists in the South were English, and it is not surprising that they copied English styles and materials. Among the families of plantation owners, clothing was made in England and was expensive to buy. 

People dressed according to their social position in a Southern colony. A plantation owner or a "gentleman"

and his family wore beautiful garments of fine cloth and velvet, silk and lace. They powdered their hair and covered their heads with periwigs, as false hair was called in those days. They used the family coat-of-arms even on their coaches and on their tombstones, so great was their love of ornament and their pride in their family.

In the South not more than one-fourth of the colonists were plantation owners or "gentlemen." The other three-fourths were "poor whites," white servants, and

Negro slaves. These people wore homespun, home-made linen and cotton clothing, and dressed very simply. 

There was little difference between their clothing and that of the colonists in New England, but the Southerners did not have to dress so warmly. 

Wool was not so plentiful as in the North, for it was many years before sheep could be raised successfully in the Southern Colonies. Wolves were so common that sheep were not safe among them. But goats were not so timid and their numbers increased rapidly, in spite of the wolves. Goat-skin jackets and caps were worn, as well as garments of rabbit and raccoon skin. The workman's feet grew weary, clumping around all day in heavy cowhide boots. 4.

OCCUPATIONS 

Just as farming, or agriculture, was the chief industry in New England, so it was in the South. But there was an important difference in the character of the farming. 

In New England there were hundreds of small farms, on which the owner and his family worked, perhaps with

EARLY AMERICA TOBACCO SHIPS ON THE JAMES RIVER 

the help of one or two "hired hands." Such a farm was expected to supply the needs of the farmer and his family. 

Whatever was left over was usually sold in the neighborhood.

The plantations of the South were much larger than the farms of New England. On the plantations the owner did not actually work in the fields with his white servants and slaves. His hundreds or thousands of acres produced not only what was needed for use on the plantation, but a great deal more besides. The products they did not use themselves were called "surplus." 

The surplus was exported (ex'pôrt ěd), or sold to other countries to England, the West Indies, and the Canary Islands, for the most part. Tobacco was the chief product of export in Virginia and Maryland for many years.

This table will show how the tobacco exports grew, as the plantations increased in number and size: 

Year 

1619 1622 1627 1700 1775 

Tobacco Exports 

20,000 pounds 

60,000 pounds 

500,000 pounds 

28,000,000 pounds 

85,000,000 pounds But tobacco was not the only product which the Southern Colonies sold to other countries. South Carolina exported rice and indigo. Tar, turpentine, and lumber were also shipped out of the South in large quantities. 

Farm tools, or implements, as they are usually called, were about the same on the Southern plantations as on the small farms in New England and the Middle Colonies. 

Ploughs, hoes, axes, and shovels were used. Most of these were made by the workers on the plantation. 

The slaves were taught to make such farm implements, and also to do the work of carpenters, blacksmiths, and coopers, who make barrels. Manufacturing was slow in the South for several reasons. There was not money enough to start it in a big way; there was no machinery; the Negroes were not skilled workers; and England wanted to do all the manufacturing for the colonies. 

As early as 1649, however, an attempt at manufactures began. There is an account of a Virginia plantation owner who "sows each year hemp and flax, and causes it to be spun; he keeps weavers, and hath a tan house, causes leather to be dressed, hath eight shoemakers employed in their trade, hath forty Negro servants, brings them up to trades in his house."

EARLY AMERICA 

As in New England, the production of linen and woolen cloth was encouraged in the South. But the products of such manufactures were small and of little value compared with tobacco and the products of the forests. 

Along the whole coast from Maine to Georgia were mighty forests. During the colonial period there was a scarcity of wood in Europe. By 1700 firewood was almost too expensive there to be used in the homes, and the price of building materials was very high. Ships were needed, as trade increased, and Europe looked toward the American colonies for a fresh supply of wood. 

Three leading industries in the South came from the richness of the American forests: lumbering and lumber products, shipbuilding and ship timber, and the production of naval "stores." These naval stores were pitch, tar, turpentine, and rosin (roz'in), all of which were used on sailing vessels. North Carolina found the naval stores very profitable.

The best lumber was used for barrel staves, and a good business grew up in the making of barrels. Even by hand one man could make 15,000 barrel staves in a year. 

For these he received $20 a thousand in the colonies and $100 a thousand in the Canary Islands, where wine was made for export. Barrels were also needed for shipping fish, meat, flour, naval stores, and whale oil.

From the forest products came most of the farming implements of the South, as well as many household articles and furniture. Trenchers or plates, bowls, buckets, looms, spinning wheels, churns, mortars for grinding grain, and a great many other things were made of wood.

5. TRAVEL AND COMMUNICATION 

The early colonists in the South picked their way on foot over Indian trails and used boats on the streams near their homes. As in New England, the coming of horses widened the paths and led to the making of roads. 

As soon as a person could afford to own a horse, he gave up travel on foot; just as many people today never walk, even short distances, if they can go by automobile. 

The first roads were rough, ribbed with ruts, and often muddy. Here, again, logs were laid across the low, muddy places, adding to the discomforts of travel, but preventing wagons from sinking in the mud. Coaches were used by the rich and wagons by the poor. . 

New England, as you know, was made up of many small farms and towns. The towns were connected by roads, as the law ordered; not very good roads, to be sure, but roads that were usually passable. The Southern Colonies were thinly settled and the towns were few and far between. Because of the great distances between plantations, there was less travel in the South than in the other colonies.

If a person wanted to take a long journey, he might go from Philadelphia to Baltimore by stage, and from there to Savannah, Georgia. Such a trip took longer than it takes now to go to Europe, and it was much less comfortable. As in New England, there were taverns along the way which provided food and rest for the weary traveler. And when he reached Savannah, a warm 

Southern welcome awaited him!

The rough roads, long distances, and high prices for

EARLY AMERICA 

transportation made news travel slowly. Williamsburg, Virginia, and Charleston, South Carolina, had weekly newspapers after 1700, but not many people bought them. 

In their place "news letters" were issued. The news letters gave the most important bits of news, and passed from hand to hand among the colonists. 

Letters and packages were carried by private messengers who charged high prices for their services. The price for a single letter was from 8 cents to 372 cents; the greater the distance, the greater the cost, and the receiver of the letter paid the postage. There was no general postal system until 1775, when Benjamin Franklin established a line of posts from Providence, Rhode 

Island, to Savannah, Georgia. 

6. EDUCATION 

In New England the education of the boys and girls was thought to be a matter of importance and interest to all the colonists. Accordingly they made laws compelling the children to go to school and requiring the parents to pay taxes for the support of public education. 

The people of the South had a different idea about education. To them education was a private, not a public, matter. In the Southern Colonies there were a few welleducated men, but most of the people had little or no education. At one time, late in the colonial period, half of the men and three-fourths of the women could neither read nor write. There

The reason for this condition is easy to see. were no free schools in the South for the first hundred years or more. The plantation owners usually hired

EX 

tutors (tew'tôrz), or private teachers, for their own boys and girls. George Washington was taught by such, a tutor in 1740, and spent many hours on reading, writing, and "accounts."

Sometimes several neighbors joined together and paid a private teacher. Their children met in an old building out in a field. This sort of school was called a "field house."

A few of the churches established free schools for the children of the church members, and some rich men left money to support free schools. But these were few and scattered; there were probably not more than six such schools in all the Southern Colonies at one time. 

Virginia and Maryland had a few laws providing for the education of the poor and for help to those in the Grammar Schools who wanted to go to college. Under this plan in 1681 one poor boy in Virginia was "bound out" by the county court to a blacksmith who was "to teach him his trade and how to read and write." 

Many of the wealthy planters sent their sons to Oxford and Cambridge, in England, for their college education. 

But this was not necessary after the College of William and Mary was established in Williamsburg, Virginia, in 

1693. Its purpose may interest you: "That the Church of Virginia may be furnished with a seminary of ministers of the Gospel, and that the youth may be educated in good letters and manners, and that the Christian faith may be spread among the Western Indians to the glory of Almighty God."

But few boys went to college. Most of them learned to read, write, and cipher, and were then put to work

EARLY AMERICA A BUSY STREET SCENE IN COLONIAL TIMES 

with a craftsman such as a carpenter or a blacksmith to learn a trade.

Most of the girls in the Southern Colonies did not go to Grammar School or college. Some of the daughters in wealthy families went to private schools in the towns where they learned music, painting, fancy-work, and French. 

7. PLAY OR RECREATION 

The colonists in the South were not so strict about play as were the stern Puritans of New England. Cardplaying and horse-racing were forbidden in New England, but were favorite sports in the South. Hunting was

another pastime which occupied a great deal of their attention.

Entertaining guests and travelers always gave the Southerners a great deal of pleasure. The best they had was none too good for anyone who paid them a visit. 

Tea-drinking, big dinners served with liquor of several kinds, and dancing were usual in the homes of the wealthy.

They enjoyed making paintings and drawings. Sometimes they took these to Charleston, South Carolina, which became an art center for the South. 

The children's games were much the same as they were in the other colonies. Boys early learned to handle a gun, shoot at targets, ride horseback, and find whatever pleasure they could in the open country.

35. HOW THE DUTCH COLONISTS LIVED IN AMERICA 1. HOMES AND FURNITURE 

The first houses in New Netherland were made of logs and had thatched roofs. At a later time, when ships from Holland had brought in bricks and tiles, the houses looked more like those in the Netherlands. Then they were built mostly of wood or of wood and brick, trimmed with tile patterns. The gable ends facing the street were of brick. The houses had steep roofs, large doors, and small windows.

A weather vane perched on top of each roof so that the family might know which way the wind was blowing. 

The large front door was divided into two parts, the upper one opening like a window. There was always a big brass knocker on the door, generally shaped like an animal's head.

At the corners of the house stood large rain barrels to catch the water that fell from the roof. At each end of the house was a large chimney. The second story extended beyond the first and the steep roof came down to cover the porch. Most of the visiting was done on the porch, which was comfortably furnished. 

Through the middle of the house ran a wide hallway covered with a ceiling of heavy beams. A kitchen, a 347

EARLY AMERICA WENENTNI THE SHORE OF EAST RIVER WHEN NEW YORK CITY WAS NEW AMSTERDAM 

parlor, and small bedrooms opened into the hallway. 

The large wood-burning fireplaces furnished the only heat; after sunset, candles were used for light. The floors were scrubbed and sanded instead of being carpeted. Nearly every Dutch home had a grandfather clock and an old Dutch Bible.

On the second floor there might be one or two extra bedrooms, in addition to the large attic. The four-poster beds with curtains or canopies were high up from the floor to make room for the children's trundle beds underneath. The English colonists in the South had such beds, too, you will recall. Chests of drawers were used for storing linens and laces. 

The furniture came from Europe to furnish the homes of the patroons and the richer colonists. The rest had to be satisfied with what they could make or buy in New 

Amsterdam.

F In the attic were stored the spinning wheels and looms; foods were stored under the house-butter and lard, potatoes and other vegetables, salted meat and apples, cider and rum in barrels.

Just outside the house were the barns, well stocked 1 with horses, cattle, and corn. In the back yard room was usually provided for trees and a small garden. The houses were built close to the street, and the yards were enclosed with board fences. 

Some of the best of the Dutch colonial houses were along the Hudson River, on Long Island, and in the northern part of New Jersey.

The city parks, or commons, served as cow pastures. 

The man who tended the cows drove them to the field and home again at milking time. In the evening he sounded his horn at every gate to announce the safe return of the cows. In the morning the bell called the cows from every yard to join the others on the way to the meadows. 

2. FOOD AND CLOTHING 

Of all the colonists in America the Dutch probably cared most about their food. Deer and pigs, wild geese and wild turkeys, fish and oysters they had in abundance. 

They also had peas, beans, corn, and plenty of milk and cheese. Bread and cakes of several kinds were served in their homes. 

Then, too, they made what they called "head-cheese,' of beef heads cut up small and pressed so that the meat set firmly. The chief fruit used by the Dutch was the apple, and every year each family made several barrels of cider.

EARLY AMERICA 

The dress of the Dutch colonists would seem strange to us.

The rich officers wore high boots, silks, satins, velvets, ruffled shirts, knee-length breeches, long silk stockings with silver-buckled shoes, and high hats trimmed with buckles or feathers or lace.

The wives of the wealthy dressed in beautiful colors and rich materials, some of which came from the East. 

They wore full skirts of velvet, silver-trimmed, lace ruffles, broad petticoats, bright silk stockings and shoes to match, and white linen caps with a fold or a ruffle that came down to the shoulders.

A laborer did not dress expensively. He wore wide breeches, a long shirt-like coat with many buttons, a low collar, a broad-brimmed hat, wooden shoes on week-days and leather shoes on Sunday. The boys dressed like their fathers, and the girls plainly, like their mothers, in frocks and sunbonnets of coarse cloth of linen and wool or of cotton and wool. 

3. MAKING A LIVING 

For many years fur trading was the chief way of making a living in the Dutch colonies. You remember that the Dutch spread out from New Amsterdam to increase the fur trade, some going up the Hudson River, some to Connecticut, others down to New Jersey and Delaware. Beaver skins were wanted most, but otter and mink were also sold to the Dutch by the Indians for guns and ammunition, liquor, and trinkets. 

Over in the mother country, Holland, ships and naval stores were needed. The government there encouraged the colonists to cut down trees and send ship timber to

יד

Holland. As early as 1628 two sawmills were busily at work on Manhattan Island. Other mills were set up elsewhere, especially at Albany, which became the lumber center of New York.

Although the Dutch settlers provided lumber for ships, they did very little ship-building themselves. The New 

England colonists were able to build ships at lower prices, and naturally most orders for new ships went to New England.

The mother country, Holland, did not permit the Dutch colonists to make their own woolen, linen, or cotton cloth, except for home use. Manufacturing of cloth was therefore slow in coming; but after 

England took over the colony cloth-making became one of the chief occupations. 

A NEW YORK POLICEMAN IN 

1693 On the farms of New Netherland the soil was rich. Several different crops were raised by the farmers, who did not give all their attention and all their land to the production of one crop, like tobacco, as the planters did in the South. The farm of a Dutch settler was small, and he raised potatoes, corn, wheat, and other grains upon it. 

He sent a great deal of his wheat and flour to Europe, and in return received sugar and manufactured goods of different kinds which he could not make nor buy in the New World.

Wine came to New Netherland from Europe, but among the Dutch colonists there were several distillers and brewers, and the settlers often had more liquor than was good for them.

In the early days their trade was entirely with Holland. As the colony grew, its needs increased and also its products. Trade was then carried on with England, the West Indies, and the other American colonies.

4. TRAVEL 

What has already been said about early travel in New England is largely true of the Dutch settlements in New Netherland. Whenever they could, the colonists traveled by boat on the rivers and lakes. For shorter distances they went on foot or on horseback. 

Heavy loads of lumber and grain were carried in wagons drawn by oxen, and sometimes people rode in the same kind of wagons-but not very far, for the roads were narrow, rough, and often muddy. 

Travel for pleasure had not come into fashion. More than a hundred years after New Netherland became New York, the first stagecoach between New York and Philadelphia began to make regular trips. Two or three days were needed for the journey, but that was so short a time that people called the coach the "Flying Machine."

Today the trip can be made in two hours by train or one hour by airplane. 

5. EDUCATION 

The Dutch settlements in New Netherland grew slowly, for the first people to come were all fur traders. When the colony was fifteen years old, only three hundred colonists lived in all New Netherland, and not one minister nor teacher was among them.

When the Dutch West India Company received its charter, the company was ordered to have "good and fit preachers, schoolmasters, and comforters of the sick."

Even then the first school in New Amsterdam was not opened until 1638. The teacher's salary was paid by the

EARLY AMERICA 

A DUTCH SCHOOL 

How does this classroom differ from yours? company, but all the parents who could afford it paid small fees for their children who attended the school. 

The poor received the same teaching without charge. 

Later the school came entirely under the control of the church.

The Dutch believed that there should be elementary schools in the towns and villages under the direction of the church. Those who could should pay for the schooling, but the poor should be taught free. Reading, writing, grammar, and the Catechism and prayer-book took most of the school day.

School began at eight in the morning and lasted until eleven. A two-hour recess followed. Then school began

at one and closed for the afternoon at four. At times the children were examined in the presence of the ministers and elders of the church to show what progress they had made.

Ten out of the twelve Dutch settlements had such schools before 1664. The teacher held one or more positions in the church and was also the court messenger or the town clerk. Money to carry on the schools came from the town, from small fees paid by parents of the pupils, from the church itself, and from officers in the colony.

The schools in New Netherland were not so good as those in New England, where there were more villages. 

The Dutch farmers lived rather far apart and the villages were few and far between. The children of farmers in what is now New York had to get along without the benefits of education. The children of the wealthy patroons, of course, attended the schools in the towns or had private teachers at home. 

6. PLAY 

The patroons and their families had good times in the Dutch settlements. They lived on their estates along the 

Hudson River in the summer, and in the winter they occupied their town houses in New Amsterdam. Unlike their Puritan neighbors in New England, the Dutch enjoyed dancing, card-playing, horse-racing, cock-fighting, and a great deal of drinking. Turkey-shooting in season, sleighing, skating, and coasting in the winter were other favorite sports. Strangely enough, it was the rule in 

Albany and the countryside near by that only the unmar-

EARLY AMERICA Courtesy Metropolitan Museum of Art EARLY AMERICAN TOYS 

The chair was made for a child. The toy on the right is a Dutch sled with a doll for a driver. ried men should go coasting; apparently the sport was too childish for a married man.

The Dutch had one of the first theaters in America. 

They also had clubs and coffee houses where they met to drink, smoke, and chat, for they were friendly and sociable people.

Even among the poorer people, when the day's work was done, there was much merry-making and visiting. 

What refreshments they offered to their guests cakes, pies, and sweets fit for a king-for the Dutch enjoyed good food! 

Fairs were the gathering-places for all the people. 

When the cattle fairs were held, the settlers came to buy and sell cattle, cheese, butter, laces, and other articles for home use. But such fairs also offered the best times of the year to some of the colonists. Here they saw cock-fights, horse-races, and puppet shows, and they

were able to talk with settlers living at a distance whom they did not often see.

36. WHY THE ENGLISH COLONIES WERE 

NOT UNITED

The first thirteen English colonies in America extended along the Atlantic coast from Maine to Georgia. 

The settlements were scattered, as we have seen, and the roads were poor. For this reason people found it hard to get acquainted with people in neighboring colonies, and much harder with those far away. 

In the days when the colonists had to go everywhere on foot or on horseback or by boat, they did not get far from home. When longer journeys could be made by stagecoach, traveling expenses were so high that most colonists could not afford to travel long distances. 

Today news comes to us by newspapers and magazines, by telephone, telegraph, and radio; but the colonists did not have such advantages. There were very few newspapers in colonial days, and these had more advertisements than news. It cost so much to send anything by mail that very few newspapers were delivered by postal carriers. The people in one colony therefore were not likely to know much about the people in colonies that were not close by. 

Each colony had its own work to do, its own laws to make, and its own fighting to carry on. The colonies all belonged to England, to be sure, but the King treated 358

COLONIES NOT UNITED 

359 1. them as thirteen separate colonies, not as a union of colonies. Union came later, when the colonists found that they could improve their condition by uniting, or becoming one instead of remaining thirteen. Perhaps you already know that the words union and unite come from the Latin word unus, which means one. 

For many years each colony lived its own life-raised its own food, made its own clothing, made its own laws, defended its homes against the skulking Indians, and to sent its surplus goods to England. Farming, fishing, and ship-building in New England, the fur trade and farming in the Middle Colonies, and tobacco-growing in the South made the colonists in the different parts of the country self-supporting and self-sufficient. ! jala

If you remember that sufficient means enough, you will not find self-sufficient a hard word to understand. Since the people of each colony could produce enough for themselves, they did not need to depend on a neighboring colony for their products. This of course made each independent of the others, but it kept the colonies from uniting.

Each colony, too, had its own governor and officers sent by the King to direct the making of laws and to see that the laws were kept, or enforced. The colonists, as we have seen, had a share in making their laws, saying what their taxes should be and how the tax money should be spent, and how they should drive back the Indians or the French or the Spaniards if they came too near. The 

King sent no army to protect the colonies from these enemies. He expected each colony to defend itself from attack.

EARLY AMERICA 

There was still another reason why the people of the thirteen English colonies did not unite. Although they all called England the "Mother Country," there were many among them who had never been Englishmen, but had come from other European countries. 

In New England about nine-tenths of the colonists were English. But in the Middle Colonies the population included Dutch, Scotch-Irish, Germans, Irish, Scotch, and French as well as English. The people in the 

Southern Colonies were mostly English, but there were also many Scotch-Irish and Germans. In all the colonies there were French Huguenots, and in New York there were Jews.

Most of the English colonists were Puritans, Quakers, or members of the Church of England. The Scotch-Irish were Presbyterians, the Irish were Catholics, the Germans were Lutherans and members of the German Reformed Church. The Dutch, on the other hand, belonged to the Dutch Reformed Church, and the Swedes were 

Swedish Lutherans. 

Many of the colonists had come to America to worship in their own way, and often they lived in little groups of their own. They saw no reason for uniting with other colonists.

Jealousies and misunderstandings often arose between the people of different colonies. Sometimes there were quarrels that they could not settle alone. Then the King had to have his officers set things right, so that the colonies might be at peace with one another. 

Peace and union could come only when the colonies needed protection from danger. They could get protec-

COLONIES NOT UNITED 

361 tion by joining together as one people instead of pulling this way and that, as thirteen separate colonies.

Several things kept the thirteen colonies from uniting. 

The colonies were far apart; each had its own work to do; each made its own laws; each had people from different European countries; each had leaders who saw no need for union.

37. WHY THE ENGLISH COLONIES NEEDED TO UNITE 1. PROTECTION FROM THE INDIANS 

What is it that brings strangers together and causes them to make plans to protect themselves? It is danger -the fear of loss by fire or flood, or the fear of enemy attack. We often hear that "two heads are better than one"; this means that better plans can usually be made if two or more persons talk things over and act together. 

Since the world began, people have found that the best protection from danger is secured only when people unite or join together.

So it was in the English colonies in America. The danger of Indian attack was always present. The first settlers had taken the lands they wanted, cutting down the trees and making clearings for their farms. These lands had been the Indians' hunting grounds. It was not surprising, therefore, that the redmen grew restless and angry as more and more whites came to America and made their homes where the Indians once hunted and fished.

Some of the whites, as we have seen, believed that the Indians should be paid for their lands. Roger Williams and William Penn in this way made life-long friends of the Indians. But as years passed and the natives came 362

COLONIES NEEDED TO UNITE 

363 to understand that the whites were taking their lands, killing off the game, and driving them out, they became bitter and unfriendly.

The colonists who took the land without paying for it were always in danger of Indian attack. Such attacks were frequent and many lives were lost in the Indian wars during colonial days. 

2. THE NEW ENGLAND CONFEDERATION (1643-1684) 

The Pequot (pē'kwot) War in Connecticut in 1637 showed the people living in the weaker settlements that they needed to join with the colonists in the stronger settlements. Only in this way could they hold out against the Indians. The newcomers could not work well so long as they were in terror of their lives. 

Shortly after the Pequot War the colonies of Connecticut, New Haven, Plymouth, and Massachusetts sent men to Boston to talk together about protection against the Indians. It was decided that each of the four colonies should select two men and that the eight representatives should plan for the defense of the colonies. This union was called the New England Confederation. 

The plan provided that each of the four colonies should furnish money and soldiers according to, the number of men from 16 to 60 years of age living in the colony. 

Massachusetts, having the greatest number of people, had to furnish about two-thirds of the money and soldiers in case of war. This seemed unfair to the Massachusetts colonists and in 1684 dissatisfaction broke up the New 

England Confederation.

Although the Confederation lasted only a little more

EARLY AMERICA 

than forty years, it laid some of the foundation stones on which the United States was built. It recognized that all the colonies were equal; each had two representatives whether the colony itself was large or small. It showed that the colonists had the ability to make plans and to act together without asking permission or help from England. In addition, it gave the four colonies practice in working together and thereby helped to pave the way for a union of all the English colonies nearly one hundred years later.

With the passing of the years, the English colonists found that they had other unfriendly neighbors besides the Indians. Massachusetts settlers, pushing north, met

French fur traders who felt that they had a perfect right to the territory. Pushing south and west, the New 

Englanders came upon the Dutch fur traders. In the 

South the colonists in the Carolinas feared the Spaniards in Florida until the Georgia colony was founded as a protection against them (1733).

The line of French forts and trading posts along the Great Lakes and down the Mississippi was also a source of danger. (See page 130.) The fur trade of the interior and the fisheries at the mouth of the St. Lawrence served as reminders to the English colonists that the French were powerful enemies and that they claimed territory in North America which England also claimed. It was all very disturbing to the English colonists, and they felt the need of some kind of union against their foreign neighbors. The New England Confederation provided protection against these rivals in trade as well as against the Indians, whose lands had been seized.

COLONIES NEEDED TO UNITE 

365 

3. TROUBLE WITH THE MOTHER COUNTRY 

Meanwhile England, the Mother Country, was making laws which the colonists did not like, to control American trade. The colonists thought such laws unfair and they 

I paid little attention to them.

England saw that Holland was making a great deal of money from foreign trade, and the Mother Country did not intend to let a rival grow rich through trade with the English colonies in America. "Now," said the King and Parliament, "if we make the colonies ship their products to England, we can keep the Dutch from growing richer by carrying colonial goods. And we shall have more money here in England to pay off our debts and our taxes will be lower."

The King and Parliament wanted the colonists to raise tobacco to sell in England and to provide lumber for English ships, but they did not want them to manufacture articles which the English people could make and sell to them. Parliament even forbade the people in one colony to sell goods to another colony without first sending them to England. The merchants then shipped the goods back to the colony which wanted to buy them. Do you see how this roundabout way of selling goods increased the cost to the buyers?

The colonists thought that any unfair law might be broken. When they found that Dutch ships could carry their goods for less money than English ships, and that other European countries would pay more for colonial goods than the Mother Country would, the Dutch ships were used and other European markets received their

EARLY AMERICA 

products. They also bought goods from other countries, even though they broke the law by doing so. 

Such trade was called smuggling. You remember that smuggling went on in the early Spanish colonies in South

America, when Spain tried to control their trade and their shipping. Smuggling was not a new game by any means, and the English themselves frequently smuggled goods into their own country. 

It was easy for the English colonies to smuggle goods into their territory. The Atlantic coast is cut by many harbors, bays, and creeks, many of them far from the chief ports. Vessels were loaded and unloaded at night in these out-of-the-way waters, and goods were often transferred from one ship to another far out at sea. 

Then, too, products were falsely labeled so as to escape the notice of the English officers. Often cases of tobacco were labeled "Provisions" when they were shipped.

When the English government found that the colonists were breaking the law, it was angry. Arrests were made, and the law-breakers had to appear for trial before a judge and a jury. A jury, as you probably know, is a group of people who listen to the facts in the case, and then decides whether or not the person is guilty of the wrong-doing of which he is accused. Can you guess what colonial juries did with prisoners accused of smuggling? 

They found them "not guilty" and let them go free.

You can well understand that this was further cause for anger in England.

Thus the need for union grew among the colonies if they were to have fair treatment from the Mother Coun-

COLONIES NEEDED TO UNITE 

367 ​Tel: try. They felt that they had enemies in England, as well as Indian, French, and Dutch foes in America. 

Thus the danger of attack, of loss of trade, and of unfair laws helped the colonists to work together for their own safety and welfare.

38. ENGLAND AND FRANCE AT WAR IN EUROPE 1. THE WAR OF THE ENGLISH SUCCESSION 

Let us now turn to Europe and see what was going on over there, three thousand miles or more from the colonies in America, while the colonists on this side of the Atlantic were building homes, earning a living, and protecting themselves from unfriendly neighbors. 

You remember that Oliver Cromwell and his followers defeated the Royalists who were friends of the King of England. That defeat drove Prince Charles out of England to France, where he was protected by King Louis XIV till 1660. Then Charles returned to England and ruled as King until his death in 1685. 

During the reign of Charles II the Carolinas were settled, the Dutch were driven from New Netherland, and 

William Penn founded Pennsylvania. England now had an unbroken line of American colonies from Maine to Georgia.

Charles II was followed by his brother, James II, as King of England. This following in order is called succession. It belongs to the same word family as succeed. 

We say that James II "succeeded to the throne" when his brother died. James II wanted to rule without the help of Parliament. He also wanted England to be a 

Catholic country once again. He did not please the Eng- 368

ENGLAND AND FRANCE AT WAR 369 

lish people, and after three years they had had enough of him and drove him out of the country. He fled to France to seek the help of King Louis XIV. 

Meanwhile the English people asked William III, T Prince of Orange, and Mary, his wife, to be the Protestant rulers of England. William of Orange was Dutch, but Mary was the daughter of the unfortunate King 

James II. 

This led to a war that lasted eight years. England and Holland joined together against France and, with the help of Spain, Sweden, and Germany, defeated the French. When the peace treaty was made, William of 

Orange kept his throne as King of England, and Louis XIV promised not to help James II to try to get back the English throne he had lost a few years earlier to William.

Then followed a few years of peace in Europe, but there was not much friendliness among the nations. 

England and France were the most powerful countries. 

Each had colonies in America and in other parts of the world besides. They traded with their colonies in India and Africa, and each was jealous of the success of the other. England wanted to keep France from increasing her possessions, and France was just as eager to keep England from extending her territory. War usually comes when two countries have such unfriendly feelings toward each other. 

2. THE WAR OF THE SPANISH SUCCESSION 

After Philip V, grandson of Louis XIV of France, became King of Spain, the other countries of Europe feared

EARLY AMERICA 

that France would be more powerful than ever. Germany joined England, and for twelve years the two carried on war against France and Spain. This was called the War of the Spanish Succession. You will hear about it again in another chapter, for part of this war was fought by the English and the French colonies in America.

In the War of the Spanish Succession England captured Gibraltar (ji brâl'tár), the narrow strait between Europe and Africa, leading from the Atlantic Ocean to the Mediterranean Sea. You will readily understand why it was important for a trading country like England to hold and fortify a place like Gibraltar. It is still one of the British possessions.

A peace treaty at Utrecht (ū'trěkt) in 1713 brought this war to a close. Philip II continued to be King of 

Spain, but France lost part of her territory in America. 

Newfoundland, Nova Scotia, and the Hudson Bay region were given to the English. Gibraltar and the island of 

Minorca (mi nôr'ká), which lies south of France and east of Spain, also became English possessions, thus giving England control of the entrance to the Mediterranean. 

In this war Spain lost more than France. Although 

France had suffered a great deal and her navy was much weakened, she was still the leading country of Europe. 

And she kept first place for many years. 

3. THE WAR OF THE AUSTRIAN SUCCESSION 

When the ruler of Austria died, thirty years after the close of the War of the Spanish Succession, he left his empire to Maria Theresa (má re'á tě res'á), his daughter.

ENGLAND AND FRANCE AT WAR 371 

This gave King Frederick of Prussia, who was greedy for more land, an opportunity to seize Silesia (sĩ lē'zhá) from Austria, claiming it as his own. 

In the war that followed France helped Frederick of Prussia and England helped Maria of Austria. This war was known as the War of the Austrian Succession. 

For eight years armies hammered away at one another.

England's navy took first place on the sea, and France saw her colonies slipping away. The war was a costly one in lives, money, and ships. This war, too, had echoes in America, as we shall presently see.

For a hundred years England and France fought war after war to see which should be the more powerful in the world's trade and which should have larger colonial possessions. Gradually the power of France slipped away and England took her place as the leading nation in the world

39. THE EUROPEAN WARS HELPED TO UNITE THE AMERICAN COLONIES 1. REASONS FOR THE WARS WITH THE FRENCH 

We have seen that the profits of fur trading and fishing were the chief causes of jealousy between the English and the French colonists in America. The fur trade led the French to push farther into the interior and to build trading posts and forts along the Great Lakes and down the Mississippi River.

Meanwhile the English colonists increased rapidly until the settlers outnumbered the French twenty to one. 

But the English had other Europeans living among them in their settlements - Dutch, Swedes, and Scotch-Irish, and people from other parts of Europe, each having their own religious beliefs and customs. England expected the colonists to obey the trade laws, but did not furnish any English soldiers to protect them. For this reason, as we have seen, the colonists had to protect themselves. 

The colonists in New France were all French and all Catholics. They had one government, instead of thirteen different governments, as in the English colonies. 

Their settlements were scattered, but they had French soldiers to protect them. Then, too, they had more friends among the Indians than the English had. The 

Jesuit missionaries had lived among the Indians, many 373

EARLY AMERICA 

of whom had become Catholics. The Algonquins, you remember, were their allies (allīz'), and allies always. try to be friendly and helpful to each other. 

The friendship between the French and the Indians showed the English colonies the necessity for uniting. 

They had to decide where to get soldiers, leaders, and supplies for an army to keep the French and the Indians out of the English territory. The colonists had the Iroquois of New York for their allies. The Iroquois, you remember, had been enemies of the French since the days of Champlain.

For many years the French urged their Indian allies to attack the colonists who lived at the edge of the English settlements in New York and New England. Many lives were lost in these Indian raids, far from the towns and cities near the seacoast. Houses were burned and 

English captives were seized by the Indians. An army could not prevent such attacks, because by the time the soldiers arrived, the Indian raiders would have fled to safety in the wilderness. 

2. HOW THE WARS DREW THE COLONIES TOGETHER 

England and France were at war in Europe at different times, as we have seen. In America the wars were carried on under different names, for the colonists called each war by the name of the English King who was on the throne at the time.

The first war therefore was called King William's (the 

War of the English Succession), the second, Queen

Anne's (the War of the Spanish Succession), the third, King George's (the War of the Austrian Succession).

OE AN THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WAR 

The last clash was known as the French and Indian War; this struggle decided which should be the strongest European nation in North America.

Nothing was decided by the first and the third wars. 

In the second conflict the English colonists decided that they had better make a direct attack on the French in Canada. The land northeast of Maine and the islands in the St. Lawrence could easily be reached by boat. If the 

English colonists could seize part of the French territory, the French might put a stop to the Indian raids in New York and New England. The plan seemed excellent. 

The colonial ships, aided by British war vessels, sailed up the Atlantic coast and seized Louisburg (loo'ē bûrg)

EARLY AMERICA 

on Cape Breton (brě'tón) Island. Acadia (á kā'dē á), now called Nova Scotia (no'vá skō'shá), also became a 

British province.

The people of Acadia were required to promise loyalty to England for the rest of their lives. You can well understand how hard it must have been for these French people to make such a promise. Some of them refused to do so and they were sent away to other French colonies or into several of the English colonies along the coast. 

In Evangeline, by the American poet, Henry Wadsworth Longfellow, the story is told of the sadness of these Acadians when families and friends were separated. wing

NC 

JOIN or DIE FRANKLIN'S SNAKE 

colonies were being drawn more 

The French planned to recapture Acadia from the English, but their ships were no match for the 

English vessels and the plan failed. 

All this time the English closely together in order to fight the enemies of England. They were not soldiers, but farmers and fishermen eager to protect their homes and families against the French soldiers and their Indian allies. 

In 1754 Benjamin Franklin proposed a "Plan of

Union" for the colonies and plainly told the people that unless the colonies united they might be destroyed or captured by the French and Indians. His plan was very good, but it was not adopted either by the colonies or by the English government. The colonies thought that it gave too much power to the Mother Country, and England

EUROPEAN WARS HELPED UNION 

377 thought that it gave too much power to the colonies. So nothing was done even though the fourth war with the 

French was at hand.

40. ENGLAND AND FRANCE  1. WASHINGTON'S BOYHOOD AND YOUTH 

Washington was born in Virginia in an old, low-roofed farmhouse on the 22nd of February, 1732.

Washington's father was a wealthy landowner. He had three large farms, or plantations. He also owned part of several iron mines. He was called "Captain" 

Washington, because he was the owner and captain of a ship which carried his tobacco and iron from his own wharf on the Potomac River to England. Captain Washington's ship, like those of his neighbors, brought back coffee, tea, spices, furniture, silks, fine clothing, and other things which the planters of Virginia could not make nor raise for themselves. 

The hard work on the plantations was done, for the most part, by Negro slaves brought from Africa. Since the blacks did the work, the young men of the first families of Virginia spent much time in visiting, in riding and hunting, in horse racing and other sports. They were courteous and sociable, free and generous in entertaining visitors. Any stranger who was a gentleman would always find an open house among the gentlemen of Virginia.

George Washington was fond of all kinds of sports 378

​GEORGE WASHINGTON AND HIS MOTHER 

The boy wanted to join the navy when he was sixteen, but his mother persuaded him to stay at home. when he was a boy. could ride a horse.

When only eight years of age, he He could swim and row, and run and wrestle as well as any boy of his age. No boy "dared" him to an athletic contest without finding his match. He liked life out of doors, in the open. He enjoyed a good horse race, and he liked to ride after the hounds in a fox hunt.

When George was only seven years of age, the Washington home burned down. The father then moved his family in a sailboat fifty miles away to a new home on the Rappahannock (rap á hån'ok) River. When George was eleven his father died, and the boy was brought up by his older brother.

EARLY AMERICA 

Captain Washington left his lands and most of his wealth to his oldest son, Lawrence Washington. This was according to the law of Virginia at that time, which provided that the oldest son should have the landed estate of the father.

Lawrence Washington built a new home on the banks of the Potomac (pó tō'măk). It was a mansion for that day. He called the place Mount Vernon in honor of 

Admiral Vernon, whom he had known while a student in England.

When George was twenty-one, his brother Lawrence died. George then became the owner of Mount Vernon and his father's estate and was thus one of the richest men of his time.

But we are getting ahead of our story. While George was growing up in his plantation home, he went to school for a short time as other boys did. He also learned surveying, which is measuring land. Such knowledge was very useful in a new country like Virginia. 

At this time there lived near the Washington home a rich nobleman named Lord Fairfax. Fairfax noticed how able Washington was and admired him very much. 

In a short time they became fast friends. They often rode together in fox hunts, although Fairfax was sixty years of age and George Washington was only sixteen. 

2. A SURVEYOR IN THE WESTERN WILDERNESS 

Fairfax had so much land west of the Blue Ridge Mountains that he really did not know how much he had, for it had never been surveyed. Its boundary lines had never been marked off from the lands of his neighbors.

Fairfax now asked Washington to go over the mountains and survey these lands. Washington was glad to have this opportunity. He would be able to see more of the country and he would get good pay for his work. 

With one companion he set out on horseback for the wilderness. The two men crossed the mountains and at night they often slept out under the open sky. Occasionally they came across a woodsman's cabin, where they could have a bed of straw. 

They followed the Indian trail, or made a road for themselves through the thick woods. They carried guns to protect themselves from the Indians and the wild beasts. 

With their guns they shot the wild turkey or other game, and at times they caught fish in the streams.

WASHINGTON AS A SURVEYOR 

These they cooked before their camp fire. They held their food on forked sticks over the fire and used large chips for plates. 

Washington came to know the "signs and symbols" of forest life, the habits of birds and animals, and the way the Indians lived in the woods. 

3. TROUBLE WITH THE FRENCH 

Soon after this the English and the French began the French and Indian War, the fourth and last of the colo-

EARLY AMERICA 

nial wars.

The English had settled in colonies along the Atlantic coast from Maine to Georgia. But none of their settlements went back farther than one hundred and fifty miles from the coast, although they claimed the land "from sea to sea."

While these settlements were being made, the French were exploring the inland rivers and lakes. They had gone up the St. Lawrence over to the Great Lakes and down the Ohio and the Mississippi. They had built forts at important places-Niagara, Detroit, Mackinac, St. 

Louis, Kaskaskia, Vincennes, and at other points on the inland waters. From these forts the French thought that their soldiers could control the country. (See maps on pages 375 and 130.)

Among these French forts was one called Fort 

Duquesne (doo kan') where Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania, now stands. Here two rivers come together to form the 

Ohio. This point was a "gateway to the West" and it was on land which the English claimed for themselves. 

When the English, especially those in Virginia, found out what the French were doing, they were much displeased. They were afraid the French would stir up the 

Indians to fight the whites and to destroy the English settlements that might afterwards be formed in the West. 

Something had to be done. 

4. THE MESSAGE TO THE FRENCH 

At last Governor Dinwiddie (din wid'e) of Virginia decided to send a messenger to the French at Fort Duquesne to warn them to leave the country which the 

English claimed as their own.

383 5 12 月 

​CROSSING THE ALLEGHENY RIVER 

Why was this a dangerous crossing for George Washington and his companion? 

Whom should he choose for this important errand? It was George Washington. No better man could be found, for Washington had courage and strength, and he knew the western country. He knew the Indians and their trails, the traders and the woodsmen. 

Washington, then twenty-one years of age, set out on this mission with seven companions. He went through the forest on a hard journey of nearly six hundred miles. 

He and his companions faced many dangers, and had to travel through deep snows and heavy storms for many hours at a time.

Washington gave the Governor's message to the French commander, who replied that he was going to hold the Ohio valley for his country. Thus war between the 

French and the English began over the ownership of the

EARLY AMERICA 

lands west of the Allegheny Mountains. Many Indians helped the French in this war. 

Washington, accompanied by a woodsman, returned from Fort Duquesne to Virginia to report what the French commander had said. For more than five hundred miles through the forest his keen eye took note of everything. The journey was full of danger. At one time an Indian shot at him from a short distance, but missed. At another time, while the two travelers were crossing the Allegheny River, they were almost drowned. 

They had made a raft to float down the river, but blocks of ice in the swift current almost broke the raft to pieces. 

Once Washington's foot slipped and he fell overboard, but he caught the raft just in time to save himself from sinking in the deep water.

It was bitterly cold that night, but the two men dared not build a fire for fear the Indians would see them. 

Finally they reached home in safety. Such were the hardships and perils of the brave men who made the great West known to the world! 

5. BRADDOCK'S DEFEAT, 1755 

Washington's report aroused the English. The Governor now sent out more men. Washington was in command, but he was defeated by a larger body of French at Great Meadows, where he had built a rough stockade which was a fence of posts and stakes. He called this protected place Fort Necessity.

The British then sent over General Edward Braddock with 10,000 regular troops. The Virginia soldiers were placed under his orders.

மச் 

The BRADDOCK'S DEFEAT 

In the thick of the smoke the Indians were fighting from ambush. 

Braddock knew nothing of the country or of Indian ways of fighting. He looked upon a Virginia officer, like 

Washington, as a beginner. Washington tried to tell him about the country and the ways of the Indians, who were swarming in the woods. Washington also suggested that the army should break ranks and skulk along from tree to tree in Indian fashion. Braddock only laughed and said that the British troops would be able to conquer the foe in the way they had been trained. 

As a result, Braddock's army was defeated and the General himself received his death wound. Washington then took command and saved the army on the retreat by having his men fight from behind trees like Indians. 

Two horses were shot under Washington and four bullets pierced his coat, but he escaped harm. The Indians thought he had a charmed life.

EARLY AMERICA 

Before the war was over, Washington had the satisfaction of joining an expedition that succeeded where Braddock had failed. Three years after Braddock's defeat (1758) the English and colonial troops set out to capture Fort Duquesne. When the French heard of this expedition, they set fire to the fort and fled. When Washington and the troops arrived, they found nothing but smoking ruins. The British flag was unfurled and a day of thanksgiving ordered. The French name was no longer used and the place was called Pittsburgh in honor of 

William Pitt, a great Englishman. 

6. THE FALL OF QUEBEC, 1759 

The French and Indian War, which "began over a few miserable huts" in western Pennsylvania, brought a great empire to England. In Europe the war was called the 

Seven Years' War (1756-1763). 

The English finally carried the war into Canada. They won a crowning glory in the fall of Quebec in 1759, when the city was captured by General Wolfe after a hardfought battle.

Quebec was the strongest fortified place on the American continent. It was defended by Montcalm (mont'käm'), a brave French officer, at the head of a fine army. 

Its position on a high bluff on the river bank made it hard to reach. However, Wolfe managed to lead his men, under cover of the darkness, up a steep, narrow, and winding path to the plains above. When the morning dawned, Montcalm looked out and saw the English army in possession of the plains-the historic "Plains of Abraham."

[Donday, April 4, 1763.]

NEWS ARRIVED LATE IN THE COLONIES 

You will notice that the news from London in December, 1762, about the close of the French and Indian War was not printed in the New York newspaper until April, 1763. Why do we not have to wait so long for 

London news today?

A fierce battle followed, in which both Wolfe and Montcalm were killed. In the governor's garden at Quebec a monument was later erected in honor of these two brave commanders.

New France had fallen, but the war dragged on and did not finally come to an end until 1763. By the treaty of peace England received all the land on the continent of North America east of the Mississippi River, except New Orleans. New France in America was now only a memory. France kept only two small islands off the coast of Newfoundland, which were needed by French fishermen, and two islands in the West Indies. Spain gave up East and West Florida to England. England
