PART SIX THE COLONISTS BECOME AMERICANS

THE COLONISTS BECOME AMERICANS 

In overcoming the many dangers and hardships which threatened their success in America, with very little help from the Mother Country, the English colonists learned to do their own thinking and planning. Once thirteen separate colonies, each with its own government, they united to form one nation. 

At first they had no thought of breaking away from England and establishing an independent nation. The trade laws and the taxes, however, which England tried to impose on the colonies seemed to them unfair. The Stamp Act made them so indignant that they formed a Stamp Act Congress to decide what steps to take against it. Then the tax on tea led them to hold the famous Boston Tea Party, in which leading citizens of Boston, disguised as Indians, tossed the tea overboard. The Boston Port Bill brought help to Massachusetts from the other colonies, and the Mother Country knew that she would have serious trouble in America. 

Samuel Adams stirred up the colonists by writing letters to citizens throughout the country to let them know what England had done to Massachusetts and how the liberties of the Americans were put in danger. The Continental Congress met and made plans for union. Minute men promised to be ready at a minute's notice to fight for American rights. The "Red Coats" or British soldiers, whom England had sent to

America, were a constant source of trouble. Fighting broke out and at Lexington, Concord, and Bunker Hill many lives were lost.

Then came the Declaration of Independence, on July 4, 1776, the birthday of our United States. But it was not enough to declare independence; the United States had to fight to win it. The Revolutionary War, which lasted for eight years, was won by the Americans with the help of France.

During the Revolution the United States had an unsatisfactory form of government. This gave way to the Constitution of the United States which has been the law of the land since 1789.

"I HAVE NOT YET BEGUN TO FIGHT.” -JOHN PAUL JONES

41. 

WHY THE COLONISTS QUARRELED WITH THE MOTHER COUNTRY 1. TAXATION WITHOUT REPRESENTATION 

We have seen the result of the French and Indian War -England had earned a great empire in America. 

She now owned all the lands east of the Mississippi River; France had no land at all on the North American continent; Spain was removed far away beyond the Mississippi.

The English colonies were now ready to spread out toward the west. They no longer had the Spanish to fear on the south or the French on the north and west. 

The colonists were grateful for what the English had done to give them this feeling of safety. The great lands beyond the Allegheny Mountains were unsettled. 

Hunters, trappers, and pioneer home-seekers could now go there and make settlements. All that the colonists now wanted of the Mother Country was to be left alone, to be allowed to grow in their own way, and to govern themselves as they saw fit.

But England had other plans for the American colonies. These plans required a great deal of money. We have already seen that England looked upon the colonies as a means of profit to the Mother Country. 

Why does a government need money to carry on the 399

EARLY AMERICA 

nation's business? Money is needed for an army and a navy, for roads and public buildings like post offices and government offices, and for the payment of debts. 

England had been carrying on wars for almost a hundred years and her war debts were large. The English people were staggering under the burden of paying the debts and meeting running expenses, which included funds for the protection and the defense of the Americans. England now proposed to have America pay part of the cost of defending itself.

We all know that a government raises money by taxing the people. In our own homes we hear about the expense of taxes for the United States, for our own State, and for the town or city in which we live. Even if our fathers and mothers do not own the houses in which we live, they pay rent to a landlord, and that rent goes partly to pay taxes for the support of the government.

But the people in the United States today have representatives in the government of the nation, the State, and the town or city, who decide how much money must be raised in taxes and how that money shall be spent. 

The American people insist upon doing business of this kind through their representatives. 

We have seen that the thirteen English colonies in

America had their own separate governments and that they raised money for schools, for roads, and for soldiers to protect them from the French and the Indians. They were willing to pay taxes, and they expected to pay them, for the support of their own colonies; for they made their living in America and their homes were here. But

they were not willing to pay any taxes which were not made by their own representatives. 

The English government was badly managed at that time. England had a new King named George III. He was very stupid and wanted all the power in his own hands. Instead of allowing wise ministers to rule, as the two Georges before him had done, George III tried to be his own minister and to carry out his own plans. 

He meant to be the real ruler, and Parliament must do as he said. 

George III surrounded himself with a few favorites known as "The 

King's friends." The

King had many favors to give. Members of Parliament and their wives were pleased to be invited to court. The King could appoint men to 

GEORGE THE THIRD 

high positions with good salaries. He could raise members of Parliament to the position of nobles so that they could sit in the House of Lords, which was part of Parliament. George III used these honors to get power for himself. 

The result was disaster.

When he had the members thumb,' ," the King caused of Parliament "under his them to make one law after another that stirred up a ten-year quarrel between the colonies and the Mother 

Country.

EARLY AMERICA 

Parliament was made up of men whose homes were in England. They were supposed to be representatives of the English people and play a large part in making the laws. There were no representatives in Parliament from the thirteen English colonies in America. For this reason the colonists felt that they had no part in laws made by Parliament. They were especially angry when 

Parliament passed laws to raise taxes in America. They declared that such laws provided for "taxation without representation." The dispute led to bitter feelings between the colonies and the Mother Country. 

2. LAWS THAT CAUSED TROUBLE IN THE COLONIES 

(a) Forbidding Colonists to Move to the West George III forbade the colonists to cross the Allegheny Mountains and find new homes in the western regionthe very thing they were expecting to do. The purpose of this order in 1763 was to keep all the unsettled lands east of the Mississippi and south of Canada for the use of the Indians. The King and his ministers intended to found an Indian state to preserve peace with the Indians and to keep the colonies in check, lest they should grow too strong and want to become independent of the 

Mother Country.

The Americans did not like to have an Indian state in control of all the western country. The Indians were powerful and dangerous enough as scattered tribes.

The colonists knew that a union of Indian tribes would be perilous to all white settlers in the West or would

prevent them from going there. Certain colonies claimed the western lands. Their charters had said that their lands extended "from sea to sea." Nobody knew at the time how far that was. Now that the country was all English, the Americans expected to be allowed to move westward, at least as far as the Mississippi. They knew there were unclaimed and fertile lands in the region, where people without jobs and without land could make new settlements and find new homes. This was the frontier (frun tēēr'), the part of the country farthest away from the villages and towns, on the border between the settled lands and the unsettled. 

Some of the Englishmen seemed to think the region was a desert. "Let the savages enjoy their deserts," said one of them; "if we drive them from their forests our fur trade will decrease." The English were thinking more of themselves and their own riches than of the good of the colonies. But the backwoodsmen on the American frontier would let nothing stop them in their westward march. The attempt to keep them from going west was one serious grievance which the King's government put upon the Americans. 

(b) Trade Laws that Caused Trouble 

Then, too, the King's ministers began to enforce some of the old trade laws which had been passed from time to time since 1651. (See page 365.) These laws had not been used for some time and the colonists no longer expected them to be enforced. But England sought to govern her colonies for her own good, not for their good.

EARLY AMERICA 

England made the trade laws, as we have seen, to keep the colonies from trading with other countries. She ordered that the raw materials of America be sent to England to be worked up into goods in English factories. 

American products such as tobacco, cotton, indigo, rice, and lumber could be sent only to England. Goods sent out of the colonies, or into them, had to be sent only in British vessels. Goods brought from other countries, like wines from France or Spain, silks from China, coffee, sugar, and molasses from the West Indies, all had to be taken first to an English port and be taxed before they could be brought into the colonies. This made the goods more expensive. Besides, nearly all manufacturing in 

America was stopped so that the English factories might do more work and make more money. 

The colonists had never liked the trade laws and they were glad to have them fall out of use. 

Now England decided to make the colonists obey the laws and to collect taxes on goods brought into the colonies. To do this would have interfered with the rights and the profits of colonial merchants and shippers. These shippers had built up a profitable trade with the West Indies, bringing in sugar and molasses in exchange for lumber and fish. 

Enforcing the old trade laws would break up this trade. 

The American merchants were so angry about the trade laws that they refused to obey them. We know that the colonists secretly brought in the forbidden goods at night and hid them away. (See page 366.) In this way they escaped the English officers who were at the ports to collect the tax. This smuggling was not thought to be wrong; the laws were wrong, so the colonists be-

JAMES OTIS BEFORE THE JUDGES 

In those days judges wore gowns and wigs. See if you can find out whether gowns are worn in any of our courts today. lieved, and should therefore not be obeyed. Many respectable New Englanders, like John Hancock, became rich in this way. 

To prevent smuggling, the British officers asked the courts to help them. As a result officers were permitted to search cellars, houses, and store-rooms for goods that had been smuggled in without paying the necessary taxes to the King's officers. The attempt of English officers to search the homes of the colonists caused great dissatisfaction. If an officer had the necessary paper, or writ of assistance, as it was called, he could search anyone's house and seize his goods without being called to account for what he had done. 

Sometimes the writs passed from one person to another. 

If a man secured one, he might take out his spite on a neighbor or a merchant whom he disliked. He would not have to answer to court for what he did. James Otis

EARLY AMERICA 

gave up his position as State's Attorney in Massachusetts to speak in court against these writs. 

He was a patriot (pā'tri ŏt), a man who loved his country as a son loves his father. John Adams said the speech of Otis was like "a flame of fire," and that "then and there 

American independence was born."

These efforts of the Mother Country to control the trade of the colonies and to increase her own riches were very annoying to the colonists, especially to the traders in New England. (c)

An English Standing Army that Caused Quarrels in the Colonies

In the third place, George III and Parliament insisted on keeping a standing army in America. They planned to place ten thousand soldiers in the colonies and to have the colonists pay part of their expense. This was necessary, they said, to protect the colonists against the Indians and the French.

The colonists did not want the soldiers. They did not like armies and did not wish to have them living among the people in time of peace. The colonists said they could protect themselves. They had been left alone when they were weaker and needed help from the King and Parliament. Now that they were stronger they could fight their own battles. The colonists could rely upon their militia (mi lish'á) for their defense. They could control their citizen soldiers themselves. They did not want to have a regular army in the colonies controlled by the King. The English soldiers caused quarrels and trouble as long as they remained in America.

MA 

JOHN HANCOCK EXPRESSING HIS DISLIKE FOR THE STAMP ACT 

(d) The Stamp Act that was "the Last Straw" In the fourth place, the King and Parliament put a tax on America to help pay for the army sent to the colonies. The colonists were to be called on to pay for what they did not want. This was "the last straw."

That is, it was the last act of the English government which aroused the Americans to disobey the Mother 

Country. 

The hated Stamp Act was the form of the tax. By this act the colonists were forced to pay government stamp-duties which were to be required on such papers as wills, licenses, contracts, newspapers, and pamphlets. 

A furious storm against the stamp tax swept through the colonies in America. The colonists were ready to fight

EARLY AMERICA 

against it by force. The "Sons of Liberty" marched through the streets shouting and threatening. Stamp officers were forced to give up their positions and some of them were chased away. The stamps were burned or destroyed. When the time came to buy stamped paper, none was to be had.

The colonists began to unite. Representatives of the colonies were called to meet in New York in 1765. This meeting was called the "Stamp Act Congress." Nine colonies took part in it. The "Congress" drew up a list of American rights. "No taxation without representation," they declared. The colonies were not represented in the English Parliament; that body of men was too far away. Therefore, Parliament should not tax the Americans. The only bodies that should tax the colonies were the colonial assemblies, or legislatures. There the colonists were represented, and taxation there would be taxing themselves.

If the King wanted money for the support of the empire, let him request it from a colonial assembly. Then the colony might pay, or not pay, as it chose. This was the old way of raising money by the King or his royal governor. If the cause was good, the colonies were usually ready to pay. In this way the colonies had paid their fair share of the expenses of the last war. "The Stamp Act Congress" published a "Declaration of Rights." In this the colonies declared that they had all the rights of Englishmen and that taxes could be voted only by the representatives of the people who were to pay the taxes.

Here were planted the seeds of union, and here was an

t expression of "government by the people." Patrick 

Henry, of Virginia, raised his voice against English taxation. Henry was not afraid to speak out against the kind of government provided by the King. 

The strong feeling in America caused 

Parliament to repeal or remove the "Stamp Act" the year after it became a law. In doing so, however, Parliament claimed the right to tax Americans and to make laws for them "in all cases whatsoever." The Americans paid no attention to this. They cared little about such 

PATRICK HENRY 

"I know not what course others may take, but as for me, give me liberty or give me death." a claim so long as no attempt was made to put it into force. 

3. RESISTANCE OF THE COLONIES 

The New York legislature, which was the governing 1 body for that colony, refused to provide quarters for the English soldiers. Parliament put an end to the work of the legislature until it would agree to do so. Parlia-

EARLY AMERICA 

ment also took steps to enforce the trade laws more strictly, and it declared new taxes for the Americans to pay taxes on glass, lead, painters' colors, paper, and tea.

The colonists agreed among themselves not to buy English goods. Franklin said the people could and would wear homespun.

Samuel Adams, who was called the "Father of the American Revolution," stirred up the people in town meetings. He wrote a letter which the Massachusetts 

Assembly approved and sent to the other colonies. In this way the colonies kept in touch with one another and could act together. The letter objected to the new acts of Parliament and again declared that the Americans should fix their own taxes. The Massachusetts Assembly was dismissed by Parliament for sending out this letter. 

The quarrel between the Mother Country and the colonies was rapidly getting worse. or

The English soldiers angered the colonists still more. 

There were two parties among the colonists. Those who were against the laws of Parliament and stood up for the rights of the colonies were called "Patriots" "Whigs"; those who stood by the laws of Parliament and made excuses for the English government were called "Tories." When the "Patriots" set up a liberty pole in Boston, the soldiers cut it down. When it was set up again, the soldiers cut it down again; this time they sawed it up and piled the pieces before the meeting place of the "Sons of Liberty." There was a riot and one citizen was killed. Worse trouble was sure to come if the soldiers remained in the city.

4. THE BOSTON MASSACRE 

The citizens of Boston met in mass meeting and declared that no standing army should stay in the city without the consent of the colony. The people thought. that the soldiers had a bad influence with their Sunday horse-racing, their gambling, and loud merry-making during church hours. Finally, when a band of soldiers appeared upon the street, they were hooted at by a crowd of men and boys.

Some of the boys called them "Red 

Coats" and "bloody backs" and threw stones and sticks and snowballs at them. 

The soldiers were vexed and they fired upon the crowd. 

THE BOSTON MASSACRE 

Five citizens were killed and six were wounded. The whole city was stirred to angry excitement. A mass meeting was held which demanded that the troops be withdrawn. The feeling was so violent that the English government felt compelled to yield and have the soldiers taken away for a time. Before 

This event was called the "Boston Massacre." the people had the Fourth of July to celebrate (and for some time afterwards), the people of Massachusetts celebrated the Fifth of March ("Massacre Day"), when

EARLY AMERICA DETROIT PUBLISHING CO. 

THE BOSTON TEA PARTY 

memorial meetings were held and speeches were made which helped to keep alive the hatred of the people against the English. 

5. THE BOSTON TEA PARTY (1773) 

In 1770 the English repealed or removed all the taxes on America, except one. Since they did not want to give in entirely, they kept the tax on tea. This was done merely to show their right to tax America. It was a very foolish thing to do. The tea tax did not bring in enough money to pay for the cost of collecting the tax.

It only kept the flame of anger burning between the English and the Americans. The English tax plan should have been given up entirely. If the American idea of taxing themselves had been recognized, the old differences would have been forgotten.

As it was, the Americans continued to resist the tea tax, as they had the others. A band of citizens in Boston, led by Samuel Adams, disguised themselves as Indians, and silently at night went aboard an English vessel in the harbor and threw the boatload of tea into the sea. This was called the "Boston Tea Party." The people in other parts of the country refused to buy the tea; the quarrel between the colonies and England was growing more bitter.

The King and the Parliament now determined to enforce the law more firmly and to make the Americans give in. Parliament passed three laws which were more hated by the colonists than anything that had gone before. 

6. THE BOSTON PORT BILL AND OTHER UNFAIR LAWS 

One of the laws was the Boston Port Bill, which closed the port of Boston until the citizens of that town should pay for the tea destroyed. Closing the port was bound to ruin the trade of Boston. The Boston colonists believed that "grass would grow in their streets." The other colonies stood by Boston and sent provisions to her people.

A second law of Parliament was the "Massachusetts Act," which overthrew the written laws of Massachusetts, or so changed her charter as to put the colony almost completely under the control of the King and his gov-

EARLY AMERICA 

ernor. The old town meetings where the colonists practiced self-government were to be broken up, so that persons like Samuel Adams could no longer arouse the patriots to take sides against the British laws. 

The third law of Parliament was the "Act for the Administration of Justice." By this law any English officer or soldier who might be accused of murder when trying to stop a riot or disorder in a public place should not be tried by a Massachusetts jury, but should be sent to England for trial or to some other colony. A Boston jury had set free the officer who commanded the English soldiers in the "Boston Massacre," and it seemed to the colonists that such juries might be trusted in other cases of that kind. The colonists felt that this law would make it safe for soldiers to shoot down citizens when some quarrel or disorder arose.

Parliament also passed the Quebec Act which provided that Canada should be extended south to the Ohio River. 

This would set aside the claims of the colonies to the western lands and prevent settlers from going to the region. The colonists disliked the Quebec Act as much as they did the King's decree of 1763 which forbade them to settle in the western region (see page 402). 

Another law that Parliament passed provided for sending more soldiers to America to be quartered upon the people. This was called the "Quartering Act." It was now clear that these soldiers were not sent here to "protect" the Americans, but to keep them in order. It seemed that the Parliament was doing everything it could to offend the colonies and to excite a spirit of rebellion in America.

42. HOW THE AMERICANS MOVED TOWARD INDEPENDENCE 1. THE FIRST CONTINENTAL CONGRESS (1774) 

The laws of Parliament caused the Americans to draw closer together. The colonial Committees of Correspondence renewed their activities. Correspondence, as you know, is the writing of letters. Through the committees the colonies wrote to one another, which enabled them to act together. Another meeting was called for all the colonies on the continent; for this reason it was called the "First Continental Congress." This "Congress" met in Philadelphia in September, 1774. All the thirteen colonies, except Georgia, sent representatives.

Here began a movement for a union, which finally made 

America into a nation.

The Congress sent a petition or earnest request to the King and addressed letters to the people of England and of Canada, urging them to consider the American cause. 

The Congress claimed that Englishmen in America had the same rights as Englishmen at home. Therefore, they had the right to be governed and taxed by their own parliaments. Their colonial assemblies were their parliaments. These assemblies were willing to support the 

Mother Country when it was right to do so, but they wanted to be allowed to say what that support should be. 416

The American colonies wanted to be as Canada is today. Canada governs herself entirely and is just as free as the United States. But Canada is a loyal member of the British Empire, or the "British Commonwealth of Nations." She is treated like an independent nation and she can be entirely independent whenever she chooses.

Perhaps that was too much for the American colonists to have expected in 1776. England had to have the experience of losing the thirteen colonies before she could reach this sensible way of treating the rest of her colonies.

The first Continental Congress agreed that a Second 

Continental Congress should meet the following May, if the King and Parliament had not righted the wrongs of the colonies by that time. As the months passed, nothing was done to make things right, for England refused to change. Before the time for the Second Continental

Congress to meet, war had begun in New England. 

When May, 1775, came, therefore, the Second Continental Congress met and continued to meet from year to year to lead the colonies during the long war that followed. 1 2. 

LEXINGTON AND CONCORD 

England was much disturbed by the activities and independent spirit of the Americans. The King's chief minister offered the colonies new terms. He said America would not be taxed if the colonies would tax themselves and turn the money over to help pay the English soldiers and help to support the King's government in

EARLY AMERICA THE BATTLE OF LEXINGTON

America. The colonies refused to agree to this and continued to disobey the laws of Parliament. The King then declared Massachusetts in a state of rebellion. The other colonies said that they would stand by Massachusetts. 

The patriots in Massachusetts then began to prepare for battle. They organized "minute men," who were to be ready to answer a call to arms at a minute's notice. General Gage, who was in command of the British troops in Boston, sent a company of soldiers to Concord, about eighteen miles away, to destroy supplies which the colonials had collected there. The soldiers were also directed to arrest Samuel Adams and John Hancock, leaders of the patriot party.

When the soldiers started, Paul Revere mounted his horse and rode through the villages and countryside arousing the people. He shouted, "The regulars are coming!" The minute men seized their rifles and came together to their place of meeting. About fifty of them faced the British troops at Lexington, April 19, 1775. "Disperse, ye rebels!" shouted the English captain, for he wanted the crowd to break up and go different ways. "Don't fire unless fired upon," said Captain Parker of the minute men, "but if they mean to have war, let it begin here." The minute men stood their ground. The 

English fired. Eight Americans were killed and eight were wounded. On that famous day was fought the first battle of the war of the American Revolution. 

The English soldiers could not find Adams and Hancock whom they were sent to arrest. The two men were in Lexington the night before, but when the soldiers came, "the birds had flown." The soldiers marched on

EARLY AMERICA PAUL REVERE AROUSES THE COUNTRYSIDE 

to Concord and destroyed the colonial supplies. The minute men attacked them at Concord bridge. Here, as our poet Emerson said, was "fired the shot heard round the world." What he meant was that all the world would hear of the fight at Lexington and Concord which opened the war that led to American independence. He thought that America's becoming independent was one of the greatest events in the history of the world. 

When the English soldiers turned back to Boston from Concord, they found the countryside alive with riflemen. 

The minute men poured in a deadly fire upon them from behind haystacks and bridges, stone walls, houses, barns, and mounds. Before the English reached shelter, they had lost two hundred and seventy men; the Americans, ninety-three.

When Ethan Allen and the "Green Mountain Boys," of Vermont, heard about the fight at Lexington and Concord, they took up arms. They seized the strong fort at Ticonderoga (tī kon'dě ro'gá) on Lake Champlain. 

Allen demanded its surrender in "the name of the Great Jehovah and the Continental Congress"-and the British commander yielded. (See map, page 433.) 

3. BUNKER HILL 

The Americans now gathered a small army about Boston. Some of its members came from other colonies. 

The English soldiers attacked this army in the famous battle of Bunker Hill (June 17, 1775). The Americans resisted two desperate assaults, but at the third they had to give way for lack of ammunition. Each time the welltrained English Red-Coats marched bravely up the hill. 

The Americans behind the earthworks, which they had thrown up, were ordered not to fire "until they could see the whites of the eyes" of the attacking English soldiers. 

Their fire was then deadly, and after the battle the English found they had left a thousand dead and wounded on the hillside. The battle showed that untrained colonial troops could put up an able fight against the English regulars.

The Continental Congress now began to prepare for war in earnest. The colonies did not want war. They wanted peace. Even after these first battles, the Contimental Congress sent another letter to King and Parliament. This was called the "Olive Branch of Peace." 

They asked only that Americans might have the rights of Englishmen at home. But the King would not change

EARLY AMERICA 

THE BATTLE OF BUNKER HILL 

his mind; and the colonists were determined that they would not give up their liberties. 

ence. 

Up to this time nobody had thought much of independ- The Americans did not take up arms to win independence, but to safeguard their rights as they saw them. Naturally, when the English began to make war on the colonies, attacking their coast and burning some of their towns, the Americans began to think of inde pendence. 

While the Americans were feeling bitter toward the 

English, Thomas Paine wrote a powerful pamphlet, called "Common Sense." This was widely read throughout the colonies. Paine argued boldly for independence, declaring that it was foolish for a great continent like America to be governed by a little island like England. 

At the same time King George hired German soldiers to come over to America to fight against the colonies.

These were called "Hessians" (hěsh'ánz), because they came from the little German state of Hesse (hěs'sé). 

Their ruler was ready to hire them out to fight for anyone who would pay him for their services. 

The sending of the Hessians increased the bitterness of the colonists. Why, they asked, should the English permit their King to hire foreign soldiers to come to 

America to fight against their own flesh and blood? The great Englishman, William Pitt, said in Parliament, "If

I were an American as I am an Englishman, while a foreign soldier remained upon my soil I never would lay down my arms. Never! Never!" Pitt referred to the Hessians. He did not think of the English soldiers as "foreigners" in America. He thought the English in America and the English at home should be treated and governed in the same way. 

This reminds us that many of the leading Englishmen in public life sympathized with the colonies in their quarrel with the Mother Country. In fact, many people in 

England rejoiced at American victories. One Englishman even called Washington's army "our army." the other hand, many Americans were Tories who sympathized with England and enlisted in the English armies to fight against the American patriots. 

4. THE DECLARATION OF INDEPENDENCE 

On By the middle of 1776, for the reasons we have given, the colonies were ready to break entirely away from

Great Britain. The Continental Congress resolved that "these united colonies are, and of right ought to be, free and independent States." A committee was appointed.

EARLY AMERICA 

to draw up a suitable Declaration of Independence. 

Benjamin Franklin, John Adams, and Thomas Jefferson were put on that committee. Thomas Jefferson wrote the document. An English historian said, "It ought to be hung up in the nursery of every king," to teach kings

The Declaration claimed that all people should have equal rights under the law; that every man should have the "right to life, liberty, and the pursuit of happiness"; that all fair governments get their powers from the willingness of the people governed; and that, if a government destroys the rights of the people, they have the right to overthrow that government. On these foundations the American government was built. 

The Declaration was adopted by Congress on July 4, 1776, the day we celebrate as the birthday of our nation.

43. HOW  WAS FOUGHT AND WON 1. WASHINGTON IN COMMAND 

We cannot tell here the battle history of the War for Independence. The Continental Congress appointed 

George Washington, of Virginia, as commander-in-chief of the American forces. No better leader could have been found. Washington's courage, patriotism, and devotion brought success to the American cause. He served throughout the war without pay, accepting only his expenses. 

No one man did more to win American independence. He well deserves to be called the "Father of his Country.”

Washington took command of the American army at 

Cambridge, Massachusetts, in July, 1775, a year before the Declaration of Independence was adopted. It was a mixed army, wearing different uniforms and armed with different kinds of rifles and guns. Each State sent its own soldiers, fitted out in its own way. Let us remember that after the Declaration of Independence we no longer speak of colonies, but of States; no longer of the united colonies, but of the United States. 

2. RAISING MONEY TO CARRY ON THE WAR 

Washington made the most out of his little army. He had to ask the States for provisions and more soldiers. 427

EARLY AMERICA 

The Continental Congress could not help him much. It had no power to raise troops, or to tax anyone in order to get money to buy provisions. Congress issued paper money, but merchants often refused to sell their goods for paper money. Since people could not use this money to pay their debts or their taxes, its value grew less, and when Congress continued to issue more and more of it a piece of continental paper money came to have no value at all. Finally, if people wished to say how worthless a thing was, they would say it was "not worth a continental."

War cannot be carried on without money. The States gave some, but not nearly enough. Loans were obtained in France, Holland, and Spain. Robert Morris, a wealthy citizen of Philadelphia, gave of his own fortune and went from door to door getting money from his friends to help out the American cause. Morris sent 

Washington $50,000 for the army and Washington pledged his private fortune to pay the soldiers if Congress should fail to do so. Washington was about the richest man in America in his day. Morris, after giving away a fortune to his country, had to go to prison for debt and died a poor man. 

3. VICTORIES AT TRENTON AND PRINCETON 

Washington lost many battles, but he did not lose the war. Sometimes he would face an army five or six times as large as his own. He often found that to retreat was better than to suffer defeat. At different times he had to give up New York, then Philadelphia.

When Washington's army was defeated and in retreat

THE BATTLE OF PRINCETON 

across New Jersey from New York, the people were disheartened, the Tories increased, and soldiers deserted from the army. Citizens seemed to be helping the English more than they were helping Washington. Washington's men were ragged, poorly armed, without food. 

It was a dark hour in the war. 

But Washington did not lose heart. He determined upon a bold stroke. It was the day before Christmas.

The Hessians were at Trenton, in New Jersey. Washington knew that they would be drinking, feasting, and making merry on Christmas day. He decided to make a surprise attack.

Washington got his little army of 2500 men together. 

In open boats they crossed the Delaware River. The night was bitterly cold, and large cakes of floating ice were in the river, carried along by a swift current. But in spite of difficulty Washington got his men safely

across. After a quick march he came upon the Hessians, completely surprised and defeated them, and took a thousand prisoners.

Washington retreated across the Delaware before the main body of English troops could come up. A week later he crossed the river again and gained another victory at Princeton. These victories gave new hope to the 

Americans. Their courage was renewed and they were ready to continue the struggle. 

4. WINTER AT VALLEY FORGE 

The next winter was a hard one. Washington had to give up Philadelphia to the English. He led his army into winter quarters at Valley Forge. Here the Americans suffered greatly. The winter was bitterly cold. 

The soldiers cut down trees and made such huts as they could to shelter themselves from the weather. There were not enough blankets and shoes to keep the men warm. Many of them became ill. They had no medicines. Washington had to report three thousand of his men "unfit for duty, barefoot and naked." There was blood upon the snow from frost-bitten feet. 

Soldiers deserted at Valley Forge nearly every day. 

Those who reached Philadelphia, where the English army was enjoying the good things of life, were given food, clothing, shelter, and protection. Is it any wonder that many of Washington's men deserted? Perhaps the wonder is that so many remained. 

Amid all these difficulties there were grumblings and complaints against Washington. A plot was hatched against him to remove him as commander-in-chief. It

EARLY AMERICA EWING GALLOWAY, NY. 

YOUNG LAFAYETTE WITH WASHINGTON AND THE AMERICAN SOLDIERS AT VALLEY FORGE 

was said that he ought to win more battles. Fortunately the plot was not successful and Washington remained in command.

During the winter of Valley Forge Washington was. greatly cheered by news that came to him from the North. The Americans had won their greatest victory in one of the decisive battles of the war. This was the battle of Saratoga, in New York. Let us hear the story of this victory. 

5. FAILURE OF BURGOYNE'S PLANS 

The English sent General Burgoyne (bûr goin') from 

Canada to invade New York from the North. Another

English force was to march north from New York City. 

The two forces were to meet at Albany. They were ex-

pected to capture or scatter all the American forces in that region. The English then would have control of the 

Hudson River and would thus be able to separate the New England States from those of the South. It was a smart plan, but the English could not carry it out. 

Burgoyne started 4 from Canada with about seven thousand men. The American commander, General

Schuyler (ski'ler), had only four thousand men. The farther 

Burgoyne went, the harder he found the going. The Americans blocked the road, destroyed bridges, chopped down trees, cut off his provisions, and put all kinds of difficulties in his way.

routed them. The English and the Indians escaped, but most of the Germans were killed or captured. 

The victory at Bennington made men more willing to join the American army. Burgoyne captured Ticonderoga and left a thousand of his soldiers there. He lost eight hundred men at Bennington. As his army decreased, the American army increased. 

Finally, after Burgoyne had been greatly weakened, the two armies came into battle at Saratoga (săr'à tō gá). 

Burgoyne was entirely defeated and had to surrender his entire army, at that time more than five thousand men (October, 1777). (See map, page 436.)

Burgoyne's surrender brought help to America from France. When the king of France and his minister heard of the capture of Burgoyne, they believed the

Americans would be able to stand out against the English. The French had been helping the Americans secretly, sending them money and ammunition. Now they were ready to come out in the open and make an agreement with America to fight on her side. This brought 

France into the war against England, her old-time enemy. Thus France and America became allies and in 

February, 1778, they agreed to fight together until American independence was won. This was the first and last alliance or partnership America ever made with a foreign nation. 

6. THE END OF THE WAR IN SIGHT 

Washington now felt sure that America would win. 

But the Americans had yet to fight many more battles. 

Washington suffered a great disappointment when he found that an officer he had trusted was a traitor. Benedict Arnold had shown himself to be a brave soldier and general in the battle of Saratoga. Washington placed him in command at West Point, on the Hudson. That was a very important post. If the English captured that, they could control the river. 

Arnold, vexed at bad treatment he had received from Congress, offered to surrender West Point to the English. He was to be paid for his treachery. He thus proved disloyal to Washington and a traitor to the United States, for he was selling his country for gold. 

Three American militiamen captured the English agent who was sent to Arnold to arrange for the surrender, and the plot was defeated. Arnold escaped to the English lines, was made a commander in the English army,

EARLY AMERICA 

and for the rest of the war fought against the Americans.

7. THE WAR AT SEA 

Most of our great wars have been fought on the sea as well as on the land. This was true of the Revolutionary War.

When the conflict began, the Americans had no navy. In the first year of the war, however, Congress ordered thirteen fighting ships to be built, and bought several merchant ships to be made over into vessels of war.

This was a small beginning for a navy, but the American sea rovers made the most of what they had. All the States except two built navies of their own to defend their coast towns. 

The United States navy and the State navies were not the only vessels carrying on the war at sea. 

A great many trading ships and fishing vessels were fitted out with guns and sailed as privateers in search of enemy supply ships and enemy merchant vessels. They were not strong enough to attack English warships and therefore tried to keep away from them. But the privateers had written permission from the Continental Congress to attack English trading vessels.

Many a brisk fight occurred, and many a merchant vessel was captured by the privateers. The members of the crew of such a captured ship were taken to an American court, and the cargo of army supplies, sugar, molasses, or whatever it might be, was sold. Then the ship herself was either sold or fitted out with guns to be used as a privateer.

But the privateers did not have everything their own way. Although they were successful in capturing almost six hundred English merchant vessels, the English

d navy captured nine hundred of the American ships. The time came when it was dangerous to ship goods by sea even for short distances along the Atlantic coast. The warships of the enemy were too many and too powerful for the Americans to carry goods safely by sea. 

A regular navy was a serious need of the United States, for the protection of the coast and of American shipping, and also for the cutting off of supplies from England for the English armies in America.

But a navy is expensive, and the United States was a struggling new nation with little money. To build large ships like England's was impossible. It was John Paul 

Jones who first urged Congress to build several small sailing vessels. For this reason he has been called "the 

Father of the American Navy."

John Paul, as he was called when a boy, was born in Scotland. He was working with a shipmaster at the age of twelve. He made many sea voyages when quite young and, for a short time, was engaged in the slave trade. He did not like this job, however, and soon gave it up. 

When he was only seventeen years of age he was given command of a vessel which was trading with the 

West Indies. Two years before the Revolution broke out John Paul came to Virginia and settled down in the colony. His brother had recently died in Virginia and 

John Paul had inherited his estate. But he did not really settle down in Virginia, because he never settled down anywhere. He was always too active for that.

At the outbreak of the Revolution, and when John Paul was twenty-eight years of age, he offered his services to

EARLY AMERICA 

Congress. They were accepted, and he was made an officer in the infant navy. It was at this time that he took the name of Jones out of admiration for General Willie Jones, a wealthy planter of North Carolina, who had befriended him in his days of poverty. From this time on he was known as John Paul Jones. 

Soon after this he hoisted an American flag on the ship-of-war, Alfred. This was the first flag ever hoisted on an American warship.

The flag was a banner of yellow silk bearing the picture of a pine tree and a rattlesnake, with the words, "Don't tread on me." 

Jones struck wherever he could find an enemy. In the summer of 1777 he sailed away to Europe on board the 

Ranger, looking for victims.

In the spring of the following year, while prowling around the coasts and harbors of the British Isles, he landed at Whitehaven, England, spiked thirty-eight of the big British guns and then sailed away. 

JOHN PAUL JONES 

In 1779 came the most memorable exploit of this great sea-fighter. He was then cruising along the eastern coast of England. His ship was the Bon Homme Richard (Good man Richard). The boat was an old hulk which had been given to Jones by the King of France. "Her decks were too weak for her guns; her guns were too

old for service," and her strange crew was made up of men from all parts of the world. 

While cruising along the shore in September, Jones sighted the Serapis (ser'á pís), a fine, new, large, British warship. In a moment the fight was on. The Serapis was the better sailer and thus had a great advantage. 

But Jones ran his old ship alongside and lashed the two vessels together with a two-inch cable. Then took place one of the most desperate sea fights in history. The 

Richard was pretty well shot to pieces, but when Jones was asked if he was ready to surrender, his calm reply was, "I have not yet begun to fight. 

The battle was fought at night and the sky was lit up for miles around by the flames from the burning vessels. 

Both vessels were on fire, the Serapis in a dozen places at one time. Finally Jones shot away her main mast and she hauled down her flag and surrendered. The 

Richard, in its worthless condition, was abandoned and sank soon after in the North Sea.

About ten years after the close of the Revolutionary War, Captain John Paul Jones died in Paris. Later his body was brought to the United States and buried with high honors at the Naval Academy at Annapolis. 

8. GEORGE ROGERS CLARK AND THE NORTHWEST TERRITORY 

The land between the Ohio, the Mississippi, and the Great Lakes has long been known as the Northwest Territory. It included what are now five important States: 

Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, Michigan, and Wisconsin and part of a sixth, Minnesota.

EARLY AMERICA 

In the early part of the Revolution the Northwest Territory was held by the English. It was then taken by the 

Americans when Vincennes, Indiana, was captured in 

February, 1779. It was General George Rogers Clark and his men who saved the Northwest Territory to the 

American Union. 

George Rogers Clark was born in Virginia in 1752.

His birthplace was only a mile and a half from the old home of Thomas Jefferson, who wrote the Declaration of Independence. When the war broke out, several of his brothers enlisted in the American army for regular service, but George Rogers had a plan of his own for attacking the British in the Northwest. He thought he could do more good in this way. 

Clark was familiar with the Northwest. He had been there before. Like Washington, he had gone across the mountains to survey. He had also gone there as a soldier. In fact, he was much more of a soldier than surveyor.

Clark felt strong enough to attack, not only the Indians, but also the British. During the Revolution English troops were stationed at Detroit, Vincennes, Kaskaskia, and other places in the western country. Clark felt that the British were arousing the Indians to make raids on the white settlements. He planned to put a stop to all this by striking boldly at the British posts. 

In order to secure help for his expedition, he went to the Governor of Virginia, Patrick Henry, and explained his plan for attacking the English. Henry was favorable to the plan, and called in Thomas Jefferson and two other men to talk the matter over with Clark and himself.

As a result Clark's plan was approved and he was given $60,000 to carry it out.

Meanwhile the whole matter was to be kept a secret. 

Governor Henry prepared two sets of instructions—one to be made known to the people, and the other, the real set, as a guide for Clark. The public instructions said nothing about attacking the English posts. Clark, of course, was much pleased to have his plan approved by the Governor.

In January, 1778, Clark left Virginia and began to gather men for the expedition. He got together a few men and they all started joyfully down the Ohio River.

They pitched camp on Corn Island in the Ohio River, near the site of Louisville. Corn Island was a rather high spot of land. Clark said he noticed that it was almost always above water. Perhaps we should mention that Clark had not yet told his men what his secret instructions were, and they had no idea that they were going to attack the English.

Finally, when all was in readiness, Clark told his men that they were going to march against the English at 

Kaskaskia, Illinois. (See map, page 130.) A few of the officers had perhaps known this before.

At the proper time Clark let out the rest of his secret by saying, "We start at dawn tomorrow morning.

There was great excitement on the island. The timid men had, for the most part, backed out and those remaining were ready for "glory or the grave." 

It was a bright morning on the 24th of June, 1778, when Clark's men took to their canoes. They pressed on down the river for four days. Then they hid their

EARLY AMERICA 

GEORGE ROGERS CLARK CAPTURES KASKASKIA 

canoes and went the rest of the way by land. Without horses or wagons they pushed on for six days, through brush and over swamps, until they came within sight of 

Kaskaskia. 

About midnight Clark and his men entered the town without being seen by the sentinels. Inside the fort a merry dance was going on. Clark is said to have walked in and told the dancers to keep right on, but to remember that they were now dancing under the control of Virginia instead of England.

Simon Kenton, a powerful giant from Virginia, went to the bed where the governor was peacefully sleeping and quietly placed him under arrest. In this 

In this way Kaskaskia, the famous English post on the Mississippi, fell into the hands of the Americans without the striking of a blow.

Kaskaskia had once been a French post and most of its people were French. The English in command of the

place had told them that they should expect no mercy if they were captured by the Americans. Now that they were in the hands of Clark and his men, the people were terrified. They expected the worst. He

However, Clark showed himself to be a wise man. found that the people of Kaskaskia did not know anything about the real causes of the American Revolution. 

Clark now explained the whole conflict to the people plainly and simply. He said he did not wish to punish them in any way, and that if they would show their loyalty to the United States, they should have the protection of the American government and all the rights and privileges of American citizens. There was great rejoicing among them. Since the people of these French posts had no great love for their English masters, they were glad enough to support the American cause. Kaskaskia, with its two hundred and fifty families, became 

Fort Clark. 

Clark now turned his attention to Vincennes, the most important of the English posts. He planned to march across Illinois through mud, ice, snow, and sleet to thefort on the Wabash River. (See map, page 130.) 

On February 5, 1779, General Clark with his brave band started against Vincennes. It was a great day at 

Kaskaskia. Everybody turned out to see the soldiers off on their march of two hundred and thirty miles. A few days later the little army came to a river which they crossed on trees felled for that purpose, for the river was too deep for fording. After getting across they camped on the bank without tents in the winter rain. 1

EARLY AMERICA 

The great level plain about them was covered with water, sometimes so deep that the horses had to swim. 

Clark made every effort to keep up the spirits of his men; often he had them sing marching songs. When they found a small piece of high land, they thought they were lucky and pitched camp for the night. One morning on starting out they had to break ice one-half to three-quarters of an inch in thickness in order to wade through the water.

Although Clark encouraged his men in every way, he was a stern commander. On one occasion, when the men were in the water, he ordered one of his majors, with twenty-five men, to bring up the rear and shoot down any man who tried to turn back. On another occasion Clark says that the water was up to his shoulder and that the men were compelled to steady themselves by taking hold of the bushes and trees along the line of march. 

One day, when they were chilled through, half starved and nearly exhausted, an Indian canoe loaded with buffalo meat and other provisions came into sight. The canoe was promptly seized and hot buffalo broth was prepared to refresh the worn-out soldiers. 

When Clark was about two miles from Vincennes, he wrote a letter to the people of the village and told them to keep out of the streets, as there was likely to be trouble. He said: "I am determined to take your fort this night and I am now only two miles away.”

He expected to see the fort bristle up, but there were no signs of life or activity. Finally Clark opened fire on the fort. Even then the English did not realize what was going on. They thought that a party of drunken Bi

2 Indians were saluting them. But, when one of their men was shot down, the drums began to roar and the troops were aroused to action. Clark later found that 

This letter had been posted up in the town but that no one had told the soldiers in the fort anything about it. 

Clark kept up the firing from eight o'clock in the evening until nine o'clock the following morning. During this time his men were not more than thirty yards from the walls of the fort, but they lay in protected places. 

They were also such good marksmen that they picked off any man who appeared in an opening of the fort to fire a gun.

Clark then sent Colonel Hamilton, the English commander, a letter demanding his surrender and saying that if the Americans were compelled to take the fort by storm he might expect "such treatment as is justly due to a murderer." He was thinking of Hamilton's use of the Indians to kill settlers on the frontier. 

Hamilton quietly replied that he would not be frightened into taking any step which was not worthy of an 

English officer.

The firing then began again. Finally Hamilton suggested that he and Clark have a meeting and talk the matter over. This was done, and as a result Hamilton surrendered the fort and his men as prisoners of war. 

Clark had one man wounded and none killed.

The surrender was arranged on the afternoon of February 24, 1779, and on the morning of the next day, at ten o'clock, the fort was turned over to General Clark. 

Clark had now seized the whole of the territory between the Ohio and the Mississippi Rivers from the

EARLY AMERICA 

English and made it part of the United States. The legislature of Virginia thanked him for his services, and the people of the United States have thanked him ever since.

At the close of the Revolutionary War, when the treaty of peace was signed, the Northwest Territory was in- 

Icluded in the United States.

44. OUR UNITED STATES 1. THE ARTICLES OF CONFEDERATION 

Benjamin Franklin, John Adams, and John Jay met the English representatives at Paris and arranged the treaty of peace with them in 1783. American independence was recognized. The boundary of the United States was fixed at Canada and the Great Lakes on the north, at the Mississippi on the west, and at Florida on the south. At that time Florida reached from the present 

State of Florida west to the Mississippi River.

The new States were now ready for an independent life of their own. They had gone to the very last year of the conflict without having adopted a form of union. 

When the Continental Congress appointed a committee to draw up a Declaration of Independence, it also appointed a committee to draw up a plan of union. The plan proposed was called the "Articles of Confederation." But before the Articles could be put to work, each State had to agree to accept them. The last State did not agree until 1781, the last year of the war. 

In the meantime, while the war lasted, the States stood together under the direction of the Continental Congress. 

This Congress was hardly a real government. It could only advise the States what to do and take the leadership in carrying out its plans and suggestions. The States 450

OUR UNITED STATES 

451 all faced the same dangers, had the same interests, and desired the same result. Naturally they acted together and looked to Congress for leadership.

During the war the people were governed by the States, each State for itself. The people looked to their legislature, their governor, and their courts for the making of new laws, the collection of taxes, the keeping of order, and the enforcement of law. They did not look to the Continental Congress for such things. 

After declaring themselves free from the Mother Country, the people of each State set up a new State government. Connecticut and Rhode Island kept their old colonial charters as their State constitutions, but all the rest of the States adopted new constitutions for themselves. 

2. THE AMERICAN CONSTITUTION 

The Articles of Confederation proved too weak to preserve the union of the States. To be sure, the Articles provided for a Congress made up of representatives of the various State governments. But this Congress had very little power, for it had to ask the various States for money to pay its bills. Sometimes a State felt that it was being asked to pay more than its share of the expenses and it was slow in paying. Because of the weakness of Congress, the States began to quarrel with one another over boundaries and other matters, so that many men believed that the Union would break up. Under the 

Articles of Confederation the government had no power to enforce the laws, to raise money, or to control trade. 

This condition alarmed patriotic men in the various

EARLY AMERICA INDEPENDENCE HALL, PHILADELPHIA 

States. Under the leadership of Washington, they asked the States to send representatives to a convention to talk over ways for improving the national government, or, as our Constitution says, "to make a more perfect union."

The delegates, or representatives of the States, met in Philadelphia in the summer of 1787 to make a new Constitution for the United States. These men met in Independence Hall, where the Declaration of Independence had been signed a few years before. Washington was

OUR UNITED STATES 

453 1 the president of the Convention and Benjamin Franklin, Alexander Hamilton, James Madison, and other American leaders were members of it. 

The convention worked hard during the hot months of the summer and finally the Constitution was finished on September 17, 1787. This date is now celebrated in all parts of the United States as "Constitution Day."

After the Constitution was completed, it had to be sent around to the various States for their approval. We now think a great deal of our Constitution, but at that time many good men were strongly opposed to it. 

Patrick Henry, for example, tried very hard to have it thrown overboard. But Washington, Franklin, Hamilton, Madison, and others were in favor of it, and the thirteen States finally agreed to accept it. 

The Constitution clearly tells what powers are given to Congress. Among them are the power to coin money, to raise money by taxation, to raise an army and a navy, to declare war and make peace, and to regulate trade. 

The Constitution also provides that there shall be a President of the United States to enforce the laws made by Congress and a Supreme Court and other courts to apply these laws.

It was arranged that the new form of government should go into effect on March 4, 1789. This was a very important event. On the afternoon of March 3 the guns in New York harbor fired a farewell salute to the old form of government. On the following morning the same guns fired another salute to the new government and church bells all over the city rang out in honor of the event.

EARLY AMERICA "LONG LIVE GEORGE WASHINGTON, PRESIDENT OF THE UNITED STATES!" 

New York was chosen as the first capital of the new nation, and George Washington was elected the first 

President of the United States. 

In all the nations of the world today the United States Constitution is looked upon with respect and honor as one of the greatest constitutions for the government of a nation. It has protected the people of the United 

States and has provided for their changing needs from 1789 down to the present time. Under its guidance 

America has steadily grown and has become one of the leading nations of the world.

OUR UNITED STATES 

455

TEST IV 

Choose the correct ending for each of the following sentences: 

1. The American colonies quarreled with England because a. the colonists were quarrelsome people. b. they did not like King George III. c. they had to pay taxes passed by Parliament in which they were not represented. 

2. When the Revolutionary War began, the colonies fought the Mother Country because a. they wanted to be an independent nation. b. they wanted the same rights that Englishmen in England had. c. they wanted to build their own navy. 

3. The surrender of Burgoyne at Saratoga a. brought an end to the fighting between England and the United States. b. brought the help of France to the United States. c. brought about the meeting of the First Continental Congress. 

4. Our United States became a separate country a. when the Constitution was drawn up. b. When Cornwallis surrendered at Yorktown. c. when the Declaration of Independence was signed.

EARLY AMERICA 

5. The Northwest Territory became part of the United States. because a. French people were living there and the French were allies of the Americans. b. it was an Indian state. c. George Rogers Clark seized the forts and claimed the land for the United States. 

6. The first governments of the United States were not successful because a. the thirteen States had no money. b. the States were jealous of one another. c. the States would not let the United States have any real power. 

7. Our United States government today gets its power from a. the Declaration of Independence. 

b. the Articles of Confederation. c. the national Constitution. 

8. The first President of the United States was a. John Paul Jones. b. George Washington. c. Patrick Henry.
